Integrability and RG flow in 2d sigma models
Thesis for the degree of

Abstract
Motivated by the search for solvable string theories, we consider the problem of classifying the integrable bosonic 2d σ \sigma -models. We include non-conformal σ \sigma -models, which have historically been a good arena for discovering integrable models that were later generalized to Weyl-invariant ones. General σ \sigma -models feature a quantum RG flow, given by a ‘generalized Ricci flow’ of the target-space geometry. This thesis is based on the conjecture that integrable σ \sigma -models are renormalizable , or stable under the RG flow. It is widely understood that classically integrable theories are stable at the leading 1-loop order with only a few parameters running. Here we address what happens at higher-loop orders . We find that integrable σ \sigma -models generally remain RG-stable at higher-loops provided they receive a particular choice of finite counterterms, or quantum ( α ′ ) (\alpha^{\prime}) corrections to the target-space geometry. Thought to be preserving integrability at the quantum level, these corrections are analogous to those required for higher-loop conformal invariance of gauged Wess-Zumino-Witten models. We explicitly construct the quantum corrections restoring higher-loop renormalizability for examples of integrable η \eta - and λ \lambda -deformed σ \sigma -models. We then consider the integrable G × G G\times G and G × G / H G\times G/H models and also construct a new class of integrable G × G / H G\times G/H models with abelian H H . We also reformulate the λ \lambda -models as σ \sigma -models on a “tripled” G × G × G G\times G\times G configuration space, where they become automatically renormalizable due to the decoupling of some fields. In the limit when they become non-abelian dual (NAD) models, this suggests that the corresponding ‘interpolating models’ for NAD are also renormalizable, with 2-loop β \beta -functions matching the group/symmetric space σ \sigma -models. We then present a new and different link between integrability and the RG flow in the context of σ \sigma -models with ‘local couplings’ depending explicitly on 2d time. Such models are naturally obtained in the light-cone gauge in string theory, pointing to the possibility of a large, new class of solvable string models.

\sigma
-models and their time-dependent generalizations

Chapter 1
Introduction

1.1
Background

The AdS/CFT correspondence is a remarkable set of dualities between
string theories in Anti-de Sitter space AdS
d+1

and
conformal field theories CFT
d
in one fewer dimension
[
6
,
7
,
8
]
. In the Poincaré coordinates for AdS,

\displaystyle ds^{2}=\frac{R^{2}}{z^{2}}\big(dz^{2}+dx^{\mu}dx_{\mu}\big)\ ,\qquad\mu=1,\ldots,d\ ,

(1.1)

one may picture the CFT as existing at the “conformal boundary”
z=0
, where the metric formally diverges but is conformally equivalent to the flat
d
-dimensional one. Gauge-string dualities of this type are often called “holographic” as they conjecture a mapping between the degrees of freedom on the boundary (
z=0
) and those in the “bulk”, or interior of the AdS space. A natural first check is the matching of internal symmetries: indeed the isometry group
SO(2,d-1)
of AdS
d+1
coincides with the conformal group of
d
-dimensional flat space.

Such a correspondence has several immediately interesting and surprising properties. First, the
(d+1)
th dimension (the
z
direction in (
1.1
)) seems to emerge from a
d
-dimensional CFT description. Second, the string theory is gravitational while the CFT is not. Thus it is possible to reformulate a complicated quantum theory of gravity in terms of a simpler and better understood CFT in flat space.

In the context of string theory, the AdS space must be embedded in a string background. A canonical example discussed in Maldacena’s original proposal
[
6
]
is the type IIB background obtained in the low energy limit from a stack of
N
coincident D3-branes. The conjecture is that the low energy theory describing open strings ending on the D-branes —
SU(N)

\mathcal{N}=4
super Yang-Mills theory in 4d — is dual to the theory of closed strings in the near horizon region — type IIB strings on
AdS_{5}\times S^{5}
. The theories’ parameters are related by

\displaystyle\frac{R^{2}}{\alpha^{\prime}}=\sqrt{g_{\rm YM}^{2}N}\ ,\qquad g_{s}=\frac{g_{\rm YM}^{2}}{4\pi}\ ,

(1.2)

where
R
is the AdS radius in (
1.1
),
\alpha^{\prime}=l_{s}^{2}
the squared string length,
g_{\rm YM}
the Yang-Mills coupling and
N
the rank of the Yang-Mills gauge group.

Among the many examples of the AdS/CFT correspondence, the
AdS_{5}\times S^{5}
case is notable because it is
integrable
2
2

2

Here we give a lightning summary of integrability in the AdS/CFT correspondence, focussing on
AdS_{5}\times S^{5}

(and missing many details and important references). For a comprehensive introduction, see the reviews
[
171
,
80
,
81
,
82
]
.
,
3
3

3

Another well-studied, integrable, holographic string background is the type IIA background on
AdS_{4}\times\mathbb{C}\mathbf{P}^{3}
, dual to 3d
\mathcal{N}=6
Chern-Simons theory
[
182
]
; see, e.g., the review
[
181
]
.
in the free string (
g_{s}=0
) limit, or equivalently in the planar limit (
N=\infty
with
\lambda=g_{\rm YM}^{2}N
fixed) of the gauge theory. Here “integrability” is understood in the same sense of familiar integrable systems like the KdV or sine-Gordon model, meaning aspects of the theory can be solved exactly without recourse to perturbation theory.

On the string side (strong ’t Hooft coupling
\lambda
), the Green-Schwarz action for strings in the
AdS_{5}\times S^{5}
background takes the Metsaev-Tseytlin form
[
9
]
equivalent to a
\sigma
-model on the semi-symmetric space
PSU(2,2|4)/[SO(1,4)\times SO(5)]
with a particular Wess-Zumino term. It is a general fact that such models are classically integrable
[
10
,
11
,
13
,
14
,
15
]
in the sense of admitting a flat Lax connection and associated conserved charges (see Section
1.4.1
below). In spite of classic issues preventing a direct integrable quantization due to the non-ultralocality of the classical Poisson structure
[
228
,
14
]
, one may just
assume
the quantum theory to be integrable and bootstrap an exact integrable S-matrix for the fully gauge-fixed theory based on symmetries and consistency conditions (e.g. in an expansion around a BMN-type
[
70
]
geodesic, fixing the ‘uniform light-cone’ gauge; see the review
[
80
]
). This S-matrix describes scattering on a 2d plane, but the finite size (closed string) spectrum may be extracted by some Bethe ansatz equations, or more properly accounting for the “wrapping corrections” by double Wick rotating
[
16
]
and using the Thermodynamic Bethe Ansatz (TBA)
[
17
]
. The TBA (nonlinear integral) equations were reformulated as the simpler Quantum Spectral Curve
[
18
]
, which is the most modern approach to computing the exact spectrum (see also the review
[
19
]
).

On the gauge side (weak coupling), the structure and origin of integrability is more mysterious. It was observed that the mixing matrix for anomalous dimensions of single-trace local operators coincides with the Hamiltonian of an integrable spin chain
[
20
]
. This conjures an image of fields inside the trace corresponding to sites of a spin chain, with their R-charges corresponding to the spins. Including more and more loop orders, the corresponding spin chain was found to remain integrable, but with longer-range interactions at each order
[
21
]
. Assuming integrability to all orders, the exact spin-chain S-matrix
[
23
]
and asymptotic Bethe ansatz equations
[
22
]
(for long operators or long spin chains) can be obtained, matching results from the string side. The relation between integrability on the gauge and string sides can be understood qualitatively in terms effective Landau-Lifshitz actions for coherent states, with the string effection action being the direct continuum limit of the spin chain one (see, e.g.,
[
83
]
and refs. there).

Integrability has been extremely useful in understanding the holographic duality in the case of
AdS_{5}\times S^{5}
. Not only has it allowed the solution of the spectral problem for a non-trivial 4d gauge theory (with qualitative similarities to QCD), but it has also facilitated non-trivial checks of the AdS/CFT correspondence in this example (see, e.g.,
[
24
]
). Motivated by a wish to solve more string theories in curved space, and more 4d gauge theories, one could ask in which ways the
AdS_{5}\times S^{5}
background can be
deformed
while preserving integrability. More generally, we would like to know which string theories are integrable.

The question of classifiying integrable string backgrounds is already very difficult in the simpler case of
bosonic
strings, which will be our focus. Although focussing on bosonic strings takes a step back from the AdS/CFT correspondence that was our motivation, we note the historical fact that the integrable semi-symmetric space
\sigma
-model governing the
AdS_{5}\times S^{5}
background is a simple generalization of bosonic symmetric space
\sigma
-models discussed below.
The motion of a bosonic string is described by a 2d
\sigma
-model coupled to 2d gravity,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &S=\frac{1}{4\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int d^{2}\xi\,\mathcal{L}\\
&\mathcal{L}=-\big[\sqrt{-h}h^{\mu\nu}\,{\rm G}_{mn}(x)+\varepsilon^{\mu\nu}\,{\rm B}_{mn}(x)\big]\,\partial_{\mu}x^{m}\,\partial_{\nu}x^{n}-\alpha^{\prime}\sqrt{-h}\,R^{(2)}\phi(x)\ ,\\
&m,n=1,\ldots,D\ ,\qquad\quad\mu,\nu=1,2\ .\end{aligned}

(1.3)

Here the scalar fields
x^{m}
may be viewed as embedding coordinates for the 2d worldsheet (traced out by a string over time) in a
D
-dimensional target space-time. The parameter
\alpha^{\prime}
plays the role of
\hbar
(counting loops) in the
\sigma
-model. In this thesis we will set
\alpha^{\prime}=1
for convenience, but will sometimes reinstate
\alpha^{\prime}
to emphasize the loop counting. The
\sigma
-model couplings
({\rm G}_{mn},{\rm B}_{mn})
are respectively symmetric and antisymmetric and correspond to the background values of the target space metric and NSNS 2-form. The auxiliary 2d metric
h_{\mu\nu}
is arbitrary and
\varepsilon_{\mu\nu}
is the antisymmetric 2d Levi-Civita tensor.

The model (
1.3
) is 2d diffeomorphism-invariant and classically 2d Weyl-invariant. The role of the dilaton background
\phi(x)
, appearing coupled to the 2d curvature
R^{(2)}
at subleading order in
\alpha^{\prime}
, is to compensate quantum breaking of Weyl invariance from the
({\rm G},{\rm B})
terms
[
74
]
. Preservation of the local Weyl invariance, which is necessary for the consistency of the quantum theory, imposes that the background fields satisfy certain ‘string equations of motion’
[
25
]
(generalizing the Einstein equation),

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &R_{mn}-\tfrac{1}{4}({\rm H}^{2})_{mn}+2\nabla_{m}\nabla_{n}\phi={\mathcal{O}}(\alpha^{\prime})\ ,\qquad\quad({\rm H}_{mnp}\equiv 3\partial_{[m}{\rm B}_{np]})\\
&-\tfrac{1}{2}\nabla^{p}{\rm H}_{mnp}+{\rm H}_{mn}{}^{p}\,\nabla_{p}\phi={\mathcal{O}}(\alpha^{\prime})\ ,\\
&\tfrac{1}{6}(D-26)-\tfrac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}\phi+(\nabla\phi)^{2}-\tfrac{1}{24}{\rm H}^{2}={\mathcal{O}}(\alpha^{\prime})\ ,\end{aligned}

(1.4)

which contain corrections at each order in
\alpha^{\prime}
(corresponding to the loop expansion of the
\sigma
-model). These equations are obtained by varying an effective gravitational action for the massless fields,

\displaystyle S=\frac{1}{2{\kappa}^{2}}\int d^{D}x\,e^{-2\phi}\big(R-\tfrac{1}{12}{\rm H}^{2}+4(\partial\phi)^{2}\big)+{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha^{\prime})\ .

(1.5)

and it has been checked
to several orders in
\alpha^{\prime}
that this action coincides with the one obtained from the tree-level string S-matrix
[
163
,
146
,
147
,
148
]
(see also the review
[
12
]
).

For the purposes of classical integrability, it will be sufficient to fix the ‘conformal’ gauge
\sqrt{-h}h^{\mu\nu}=\eta^{\mu\nu}
, obtaining a
\sigma
-model on flat 2d space,

4
4

4

The 2d light-cone derivatives are defined as
\partial_{\pm}=\partial_{\tau}\pm\partial_{\sigma}
, see Appendix
A
for the conventions.

\displaystyle S=\frac{1}{4\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int d^{2}\xi\,\mathcal{L}=\frac{1}{4\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int d^{2}\xi\,\big[{\rm G}_{mn}(x)+{\rm B}_{mn}(x)\big]\,\partial_{+}x^{m}\partial_{-}x^{n}\ ,

(1.6)

where we have ignored the quantum dilaton term. At the level of classical integrability, the flat and curved 2d-space
\sigma
-models are equivalent since they are related by a redefinition
\xi^{\mu}\to\xi^{\prime\mu}(\xi)
(as the
\sigma
-model is classically 2d Weyl invariant and all 2d metrics are locally equivalent up to a conformal factor). The Virasoro constraints (
T_{++}=0
,
T_{--}=0
) coming from the variation of the 2d metric
h_{\mu\nu}
in (
1.3
) should not destroy classical integrability since these are just constraints on the initial data.

Our eventual goal is to understand which string backgrounds
({\rm G},{\rm B})
(solutions of (
1.4
) with some choice of
\phi
) correspond to integrable
\sigma
-models (
1.6
). At the classical level, one criterion for integrability is the 2d equations of motion taking the
zero-curvature
form
[
26
,
27
,
28
,
29
]

\displaystyle[\partial_{+}+L_{+}(z),\partial_{-}+L_{-}(z)]=0\ ,

(1.7)

where the “Lax connection”
L_{\pm}=L_{\pm}(z;x,\partial x)
is a 1-complex-parameter family of connections constructed from the fields. For any given
\sigma
-model couplings
({\rm G},{\rm B})
, it is very difficult to know whether a zero-curvature representation exists, and moreover there is no general recipe for constructing the Lax connection. This tricky classification problem of integrable
\sigma
-models is the motivation for the thesis.

1.2
Stability under the RG flow

In this thesis we will relax the Weyl invariance condtion (
1.4
) and consider general
\sigma
-models on a flat 2d space, again asking the question of which choices of couplings
({\rm G},{\rm B})
correspond to integrable theories. Although non-Weyl-invariant
\sigma
-models do not directly correspond to string backgrounds, their study is well-motivated by the historical observation that non-conformal integrable
\sigma
-models such as the PCM and the symmetric space
\sigma
-models have preceded the discovery of related conformal ones — the WZW model and the semi-symmetric space
\sigma
-model with WZ term. In other words, one may discover new examples of Weyl-invariant integrable theories by first discovering non-Weyl-invariant ones.

Moreover, relaxing Weyl invariance is actually helpful since it opens up a new ‘diagnostic tool’ for integrability: the
renormalization group (RG) flow
. The idea is that, in order to be able to identify the integrable theories, one should thoroughly investigate their properties and distinctive features. We shall focus on one conjectured property in particular: that
integrable
\sigma
-models are
stable
under the quantum RG flow

[
101
,
102
,
103
,
104
,
105
,
108
,
161
,
106
,
152
,
153
]
. Let us now explain the meaning of this statement.

General
\sigma
-models feature a quantum RG flow (breaking the scale invariance of the classical theory). The
\sigma
-model can be quantized preserving the target space covariance
[
99
,
100
]
and, in the simplest case with
{\rm B}_{mn}=0
, the RG flow is given by a ‘generalized Ricci flow’,
5
5

5

We denote by
t=\log\mu
the 2d RG scale.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\frac{d}{dt}G_{mn}=\beta_{mn}+\nabla_{(m}\xi_{n)}\ ,\qquad\qquad\qquad(B_{mn}=0)\\
&\beta_{mn}=\alpha^{\prime}\,R_{mn}+\alpha^{\prime 2}\,\tfrac{1}{2}R_{mpqr}{R_{n}}^{pqr}+\ldots\ .\end{aligned}

(1.8)

We have included the 1-loop and 2-loop terms, and there will be further contributions at each loop order. Here
\xi^{n}(x)
corresponds to a choice of renormalizations of the fields
x^{n}
, i.e. target space diffeomorphisms depending on the 2d RG scale. Now including the
{\rm B}
-field, the
\beta
-function (
1.8
) generalizes, in a particular 2-loop subtraction scheme
6
6

6

See Section
1.4.15
for discussion of scheme dependence.
to
[
163
]
(see also
[
169
,
170
,
221
]
)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\frac{d}{dt}({\rm G}_{mn}+{\rm B}_{mn})=\beta_{mn}+L_{\xi}({\rm G}+{\rm B})_{mn}+\partial_{[m}\lambda_{n]}\ ,\\
&\beta_{mn}=\alpha^{\prime}\,\widehat{R}_{mn}+\alpha^{\prime 2}\,\tfrac{1}{2}\Big[\widehat{R}^{klp}{}_{n}\widehat{R}_{mklp}-\tfrac{1}{2}\widehat{R}^{lpk}{}_{n}\widehat{R}_{mklp}+\tfrac{1}{2}\widehat{R}_{kmnl}{\rm H}^{kpq}{\rm H}^{l}_{\ pq}\Big]+\ldots\ ,\end{aligned}

(1.9)

where
{\rm H}_{kmn}=3\partial_{[k}{\rm B}_{mn]}
and
\widehat{R}
is the curvature of the generalized connection
\widehat{\Gamma}^{k}{}_{mn}=\Gamma({\rm G})^{k}{}_{mn}-\tfrac{1}{2}{\rm H}^{k}{}_{mn}
.
The use of
\widehat{R}
is simply a convenient notation and one can re-express (
1.9
) in terms of just
R
,
{\rm H}
and covariant derivatives: for example,
\widehat{R}_{mn}=R_{mn}-\tfrac{1}{4}{\rm H}_{mpq}{\rm H}_{n}{}^{pq}-\tfrac{1}{2}D^{k}{\rm H}_{kmn}
. Here
L_{\xi}
denotes the Lie derivative along a vector
\xi
, generalizing the diffeomorphism term of (
1.8
) above. The extra term
\partial_{[m}\lambda_{n]}
denotes a shift of the
{\rm B}
-field by an exact 2-form depending on the 2d RG scale; this is the usual 2-form gauge freedom, and only modifies the
\sigma
-model Lagrangian (
1.6
) by a total derivative.

The string equations (
1.4
) correspond to the Weyl-invariant case when the RG flow (
1.9
) trivializes, i.e.
\frac{d}{dt}({\rm G}_{mn}+{\rm B}_{mn})=0
. The diffeomorphism vector and B-gauge transformation must also take a specific form
[
262
]

\xi_{n}=2\alpha^{\prime}\partial_{n}\phi+{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha^{\prime 2})
,
\lambda_{n}={\mathcal{O}}(\alpha^{\prime 2})
in terms of the dilaton, metric and B-field. The dilaton equation in (
1.4
) then follows from the metric and
{\rm B}
-field equations up to a constant term, whose correct value ensures the vanishing of the Weyl anomaly coefficient.

More precisely, we may formulate the conjecture of RG stability as follows. For a family of integrable models
{\rm G}_{mn}={\rm G}_{mn}(g_{\alpha})
,
{\rm B}_{mn}={\rm B}_{mn}(g_{\alpha})
parametrized by a few couplings
g_{\alpha}
, the claim is that the RG flow (
1.9
) of the metric and
{\rm B}
-field consistently truncates to a flow of just the couplings
g_{\alpha}
,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}g_{\alpha}=\beta_{\alpha}(g)\ .

(1.10)

In other words, integrable
\sigma
-models are
renormalizable
, i.e. they are explicit solutions of the generalized Ricci flow equation.

Let us give an intuitive explanation for this conjecture. As we shall discuss below, classically integrable
\sigma
-models feature an infinite set of commuting conserved charges, as part of an intricate Poisson structure governed by a so-called Lax operator. In general one may expect this structure to be sufficiently constraining to uniquely determine the theory in question, so that the only deformations preserving it are the coupling constants
g_{\alpha}
of the theory. Now assuming the integrable structure to exist also in the quantum theory, then it should be respected by the quantum RG flow, and hence only the couplings
g_{\alpha}
should run under RG. Another explanation is that some integrable
\sigma
-models are understood to have exact S-matrices in the infrared, typically with a small number of parameters (see Section
1.4.12
below). One may then expect that these parameters should somehow match up with the semiclassical
\sigma
-model description, and that this matching should be preserved along the RG flow.

While there are no known counterexamples
to the conjecture that integrable
\sigma
-models are renormalizable,
in most examples this has only been checked to the leading 1-loop order in (
1.9
),
and there is no general proof. In this work, we shall ask the question of what happens at
higher loop orders.
We will find that the target space geometry of the classical
\sigma
-model
\mathcal{L}=({\rm G}+{\rm B})_{mn}^{(0)}\,\partial_{+}x^{m}\partial_{-}x^{n}
must generally be deformed by quantum corrections to remain a solution of the RG equation (
1.9
) beyond 1-loop,

\displaystyle({\rm G}+{\rm B})_{mn}^{(0)}\to({\rm G}+{\rm B})_{mn}^{(0)}+\alpha^{\prime}({\rm G}+{\rm B})_{mn}^{(1)}+\ldots\ .

(1.11)

Equivalently, one must add to the
\sigma
-model a particular choice of finite counterterms.
This is a scheme-independent statement because the required counterterms will be non-trivial in
arbitrary
covariant subtraction schemes.

Hence, while the classical Lax integrability corresponds just to the classical (uncorrected) geometry
({\rm G}+{\rm B})_{mn}^{(0)}
,
it is the quantum-corrected geometry that seems to posses the RG properties associated with integrability at the quantum level.
These counterterms might then be understood as preserving ‘Ward identites’ for hidden integrable symmetries at the quantum level, with the expectation that these hidden symmetries are constraining the RG flow. In this sense, one may hope to interpret the corrected
\sigma
-model as being the correct choice of bare action consistent with quantum integrability.

1.3
Structure of the thesis

This thesis is structured as follows. The rest of this introductory chapter is a review of integrable 2d
\sigma
-models with a focus on classical Lax integrability, examples of integrable models and their quantum RG flows.

In Chapter
2
(based on the papers
[
2
,
3
]
) we shall consider the higher-loop RG flow for some of the simplest non-trivial ‘integrable deformed’ models where renormalizability does not immediately follow from manifest global symmetries.
For the
\eta
-deformation of
S^{2}
(“sausage model”) or
H^{2}
, we explicitly construct the counterterms required for RG stability up to 3-loop order and conjecture the all-order form of the counterterms. We also study the closely related
\lambda
-deformation based on the
SU(2)\over U(1)
coset and the
SU(2)
group, finding the counterterms needed for 2-loop RG stability. We point out that these counterterms continue to serve the same function of restoring renormalizability in the non-abelian dual limit of the
\lambda
-model. This gives a prescription in these examples for the leading
\alpha^{\prime}
-corrections to the non-abelian duality transformation so that the non-abelian duality commutes with the RG flow, resolving prior confusion in the literature.

In Chapter
3
(based on
[
5
]
) we consider the same question for a different class of integrable
\sigma
-models on products of group spaces. We first study the integrable
G\times G
model derived from the affine Gaudin construction (for which the 1-loop
\beta
-functions were found in
[
220
]
) and show that its condition of integrability is automatically preserved also by the 2-loop RG flow without the need for any quantum corrections. We then investigate the RG flow in the gauged
G\times G/H
model, including the integrable
T^{1,1}
model found in
[
218
]
. In that case we instead find that the integrability condition relating the couplings requires a certain quantum deformation to preserve renormalizability at 2-loop order. We also construct a new class of integrable
G\times G/H
models in the case when the subgroup
H
is abelian. In the simplest case of
G=SU(2),\ H=U(1)
this leads to an integrable
\sigma
-model on the
T^{1,q}
space (with a particular
{\rm B}
-field). This model is found to be a special case that is stable under the 2-loop RG flow without deformation — and we relate this property to its invariance under T-duality in an isometric
U(1)
direction. This
T^{1,q}
model may be interpreted as an integrable deformation of the GMM model (of two coupled WZW theories with generic levels) away from the conformal point.

In Chapter
4
(based on
[
3
]
) we derive an alternate formulation of the
\lambda
-model as a
\sigma
-model on a “tripled” configuration space
G\times G\times G
by re-writing the 2d gauge field in terms of group-valued scalar fields.
Our central observation is that the
\lambda
-model quantized in this formulation is automatically renormalizable to all orders without any correction, with only the deformation parameter
\lambda
running.
This is in contrast to the standard
\sigma
-model found by integrating out
A_{\pm}
, which is 2-loop renormalizable only after adding the specific finite local counterterms discussed in Chapter
2
.
We compute the 2-loop
\beta
-function of the
\lambda
-model for general groups
and symmetric spaces.
In the non-abelian dual limit this yields an alternate formulation
of the interpolating models for
non-abelian duality
that is automatically renormalizable with
the same 2-loop
\beta
-function as the original PCM/symmetric space
\sigma
-model.

Section
4.5
contains unpublished work on the construction of similar “tripled” or “enlarged” formulations of (i) a generalization of the
\lambda
-model to product spaces
G^{N}
and (ii)
G\times G/H
models.

In Chapter
5
(based on
[
4
]
) we study a new and different link between integrability and the RG flow. We consider several classes of integrable
\sigma
-models (on groups and symmetric spaces,
\eta
-models,
\lambda
-models) with
local couplings
that may depend on the 2d coordinates, e.g. on time
\tau
. We observe that (i) starting with a classically integrable 2d
\sigma
-model, (ii) formally promoting its couplings
h_{\alpha}
to functions
h_{\alpha}(\tau)
of 2d time, and (iii) demanding that the resulting time-dependent model also admits a Lax connection implies that
h_{\alpha}(\tau)
must solve the 1-loop RG equations of the original theory with
\tau
interpreted as RG time. This provides a novel example of an ‘integrability–RG flow’ connection. The existence of a Lax connection suggests that these time-dependent
\sigma
-models may themselves be understood as integrable. We investigate this question by studying the possibility of constructing non-local conserved charges (see also Section 5 of
[
4
]
for attempts to construct local charges). Such
\sigma
-models with
D
-dimensional target space and time-dependent couplings subject to the RG flow naturally appear in string theory upon fixing the light-cone gauge in a
(D+2)
-dimensional conformal
\sigma
-model with a metric admitting a covariantly constant null Killing vector and a dilaton linear in the null coordinate. This suggests the possibility of a large, new class of solvable string models.

In Chapter
6
we summarize the findings of this work and comment on the open questions, possible applications and future prospects.

Appendix
A
is an explanation of the conventions and notation used in this thesis.

In Appendix
B
(based on
[
2
]
) we discuss the effect of scheme change on the
\sigma
-model
\beta
-functions, focussing on the particular case with a 2d target space (relevant for the
SU(2)\over U(1)

\eta
- and
\lambda
-models considered in Chapter
2
).

In Appendix
C
(based on
[
5
]
) we consider deriving the classical integrability condition for the couplings of
G^{N}
models (discussed in Chapter
3
) from first principles. We compare our results with the sufficient conditions for integrability obtained from affine Gaudin models. For general
N
we find that the dimension of the integrable space of
G^{N}
couplings coincides with that of the space of affine Gaudin models, and for the
G\times G
case we find that the affine Gaudin models are the only integrable ones.

In Appendix
D
(based on
[
5
]
) we provide the explicit formulae for the 2-loop

\beta
-functions of the
G\times G
and
G\times G/H
models (used in Chapter
3
) and explain how they were derived.

The remaining appendices, based on
[
4
]
, pertain to the ‘local coupling’ theories discussed in Chapter
5
. Appendix
E
contains details of the derivation of the RG flows from the condition of existence of Lax representations for the local coupling theories.

Appendix
F
is about the construction of non-local charges for time-dependent
\sigma
-models other than the PCM (which was discussed in Section
5.3
) — and in particular the time-dependent
\lambda
-model based on a symmetric space.

In Appendix
G
we consider 1d harmonic oscillators with time-dependent frequency, a special case of which was obtained in eq. (
5.50
) as a linearization of a 1d reduction of the time-dependent PCM.

In Appendix
H
we explain that the sine-Gordon model also obeys the same pattern as the
\sigma
-models discussed above (relating its integrability with local couplings to its RG flow).

1.4
Review of key concepts

In the remainder of this introductory chapter, we shall review some concepts priming the reader for the rest of the thesis. Let us highlight Section
1.4.13
, which is a summary of our paper
[
1
]
attempting to use the tree-level
\sigma
-model S-matrix as another ‘diagnostic tool’ for integrability.

1.4.1
Classical integrability

Most models in physics cannot be solved exactly. One example is the Standard Model of particle physics, where there is no hope to make exact predictions, but rather computations are made by expanding perturbatively around a free theory.
A simpler example is a many-body system in Newtonian gravity, where only the 2-body case is solvable and the 3-body and higher cases are chaotic.
One could ask:
what goes right
for the 2-body problem? One explanation is that the 2-body problem is integrable
7
7

7

See also the book
[
88
]
for an introduction to classical integrability.
while the others are not. For mechanical 1d systems, integrability means there exist at least as many independent, Poisson-commuting conserved charges as the number of degrees of freedom. In that case it follows that the theory can be completely solved by quadratures.

For 2d models, and in particular the
\sigma
-models that will be our focus, there are infinitely many degrees of freedom. The analogous notion of integrability in 2d is then related to the presence of infinitely many commuting conserved charges. A theory of exactly integrable PDEs in mathematical physics was developed starting from the 1960s
[
26
,
27
,
28
,
29
]
. The flat Lax connection is a central object, whose zero-curvature representation (
1.7
) expresses the relevant non-linear equation as the consistency condition for an over-determined
linear
system

\displaystyle\partial_{\pm}\psi=\psi\,L_{\pm}\ ,

(1.12)

leading to the construction of explicit solutions by the “inverse-scattering” method.

If a 2d field theory admits a Lax representation (
1.7
), then one can immediately build non-local conserved charges using the monodromy of the Lax connection along a constant-time contour from
\sigma=a
to
\sigma=b
,

\displaystyle{\cal M}=\mathcal{P}\exp\int_{a}^{b}L_{\sigma}\,d\sigma\ .

(1.13)

The path-ordered exponential in (
1.13
) is defined by the properties

\displaystyle{\cal M}^{-1}\partial_{b}{\cal M}=L_{\sigma}\big|_{\sigma=b}\ ,\qquad{\cal M}\big|_{a=b}=1\ .

(1.14)

On the support of the equation of motion (
1.7
) of zero-curvature form, it satisfies

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}{\cal M}={\cal M}\,L_{\tau}(b)-L_{\tau}(a)\,{\cal M}\ .

(1.15)

Assuming a periodicity condition
L_{\tau}(a)=L_{\tau}(b)
(e.g., following from periodic boundary conditions on the fields),
then (
1.15
) becomes
\partial_{\tau}{\cal M}=[{\cal M},L_{\tau}(a)]
and hence

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}{\rm Tr}[{\cal M}^{n}]=0\ ,\qquad n\in\mathbb{N}\ ,

(1.16)

i.e. the eigenvalues of
{\cal M}
are conserved.

If additionally the boundary value is vanishing, i.e.
L_{\tau}(a)=L_{\tau}(b)=0
(for example with decaying boundary conditions on the plane
(a,b)=(-\infty,\infty)
), then (
1.15
) gives the stronger result that all the entries of
{\cal M}
are conserved,

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}{\cal M}=0\ .

(1.17)

In either case, the number of conserved charges is actually infinite since the entries of
{\cal M}
are functions of the ‘spectral parameter’
z
(assuming the dependence is non-trivial) so each coefficient is individually conserved in any series expansion in
z
. These charges are ‘non-local’ since the monodromy matrix is a non-local object (it is not an integral of a local density).

We note that a Lax connection valued in the algebra of
G
is determined up to gauge transformations
L_{\pm}\to k^{-1}L_{\pm}k+k^{-1}\partial_{\pm}k
,
k(\xi)\in G
, since this transformation preserves the set of equations encoded in the zero-curvature condition (
1.7
). The monodromy matrix (
1.13
) rotates covariantly under such transformations so, assuming periodicity of the gauge parameter
k
, the set of non-local charges is preserved.

1.4.2
Principal Chiral Model

Let us consider the simple example of the Principal Chiral Model (PCM),
8
8

8

We denote the 2d indices by
\mu,\nu
and define the 2d light-cone coordinates as
\xi^{\pm}=\tfrac{1}{2}(\tau\pm\sigma)
with derivatives
\partial_{\pm}=\partial_{\tau}\pm\partial_{\sigma}
. The
D
-dimensional target space indices are
m,n=1,\ldots,D
. See Appendix
A
for conventions.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=-\tfrac{1}{2}{\rm h}\,{\rm Tr}[J_{+}J_{-}]\ ,\qquad J_{\mu}=g^{-1}\partial_{\mu}g\ ,\quad g\in G\ .

(1.18)

This is a special case of the general
\sigma
-model (
1.6
) with the target space taken to be a simple Lie group
G
with the bi-invariant metric (and vanishing
{\rm B}
-field). The model has a large global
G\times G
symmetry corresponding to left- and right-multiplications of the group,

\displaystyle g\to g_{L}\ g\ g_{R}\ ,\qquad\qquad(g_{L},g_{R})\in G_{L}\times G_{R}\ .

(1.19)

For this model, the global symmetry (
1.19
) (or isometries of the target space) is sufficient to uniquely fix the form of the Lagrangian (
1.18
) up to the overall constant
{\rm h}
. Hence the
\sigma
-model RG equations (
1.8
) are solved with just
{\rm h}
running according to the 2-loop
\beta
-function
[
198
,
199
,
100
]
,
9
9

9

In this PCM case, since the target space is isotropic, there cannot be any diffeomorphism vector
\xi
in (
1.8
) consistent with the symmetries.

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}{\rm h}=c_{{}_{G}}+\tfrac{1}{2}c_{{}_{G}}{\rm h}^{-1}+\ldots\ .

(1.20)

The constant
c_{{}_{G}}
denotes the dual Coxeter number of the group
G
(see Appendix
A
for conventions).

The key to noticing the model’s classical integrability is that the current
J_{\mu}
is conserved on-shell,
\partial^{\mu}J_{\mu}=0
, and also flat,
dJ+J\wedge J=0
(since
J_{\mu}=g^{-1}\partial_{\mu}g
). This information can be encoded in the flatness (
1.7
) of the Lax connection
[
29
]
,

\displaystyle L_{\pm}\equiv L_{\tau}\pm L_{\sigma}=\tfrac{1}{2}(1+z^{\pm 1})\,J_{\pm}\ .

(1.21)

An easy way to evaluate the monodromy matrix and extract conserved charges is by expanding in
z
around the value
z=-1
where
L_{\sigma}
vanishes (at which point
{\cal M}=I
is trivial). The expansion of the path-ordered exponential yields first the conserved Noether charge built from
J_{\mu}
itself (corresponding to the
G_{R}
symmetry) and then iterated integrals of
J
of increasing order,

\displaystyle{\cal M}=I+\tfrac{1}{2}(z+1)\int_{a}^{b}d\sigma\,J_{\tau}

(1.22)

\displaystyle\qquad\ \ \ \ +\tfrac{1}{4}(z+1)^{2}\Big[\int_{a<\sigma_{1}<\sigma_{2}<b}d\sigma_{1}d\sigma_{2}\,J_{\tau}(\sigma_{1})J_{\tau}(\sigma_{2})+\int_{a}^{b}d\sigma\,(J_{\tau}-J_{\sigma})\Big]+{\mathcal{O}}\big((z+1)^{3}\big)\ .

Each term in the expansion (or just their eigenvalues, depending on the boundary conditions) is individually conserved by the argument above, and their Poisson brackets satisfy a Yangian algebra
[
30
]
.

As well as these non-local charges, integrable field theories may posses higher-spin local charges. For the example of the PCM (
1.18
),
one can build holomorphic and anti-holomorphic local currents by contracting products of
J_{\pm}
with a symmetric invariant tensor
[
253
]
,

\displaystyle\partial_{\pm}\mathcal{J}_{\mp}^{(n)}=0\ ,\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\pm}^{(n)}=d_{A_{1}\cdots A_{n}}J_{\pm}^{A_{1}}\cdots J_{\pm}^{A_{n}}\

(1.23)

\displaystyle d_{A_{1}\ldots A_{n}}=d_{(A_{1}\ldots A_{n})}\ ,\qquad\qquad{f^{A}}_{B(C}d_{A_{1}\cdots A_{n-1})A_{n}}=0\ .

(1.24)

The
n
th currents
\mathcal{J}_{\mp}^{(n)}
are of spin
n
and their (anti-)holomorphic conservation implies the conservation of the associated conserved charges,

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}Q_{\pm}^{(n)}=0\ ,\qquad\qquad Q_{\pm}^{(n)}=\int d\sigma\,\mathcal{J}_{\pm}^{(n)}\ ,

(1.25)

which Poisson-commute with the non-local charges above
[
253
]
.

A canonical choice for the invariant
tensor
d_{A_{1}\cdots A_{n}}
is given by the trace,

d_{A_{1}\cdots A_{n}}={\rm Tr}[T_{(A_{1}}\cdots T_{A_{n})}]
.
The conservation of the currents (
1.23
) follows from the equation of motion and flatness of
J_{\mu}
, written in the 2d light-cone coordinates as

\displaystyle\partial_{+}J_{-}+\partial_{-}J_{+}=0\ ,\qquad\partial_{+}J_{-}-\partial_{-}J_{+}+[J_{+},J_{-}]=0\ ,

(1.26)

since these may be re-written as

\displaystyle\partial_{+}J_{-}=-\tfrac{1}{2}[J_{+},J_{-}]\ ,\qquad\partial_{-}J_{+}=\tfrac{1}{2}[J_{+},J_{-}]\ .

(1.27)

1.4.3
Symmetric space sigma model

Now let us consider instead a
\sigma
-model on a homogeneous coset space
G/H
, obtained by gauging a subgroup
H
of the
G_{R}
symmetry of the PCM (here
H\subset G
are both assumed to be simple Lie groups). The gauging procedure may be understood as (i) promoting the
H
global symmetry to a local one,
g\to g\,h(\xi)^{-1}
, (ii) writing down the transformation of the PCM Lagrangian (
1.18
),
\mathcal{L}\to-\tfrac{1}{2}{\rm h}\,{\rm Tr}[(J_{+}-h^{-1}\partial_{+}h)(J_{-}-h^{-1}\partial_{-}h)]
, and (iii) promoting the gauge parameter to a gauge connection,
h^{-1}\partial_{\pm}h\to A_{\pm}
(no longer fixed to be pure gauge). The resulting
G/H
theory,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=-\tfrac{1}{2}{\rm h}\,{\rm Tr}[(J_{+}-A_{+})(J_{-}-A_{-})]\ ,\qquad J_{\pm}=g^{-1}\partial_{\pm}g\ ,\ \ g\in G\ ,\quad A_{\pm}\in{\rm Lie}(H)\ ,

(1.28)

is invariant under the
H
gauge transformation

\displaystyle g\to gh\ ,\quad A_{\pm}\to h^{-1}A_{\pm}h+h^{-1}\partial_{\pm}h\ ,\qquad h(\xi)\in H\ .

(1.29)

The quadratic gauge field
A_{\pm}
in (
1.28
) is auxilliary (appearing without derivatives) and may be integrated out to obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=-\tfrac{1}{2}{\rm h}\,{\rm Tr}[J_{+}\,P_{G/H}\,J_{-}]\ ,

(1.30)

where
P_{G/H}
is the projector orthogonal to
{\rm Lie}(H)
in
{\rm Lie}(G)
with respect to the Killing form. The
H
gauge transformation (
1.29
) then reduces to

\displaystyle g\to g\,h\ ,\qquad h(\xi)\in H\ .

(1.31)

The theory also has a global
G
symmetry,
g\to g_{L}\,g
,
g_{L}\in G_{L}
corresponding to the isometry of the coset space
G/H
.

If
G/H
is assumed to be a
symmetric space
(meaning
{\rm Lie}(H)
is the even subalgebra under a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading of
{\rm Lie}(G)
), then these global and local symmetries uniquely determine the theory (
1.28
) or (
1.30
) up to the constant
{\rm h}
.
Like the PCM above, this automatically makes the theory renormalizable to all orders with just
{\rm h}
running. The 2-loop
\beta
-function following from (
1.8
) is (see App. B of
[
3
]
and particular cases in
[
200
,
199
]
)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}{\rm h}=2c_{{}_{G}}+4c_{{}_{G}}(c_{{}_{G}}-c_{{}_{H}}){\rm h}^{-1}\ .

(1.32)

In contrast, for non-symmetric cosets
G/H
, the theory (
1.30
) may not be renormalizable as
it should be understood as part of a multi-coupling theory
\mathcal{L}=-\tfrac{1}{2}{\rm Tr}[J_{+}\,\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\rm h}_{i}P_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}\big)\,J_{-}]
respecting the same symmetries, defined in terms a decomposition of the Lie algebra

{\rm Lie}(G)={\rm Lie}(H)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{p}_{i}
, with
\mathfrak{p}_{i}
being irreducible representations under the adjoint action of
H
, mutually orthogonal under the trace. For general cosets we may have
n>1
(i.e. multi-coupling), while symmetric spaces must have
n=1
(single-coupling) if
G
,
H
are simple.

The symmetric space condition on
G/H
is also sufficient for classical integrability of the model (
1.30
). The corresponding equations of motion are then of the zero-curvature form (
1.7
) with the Lax connection
[
31
]

\displaystyle L_{\pm}=B_{\pm}+z^{\pm 1}\,P_{\pm}\ ,\qquad B\equiv P_{H}J,\ \ P\equiv P_{G/H}J

(1.33)

Let us note that the symmetric space is actually a generalization of the PCM, since the PCM on a group
G
is equivalent to the
\sigma
-model on the symmetric space
G\times G/G
(and is obtained upon fixing the gauge
g^{(2)}=1
, where
(g^{(1)},g^{(2)})\in G\times G
, and replacing
{\rm h}\to 2{\rm h}
). All of the formulas here for symmetric spaces actually apply in that case, except that
G\times G
is not simple so the definition of
c_{{}_{H}}
in Appendix
A
should be suitably modified. Indeed the symmetric space
\beta
-function (
1.32
) agrees with the PCM one (
1.20
) upon replacing
c_{{}_{H}}\to\tfrac{1}{2}c_{{}_{G}}
and
{\rm h}\to 2{\rm h}
. The symmetric space Lax connection (
1.39
) in that case is related to the PCM one (
1.21
) by a gauge transformation.

Both non-local
[
32
]
and local
[
254
]
charges may be constructed similarly to the PCM case above. For the local charges, one now replaces
J\to P
in (
1.23
),

\displaystyle\partial_{\pm}\mathcal{J}_{\mp}^{(n)}=0\ ,\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\pm}^{(n)}=d_{A_{1}\cdots A_{n}}P_{\pm}^{A_{1}}\cdots P_{\pm}^{A_{n}}

(1.34)

\displaystyle d_{A_{1}\ldots A_{n}}=d_{(A_{1}\ldots A_{n})}\ ,\qquad\qquad{f^{A}}_{B(C}d_{A_{1}\cdots A_{n-1})A_{n}}=0\ .

(1.35)

The holomorphic conservation law in (
1.34
) follows again from the equation of motion and flatness of
J

[
254
]
(here
D^{B}_{\pm}\equiv\partial_{\pm}+{\rm ad}_{B_{\pm}}
),

\displaystyle D^{B}_{+}\,P_{-}+D^{B}_{-}\,P_{+}=0\ ,\qquad\partial_{+}J_{-}-\partial_{-}J_{+}+[J_{+},J_{-}]=0\ ,

(1.36)

since they now imply that

\displaystyle\partial_{\pm}\ P_{\mp}=[P_{\mp},B_{\pm}]\ .

(1.37)

1.4.4
Constructing Lax connections for other models

The integrable models that follow will admit the
same
flat Lax connections of the form (
1.21
) or (
1.33
) as the PCM or symmetric space (for models respectively built on groups
or symmetric spaces).
10
10

10

Of course, not all integrable
\sigma
-models will have Lax connections of this form. For example the bi-Yang-Baxter model
[
133
,
134
]
built on a group does not possess a flat, conserved current
\mathcal{A}_{\pm}
so its Lax connection is not of the form (
1.38
).
The difference is that, in each case,
J_{\pm}
will be replaced with some other model-dependent quantity in terms of the fields,

\displaystyle G\ :\qquad\quad

\displaystyle L_{\pm}=\tfrac{1}{2}(1+z^{\pm 1})\mathcal{A}_{\pm}\ ,\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\pm}\in{\rm Lie}{(G)}

(1.38)

\displaystyle G/H\ :\qquad\quad

\displaystyle L_{\pm}=\mathcal{B}_{\pm}+z^{\pm 1}\mathcal{P}_{\pm}\ ,\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\pm}\in{\rm Lie}{(H)}\ ,\ \ \mathcal{P}_{\pm}\in{\rm Lie}{(G)}/{\rm Lie}{(H)}

(1.39)

We note that admitting Lax connections of the same form does not imply classical equivalence between these different theories (not being related by a canonical transformation); e.g. for group-based models, it simply means they possess a flat, conserved current
\mathcal{A}_{\pm}
.

Non-local conserved charges are then obtained from the monodromy matrix as discussed above for the PCM. Local conserved charges are also constructed similarly to the cases discussed above, with the general form of the (anti-)holomorphic currents given by

\displaystyle\partial_{\pm}\mathcal{J}_{\mp}^{(n)}=0\ ,\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\pm}^{(n)}=\begin{cases}d_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}\mathcal{A}_{\pm}^{a_{1}}\cdots\mathcal{A}_{\pm}^{a_{n}}\ &\text{(group}\ G)\\
d_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}\mathcal{P}_{\pm}^{a_{1}}\cdots\mathcal{P}_{\pm}^{a_{n}}\ &\text{(symmetric space}\ G/H)\end{cases}

(1.40)

\displaystyle d_{a_{1}\ldots a_{n}}=d_{(a_{1}\ldots a_{n})}\ ,\qquad\qquad{f^{a}}_{b(c}d_{a_{1}\cdots a_{n-1})a}=0\ .

(1.41)

The conservation of the currents (
1.40
) follows from the equations of motion

\displaystyle G\ :\qquad

\displaystyle\partial_{+}\mathcal{A}_{-}+\partial_{-}\mathcal{A}_{+}=0\ ,\qquad F_{+-}(\mathcal{A})=0\ ,

(1.42)

\displaystyle G/H\ :\qquad

\displaystyle D^{\mathcal{B}}_{+}\mathcal{P}_{-}+D^{\mathcal{B}}_{-}\mathcal{P}_{+}=0\ ,\qquad F_{+-}(\mathcal{B}+\mathcal{P})=0\ ,

(1.43)

since these may be re-written as

\displaystyle G\ :\qquad

\displaystyle\partial_{\pm}\mathcal{A}_{\mp}=\tfrac{1}{2}[A_{\mp},A_{\pm}]\ ,

(1.44)

\displaystyle G/H\ :\qquad

\displaystyle\partial_{\pm}\mathcal{P}_{\mp}=[P_{\mp},B_{\pm}]\ ,\ \ \ \qquad\ \ F_{+-}(\mathcal{B})+[\mathcal{P}_{+},\mathcal{P}_{-}]=0\ .

(1.45)

1.4.5
T-duality and non-abelian duality

For
\sigma
-models with global symmetries, or isometries of the target space, one may “dualize” the symmetry to obtain a dual
\sigma
-model , with certain observables being related between the original and dual theories (see the review
[
33
]
and refs. therein). As we shall review below, the duality procedure may be carried out at the level of the path integral and applies to the quantum theory
[
129
]
; in particular duality is understood to commute with the quantum RG flow, and to map Weyl-invariant theories (string solutions) to Weyl-invariant theories. However, we stress that the procedures as discussed below are subject to quantum
\alpha^{\prime}
corrections, with the integrals producing quantum determinants.

First we shall consider
abelian T-duality
for a model with a
U(1)
isometry, which we take for simplicity to be

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=g_{ij}(x)\,\partial x^{i}\partial x^{j}+f(x)\,(\partial y)^{2}

(1.46)

Here the isometry corresponds to constant shifts of
y
, and the
x
coordinates are “spectators”. Gauging the isometry
\partial_{\mu}y\to\partial_{\mu}y-p_{\mu}
and imposing the condition
\epsilon^{\mu\nu}\,\partial_{\mu}p_{\nu}=0
using a Lagrange multiplier field
\tilde{y}
gives a gauge-invariant theory (under
y\to y+u
,
p_{\mu}\to p_{\mu}+\partial_{\mu}u
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\rm int}=g_{ij}(x)\,\partial x^{i}\partial x^{j}+f(x)\,(\partial y-p)^{2}+\tilde{y}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu}\,\partial_{\mu}p_{\nu}\ .

(1.47)

Fixing the gauge
y=0
gives the “interpolating model”,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\rm int}=g_{ij}(x)\,\partial x^{i}\partial x^{j}+f(x)\,p^{2}+\tilde{y}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu}\,\partial_{\mu}p_{\nu}\ .

(1.48)

On one hand, integrating out
\tilde{y}
imposes that
p_{\mu}
is pure gauge and locally given by
p_{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}y
, giving back the original model (
1.46
). On the other hand, we may instead integrate out the non-dynamical quadratic field
p
(after integrating by parts
\tilde{y}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu}\,\partial_{\mu}p_{\nu}\to-\partial_{\mu}\tilde{y}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu}\,p_{\nu})
) to get the dual
\sigma
-model with the
(x,\tilde{y})
directions

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{L}}=g_{ij}(x)\,\partial x^{i}\partial x^{j}+\frac{1}{f(x)}\,(\partial\tilde{y})^{2}\ .

(1.49)

The dual theory still has a
U(1)
isometry, corresponding now to shifts of
\tilde{y}
. In summary, the dual pair of models are related by
f\to\tfrac{1}{f}
,
y\to\tilde{y}
, and they were obtained by integrating out different fields from the interpolating theory (
1.48
). This procedure is easily generalized to cases with off-diagonal metric terms and
{\rm B}
-fields, with the resulting transformation being known as the ‘Buscher rules’
[
129
]
.

Now let us consider
non-abelian duality
(NAD), where the above procedure is generalized to non-abelian isometries
[
154
,
155
,
34
]
. We shall focus on the examples of the PCM and symmetric space
\sigma
-model (considered above), and the procedure is the same for other theories with non-abelian global symmetries. Starting from one of these theories

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=-\tfrac{1}{2}h\,{\rm Tr}[J_{+}\,P\,J_{-}]\ ,\qquad\quad P=\begin{cases}1\qquad&\text{(PCM)}\ ,\\
P_{G/H}\ \qquad&\text{(symmetric space)}\ ,\end{cases}

(1.50)

we shall dualize the
G_{L}
symmetry
g\to kg
. As above, we begin by gauging the symmetry and imposing that the gauge field is pure gauge with a Lagrange multiplier field
v
(here
F_{+-}(A)\equiv\partial_{+}A_{-}-\partial_{-}A_{+}+[A_{+},A_{-}]
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=-\tfrac{1}{2}{\rm h}\,{\rm Tr}[(J_{+}+g^{-1}A_{+}g)\,P\,(J_{-}+g^{-1}A_{-}g)+v\,F_{+-}(A)]\ .

(1.51)

This resulting theory is invariant under the gauge transformation
g\to kg
,
A_{\pm}\to kA_{\pm}k^{-1}-\partial_{\pm}kk^{-1}
. Fixing the gauge
g=1
, we obtain the interpolating theory

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\rm int}=-\tfrac{1}{2}{\rm h}\,{\rm Tr}[A_{+}\,P\,A_{-}-v\,F_{+-}(A)]\ .

(1.52)

On one hand, integrating out
v
imposes that
A_{\pm}
is flat and locally of the form
A_{\pm}=g^{-1}\partial_{\pm}g
, giving back the original theory (
1.50
). On the other hand, after integrating by parts,
A_{\pm}
appears quadratically without derivatives; integrating it out yields the NAD
\sigma
-model depending on
v
,

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{L}}=-\tfrac{1}{2}{\rm h}\,{\rm Tr}[\partial_{+}v\,\frac{1}{P+{\rm ad}_{v}}\,\partial_{-}v]\ .

(1.53)

Unlike abelian T-duality, non-abelian duality destroys the original symmetry, meaning the original
G_{L}
symmetry is not present in the NAD theory (
1.53
). Thus, at least within the framework of non-abelian duality, one cannot ‘‘dualize back’’ from the NAD model to the original one.
11
11

11

In fact the
G_{L}
symmetry becomes a Poisson-Lie symmetry of the NAD model (
1.53
) (see eq. (
1.72
) below) and, starting from the NAD theory, one can dualize back to original theory by Poisson-Lie duality.

The path-integral derivations of abelian and non-abelian duality apply in particular at the level of the classical equations of motion (they may be formulated as canonical transformations; see, e.g.,
[
63
]
and refs. therein). Hence these dualities preserve the property of classical integrability. For example the NAD of the PCM ((
1.53
) with
P=1
) inherits a flat Lax connection of the same form (
1.38
) as the PCM with
\mathcal{A}_{\pm}=A_{\pm}(v)
taking its value obtained in the quadratic integral in (
1.52
),

\displaystyle L_{\pm}=\tfrac{1}{2}(1+z^{\pm 1})A_{\pm}(v)\ ,\qquad A_{\pm}(v)=\frac{1}{{\rm ad}_{v}\mp 1}\partial_{\pm}v\ .

(1.54)

1.4.6
PCM with Wess-Zumino term

The PCM (
1.18
) is not the most general
G
-valued
\sigma
-model with
G_{L}\times G_{R}
global symmetry (
1.19
). In fact, there is also a
{\rm B}
-field coupling respecting this symmetry called the
Wess-Zumino
(WZ) term, defined by
[
35
,
36
,
196
]

\displaystyle{\rm H}\equiv d{\rm B}=\tfrac{1}{12}k\,{\rm Tr}[J\wedge J\wedge J]\ ,\qquad{\rm B}\equiv\tfrac{1}{2}{\rm B}_{mn}dx^{m}\wedge dx^{n}\ .

(1.55)

The free coefficient
k
is called the ‘WZ level’.

One can check that
d{\rm H}=0
, implying the local existence of a
{\rm B}
-field satisfying
{\rm H}=d{\rm B}
. Although
{\rm B}
is not globally defined (the expressions for
{\rm B}
in different coordinate charts may be related by gauge transformations
{\rm B}\to{\rm B}+d\lambda
), the
\sigma
-model action (
1.6
) is well-defined up to an additive constant. Let us take the 2d space to be the Euclidean plane
\mathbb{C}
(after analytic continuation), which we compactify to the Riemann sphere
S^{2}=\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}
. Then a coordinate-invariant way to write down the WZ term’s contribution to the action is
12
12

12

The factor of
i
in (
1.56
) results from the Wick rotation of the antisymmetric Levi-Civita tensor
\varepsilon^{\mu\nu}
in the
\sigma
-model (
1.3
).

\displaystyle S_{{\rm WZ}}=\frac{i}{2\pi}\int_{B_{3}}{\rm H}\ ,

(1.56)

where
B_{3}
is the solid ball whose boundary is
S^{2}
. One is instructed to continue the fields in an arbitrary way from the boundary into the interior of
B_{3}
(this is always possible since Lie groups have trivial 2nd homotopy group
\pi_{2}(G)=0
). Since
{\rm H}
is closed then its integral (
1.56
) is independent of the choice of continuation of the fields, up to an overall topological ‘winding number’ constant. For compact simple groups
13
13

13

For non-compact groups there may be no ambiguity at all and
k
may be any real number.
this gives an ambiguous contribution
\Delta S_{{\rm WZ}}=2\pi ink
with
n
an arbitrary integer in the homotopy group
\pi_{3}(G)=\mathbb{Z}
, which can be shown to classify the configurations. The Euclidean path integral
Z=\int Dx\,e^{-S[x]}
is then well-defined as long as the level
k
is quantized to be an integer.

We shall denote by ‘PCM
k
’ the PCM with WZ term with level
k
(with
\mathcal{L}_{{\rm WZ}}
being a local density for
S_{{\rm WZ}}
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=-\tfrac{1}{2}{\rm h}\,{\rm Tr}[J_{+}J_{-}]+k\,\mathcal{L}_{{\rm WZ}}(g)\ .

(1.57)

The PCM
k
is classically integrable for arbitrary values of the couplings
{\rm h}
and
k
, with the Lax connection taking the same form (
1.38
) as the PCM in terms of a modified flat, conserved current
[
38
]

\displaystyle L_{\pm}=\tfrac{1}{2}(1+z^{\pm 1})\mathcal{A}_{\pm}\ ,\qquad\qquad\mathcal{A}_{\pm}=(1\pm\tfrac{k}{h})J_{\pm}\ .

(1.58)

It is a general fact that WZ levels do not run under the RG flow (even for non-compact groups where they are not quantized). This is because the antisymmetric
{\rm B}
-field terms in the
\sigma
-model
\beta
-functions (
1.9
) are all globally defined, so cannot be proportional to WZ terms whose
{\rm B}
-fields are not globally defined. The WZ term does, however, modify the RG flow of the coupling
{\rm h}
, and the PCM
k
has 2-loop
\beta
-function

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}{\rm h}=(1-\tfrac{k^{2}}{{\rm h}^{2}})\big(c_{{}_{G}}+\tfrac{1}{2}c_{{}_{G}}^{2}(1-3\tfrac{k^{2}}{{\rm h}^{2}})+\ldots\big)\ ,\qquad\frac{d}{dt}k=0\ .

(1.59)

1.4.7
Wess-Zumino-Witten model

The PCM
k

\beta
-function (
1.59
) admits a fixed point
{\rm h}=k
(with the other fixed point
{\rm h}=-k
related by 2d parity or
g\to g^{-1}
), which is in fact an exactly conformal theory called the
Wess Zumino Witten
(WZW) model,

\displaystyle k\,\mathcal{L}_{G}(g)\equiv k\big(-\tfrac{1}{2}\,{\rm Tr}[J_{+}J_{-}]+\mathcal{L}_{{\rm WZ}}(g)\big)\ .

(1.60)

For this special value of the coupling, the
G_{L}\times G_{R}
symmetry enhances to a “chiral gauge” symmetry
[
196
]
,
14
14

14

In Minkowski signature
\xi^{\pm}
in (
1.61
) are the same 2d light-cone coordinates used above. In Euclidean signature these become complex-conjugate cooordinates
z
,
\bar{z}
on the complex plane.

\displaystyle g\to g_{L}(\xi^{-})\,g\,g_{R}(\xi^{+})\ ,\qquad g_{L}(\xi^{-})\in G\ ,\quad g_{R}(\xi^{+})\in G\ .

(1.61)

At the WZW fixed point
h=k
, the equation of motion becomes the statement of holomorphic and anti-holomorphic conservation of the Noether currents corresponding to the chiral gauge symmetries (
1.61
),

\displaystyle\partial_{-}J_{+}=0\iff\partial_{+}K_{-}=0\ ,\qquad J_{+}\equiv g^{-1}\partial_{+}g\ ,\quad K_{-}\equiv\partial_{-}gg^{-1}\ .

(1.62)

This equation is explicitly solvable in terms of arbitrary
G
-valued (anti-)holomorpic functions,

\displaystyle g=g_{1}(\xi^{-})\,g_{2}(\xi^{+})\ ,

(1.63)

with the currents
J_{+}=J_{+}(\xi^{+})
and
K_{-}=K_{-}(\xi^{-})
being holomorphic and anti-holomorphic on-shell. Computing the currents’ Poisson brackets and canonically quantizing, one finds that they satisfy the affine Kac-Moody algebra of
G
with level
k
, with the corresponding OPE,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} J_{+}^{a}(z)J_{+}^{b}(z^{\prime})\sim{}&{}\frac{1}{k}\Big[\frac{{\kappa}^{ab}}{(z-z^{\prime})^{2}}+\frac{if^{ab}{}_{c}J_{+}^{c}(z^{\prime})}{z-z^{\prime}}\Big]\qquad(+\text{ regular terms})\ .\end{aligned}

(1.64)

One can show that the affine Kac-Moody algebra contains the Virasoro algebra, i.e. the current algebra OPEs (
1.64
) imply that a certain local operator
T(z)\equiv\frac{k^{2}}{2(k+c_{{}_{G}})}{\rm Tr}[:\!J_{+}J_{+}\!:(z)]
satisfies the Virasoro OPE,

\displaystyle T(z)T(z^{\prime})\sim\frac{c}{2(z-z^{\prime})^{4}}+\frac{2T(z^{\prime})}{(z-z^{\prime})^{2}}+\frac{\partial T(z^{\prime})}{z-z^{\prime}}\ .

(1.65)

The antiholomorphic currents
K_{-}
satisfy the same Kac-Moody algebra and generate another copy of the Virasoro algebra. Hence the WZW model is exactly conformal, being invariant under the 2d conformal algebra (two copies of the Virasoro algebra). Correspondingly the
\beta
-function (
1.59
) will vanish to all orders in a particular subtraction scheme (see the discussion around eq. (
3.3
) below). The corresponding conformal field theory, and particularly its correlation functions, were studied in
[
39
]
.

1.4.8
Gauged WZW model

There is a closely related family of 2d CFTs based on coset spaces
G/H

[
42
]
described by the
gauged Wess Zumino Witten
(gWZW) Lagrangian
[
43
,
116
]
,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} k\,\mathcal{L}_{G/H}(g,A)={}&{}k\big(\mathcal{L}_{G}(g)+{\rm Tr}[J_{+}A_{-}-K_{-}A_{+}+g^{-1}A_{+}gA_{-}-A_{+}A_{-}]\big)\ ,\\
&J=g^{-1}dg\ ,\quad K=dgg^{-1}\ ,\qquad g\in G\ ,\quad A_{\pm}\in{\rm Lie}(H)\ .\end{aligned}

(1.66)

Like the symmetric space
\sigma
-model (
1.28
), the gWZW model depends on a quadratic 2d gauge field (that may be integrated out to obtain a
\sigma
-model ) and is gauge-invariant under an action of the subgroup
H
,

\displaystyle g\to h^{-1}gh\ ,\quad A_{\pm}\to h^{-1}A_{\pm}h+h^{-1}\partial_{\pm}h\ .

(1.67)

The classical gWZW
\sigma
-model (
1.66
) receives an infinite tower of
\alpha^{\prime}
corrections in order to be exactly conformal
[
113
,
114
,
115
,
117
,
118
]
. See the
SU(1,1)/U(1)
example in eq. (
2.4
) and the review in Section
4.1
below.

1.4.9
Integrable
\eta
-deformation

Starting from an integrable
\sigma
-model like the PCM, one may consider squashing or deforming the target space geometry in a way that preserves the property of classical integrability. One such integrable deformation of the PCM, called the
Yang-Baxter deformation
or
\eta
-deformation
, is given by
[
131
]

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=-\tfrac{1}{2}{\rm h}\,{\rm Tr}[J_{+}\,\frac{1}{1-\varkappa R}J_{-}]\ ,

(1.68)

where
\varkappa
is the deformation parameter. The deformation is built from a linear operator
R:{\rm Lie}(G)\to{\rm Lie}(G)
, antisymmetric with respect to the Killing form (i.e.
{\rm Tr}[xR(y)]=-{\rm Tr}[R(x)y]
) and solving the modified classical Yang-Baxter equation,

\displaystyle[Rx,Ry]-R([Rx,y]+[x,Ry])+c^{2}[x,y]=0\ ,\qquad x,y\in{\rm Lie}(G)\ .

(1.69)

With
R
satisfying these conditions, the
\eta
-deformed PCM (
1.68
) is classically integrable with the Lax connection again taking the the same form (
1.38
) as the PCM, now built from a different flat, conserved current
[
133
]

\displaystyle L_{\pm}=\tfrac{1}{2}(1+z^{\pm 1})\mathcal{A}_{\pm}\ ,\qquad\qquad\mathcal{A}_{\pm}=-(1+\varkappa^{2})\text{Ad}_{g}\frac{1}{1\pm\varkappa R}J_{\pm}\ .

(1.70)

One can check that the integrability of the model (i.e. the equivalence between the flatness of (
1.70
) and the equations of motion of (
1.68
)) relies on
R
being a solution of the Yang-Baxter equation (
1.69
). In general there may be various solutions of the Yang-Baxter equation and their classification is unknown. We shall focus on a particular class of canonical solutions
in the “non-split inhomogeneous” case, i.e. taking
c^{2}>0
in (
1.69
).
15
15

15

We shall then rescale
R
to set
c^{2}=1
. There are also other types of solutions with
c^{2}>0
. The “split inhomogeneous” (
c^{2}<0
) solutions can only exist for non-compact groups and include solutions of the same canonical type as (
1.71
); the “homogeneous” (
c=0
) solutions include so-called “abelian” and “Jordanian” solutions.
Given a choice of Cartan-Weyl basis
\{h_{i},e_{\alpha},e_{-\alpha}\}
for
{\rm Lie}(G)
, where
h_{i}
are the Cartan generators,
e_{\alpha}
the positive roots and
e_{-\alpha}
the negative roots, we shall assume a canonical choice of
R
-matrix (see Section 2.2 of
[
133
]
and refs. there) solving (
1.69
),

\displaystyle R(h_{i})=0\ ,\qquad R(e_{\alpha})=-ie_{\alpha}\ ,\qquad R(e_{-\alpha})=ie_{-\alpha}\ .

(1.71)

This
R
-matrix, and the associated
\eta
-deformed model (
1.68
), depend on the choice of Cartan-Weyl basis. However, if one restricts to the case of compact simple groups, then there is only choice of Cartan-Weyl basis (up to equivalence by inner automorphisms).

The associated
\eta
-deformation in (
1.68
) breaks the manifest global symmetry
G_{L}\times G_{R}
of the PCM to the subgroup
G_{L}\times C
where
C=U(1)^{r}
is the Cartan subgroup of
G
commuting with the
R
-matrix. The
G_{R}
isometry of the target space is actually deformed to a
Poisson-Lie (PL) symmetry

[
131
]
, with the associated Killing vectors
\{v^{m}_{a}\,\partial_{m}\}_{a}
being replaced by vectors satisfying

\displaystyle L_{v_{c}}(G+B)_{mn}=-f^{ab}{}_{c}\,v_{a}^{p}\,v_{b}^{q}\,(G+B)_{mp}\,(G+B)_{qn}\ .

(1.72)

PL symmetry is thought to be related to a semi-classical limit of quantum group symmetry
[
45
]
, and indeed the PL symmetries of the
\eta
-deformed models correspond to
non-local
conserved charges (that can be extracted from the monodromy matrix), the analogs of the leading Noether charges appearing in the expansion (
1.22
) for the undeformed models. These non-local charges are found to have a classical Poisson bracket algebra obtained from a semi-classical limit of a quantum group algebra
[
137
,
45
]
. For this reason it is assumed that the full quantum
\eta
-deformed theories have quantum group symmetry, and this is the symmetry that has been assumed when bootstrapping their exact integrable S-matrices
[
57
,
47
,
58
]
.

One can also simultaneously deform both the
G_{L}
and
G_{R}
parts of the global symmetry to obtain the integrable ‘bi-Yang-Baxter’
\sigma
-model
[
133
,
134
]
. The deformations can be generalized
[
237
,
219
]
to include WZ terms; additionally including ‘TsT transformations’
[
174
]
gives a 4-parameter integrable deformation of the PCM
[
44
,
107
]
.

Theories with PL symmetry can be dualized in a process called
Poisson-Lie duality

[
177
]
, generalizing non-abelian duality and abelian T-duality (see Section
1.4.5
). Under PL duality, the
\eta
-models (
1.68
),(
1.73
) are mapped
[
151
,
158
,
156
,
157
,
159
]
to another class of integrable deformed models called
\lambda
-models, which we shall discuss in the next section.

A similar integrable
\eta
-deformation exists
[
137
]
for the symmetric space
\sigma
-model ,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=-\tfrac{1}{2}{\rm h}\,{\rm Tr}[J_{+}\,P_{G/H}\,\frac{1}{1-\varkappa\,R_{g}P_{G/H}}\,J_{-}]\ ,\qquad\qquad R_{g}\equiv\text{Ad}_{g}^{-1}R\,\text{Ad}_{g}\ .

(1.73)

The global
G_{L}
symmetry of the symmetric space is thus deformed to a PL symmetry, with only a Cartan subgroup remaining as a manifest isometry (and the
H_{R}
gauge invariance remaining preserved).
The model is classically integrable with Lax connection of the same form (
1.39
) as the symmetric space in terms of different quantities
\mathcal{B},\mathcal{P}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} L_{\pm}=\mathcal{B}_{\pm}+z^{\pm 1}\mathcal{P}_{\pm}\ ,\qquad&\mathcal{B}_{\pm}=P_{H}A_{\pm}\ ,\quad\mathcal{P}_{\pm}=\sqrt{1+\varkappa^{2}}P_{G/H}A_{\pm}\\
&A_{\pm}=\frac{1}{1\pm\varkappa R_{g}P_{G/H}}J_{\pm}\end{aligned}

(1.74)

In agreement with the conjecture that integrable
\sigma
-models are stable under the RG flow, the
\eta
-deformed models are known to be renormalizable at the leading 1-loop order with just the couplings
h
and
\varkappa
running
[
213
,
161
,
212
]
16
16

16

The renormalizability and
\beta
-functions for the coset case (
1.76
) have only been directly studied in special cases, e.g. in
[
157
]
. However, Poisson-Lie duality has been shown to be a symmetry of the 1-loop
\beta
-functions
[
213
]
and, given that the
\eta
-models are understood to be related to the
\lambda
-models by PL duality
[
151
,
158
,
156
,
157
,
159
]
, the expression (
1.76
) may be derived from the corresponding
\lambda
-model one.

\displaystyle G\,:\qquad

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}{\rm h}=c_{{}_{G}}(1+\varkappa^{2})^{2}\ ,\qquad

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\varkappa=c_{{}_{G}}{\rm h}^{-1}\varkappa(1+\varkappa^{2})^{2}\ ,

(1.75)

\displaystyle G/H\,:\qquad

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}{\rm h}=2c_{{}_{G}}(1+\varkappa^{2})\ ,\qquad

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\varkappa=2c_{{}_{G}}{\rm h}^{-1}\varkappa(1+\varkappa^{2})\ .

(1.76)

This is a non-trivial example of the integrability–RG flow relationship because the
\eta
-deformed models are not
automatically
RG-stable due to manifest symmetries. Their stability is instead thought to be due to their hidden integrable symmetries.

1.4.10
Integrable
\lambda
-deformation

In
[
166
]
Tseytlin considered a “universal class” of models related to gWZW models
\mathcal{L}_{G/F}
by deformations parametrized by a constant linear operator
Q:{\rm Lie}(G)\to{\rm Lie}(G)
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\rm Tseytlin}=k\big(\mathcal{L}_{G/F}(g,A)+{\rm Tr}[A_{+}(Q-1)A_{-}]\big)\ ,\qquad g\in G\ ,\quad A_{\pm}\in{\rm Lie}(F)\ .

(1.77)

The 1-loop conformal invariance conditions were computed, taking the form of polynomial constraints on

Q
.

Forgetting about conformal invariance, one may instead ask which choices of
Q
correspond to integrable theories. A particular class of integrable examples of (
1.77
) was later discovered
[
138
,
139
]
, called the
\lambda
-model
based on a either group
G
or a symmetric space
G/H
. The model is defined by the choice
F=G
and
Q=1-(\lambda^{-1}-1)P
in (
1.77
), or

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}=k\big(\mathcal{L}_{G/G}(g,A)-(\lambda^{-1}-1){\rm Tr}[A_{+}\,P\,A_{-}]\big)\ .

(1.78)

Here
k
is a WZ level and the coupling
\lambda
is a deformation parameter (we will sometimes use a redefined coupling
{\kappa}\equiv\frac{1-\lambda}{1+\lambda}
for convenience). The two cases of
G
or
G/H
are encoded in the choice of the projector
P
,

\displaystyle P=\begin{cases}1\qquad\qquad&(G\text{ case})\ ,\\
P_{G/H}\qquad\qquad&(G/H\text{ case})\ ,\end{cases}

(1.79)

so that the coset case has an
H
gauge invariance (see eq. (
1.67
)), while the group case has no gauge symmetry at all.

In the group case there is a global
G
symmetry,
g\to kgk^{-1}
,
A_{\pm}\to kA_{\pm}k^{-1}
,
k\in G
; whereas the coset case does not generally have any continuous global symmetries.
In both cases, the
\lambda
-model (
1.78
) is invariant under a

\mathbb{Z}_{2}
transformation of the fields and coupling constants
[
152
,
151
]
(see also
[
168
,
166
]
),

17
17

17

Such a symmetry was also discussed for the models (
1.77
) in
[
166
]
(see footnotes 3 and 6 there).

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &k\to-k\ ,\qquad\qquad\lambda\to\lambda^{-1}\ ,\\
&g\to g^{-1}\ ,\qquad A_{+}\to A_{+}-(1-\lambda^{-1})PA_{+}\ ,\qquad A_{-}\to gA_{-}g^{-1}-K_{-}\ .\end{aligned}

(1.80)

As we shall find below in Chapter
4
, the preservation of this symmetry at the quantum level may require a particular choice of
regularization scheme.

One may integrate out the 2d gauge field in (
1.78
) to obtain a
\sigma
-model depending on the scalar
g\in G
(here
\mathcal{L}_{G}
is the WZW Lagrangian),

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathcal{L}_{\lambda}=k\big(\mathcal{L}_{G}(g)+{\rm Tr}[J_{+}M(g)^{-1}K_{-}]\big)\ ,\qquad&J\equiv g^{-1}dg\ ,\ \ K\equiv dgg^{-1}\ ,\\
&M(g)\equiv Ad_{g}-1+(1-\lambda^{-1})P\ .\end{aligned}

(1.81)

The
\lambda
-model is an integrable deformation of a WZW model on
G
or a gauged WZW model on
G/H
, obtained in the limit
\lambda=0
when the projected parts
PA_{\pm}
decouple and are set to zero by their equations.

In another interesting limit,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &k\to\infty\ ,\qquad\lambda=1-\tfrac{{\rm h}}{2k}\ ,\qquad g=e^{-\tfrac{h}{2k}v}\ ,\\
&v\in{\rm Lie}(G)\text{ and }{\rm h}\text{ fixed}\ ,\end{aligned}

(1.82)

the
\lambda
-model becomes the non-abelian dual theory (
1.53
) corresponding to either the PCM or symmetric space
\sigma
-model . The theory (
1.78
) with unintegrated gauge field becomes the corresponding “interpolating model” (
1.52
).

The
\lambda
-models (
1.81
) (after integrating out
A_{\pm}
) admit flat Lax connections of the same form (
1.38
),(
1.39
) as the PCM/symmetric space, but with modified currents,

\displaystyle G\,:\qquad

\displaystyle L_{\pm}=\tfrac{1}{2}(1+z^{\pm 1})\mathcal{A}_{\pm}\ ,\qquad

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\pm}=\frac{2}{1+\lambda}A_{\pm}\ ,

(1.83)

\displaystyle G/H\,:\qquad

\displaystyle L_{\pm}=\mathcal{B}_{\pm}+z^{\pm 1}\mathcal{P}_{\pm}\ ,\qquad

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\pm}=P_{H}A_{\pm}\ ,\ \ P_{\pm}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}P_{G/H}A_{\pm}\ ,

(1.84)

where
A_{\pm}=A_{\pm}(g)
are taken to be their on-shell values.

In line with the conjecture, the
\lambda
-model is known to be renormalizable at 1-loop with only
\lambda
running
18
18

18

The 1-loop
\beta
-functions (
1.85
),(
1.86
)
can also be extracted from
[
166
]
. Note that the WZ level is not running due to general arguments.

[
195
,
153
]

\displaystyle G\,:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}k=0\ ,\quad\frac{d}{dt}\lambda=-\frac{2c_{{}_{G}}}{k}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{(1+\lambda)^{2}}\ ,

(1.85)

\displaystyle G/H\,:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}k=0\ ,\quad\frac{d}{dt}\lambda=-\frac{c_{{}_{G}}\lambda}{k}\ .

(1.86)

These
\beta
-functions respect the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
transformation (
1.80
), being invariant under the transformation
(k,\lambda)\to(-k,\lambda^{-1})
of the couplings.

1.4.11
Integrable-deformed string backgrounds

Although we have focussed on the simplest case of bosonic theories, and relaxed the condition of Weyl invariance (i.e. that the target space is a string background), the models discussed above do admit Weyl-invariant superstring generalizations. The Green-Schwarz action for type IIB strings on
AdS_{5}\times S^{5}
with RR flux is a
\sigma
-model on the
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
“semi-symmetric” coset
PSU(2,2|4)/[SO(1,4)\times SO(5)]
with a particular WZ-type
{\rm B}
-field term (here
P^{(i)}
is the projector onto the
i
th graded part of the algebra)
[
9
]

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &S=\tfrac{1}{4\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int\,d^{2}\xi\mathcal{L}\ ,\qquad\mathcal{L}=-\tfrac{1}{2}R^{2}\ {\rm STr}\big[\sqrt{-h}h^{\mu\nu}J_{\mu}^{(2)}J_{\nu}^{(2)}+{\epsilon}^{\mu\nu}J_{\mu}^{(1)}J_{\nu}^{(3)}\big]\ ,\\
&J^{(i)}=P^{(i)}J\ ,\qquad J=g^{-1}dg\ ,\qquad g\in PSU(2,2|4)\ ,\end{aligned}

(1.87)

This is a generalization of the symmetric space model (
1.30
) above (replacing the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-automorphism by
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
) and indeed the bosonic part of the model (setting fermions to zero) is simply the symmetric space
\sigma
-model on
AdS_{5}\times S^{5}

={SO(2,4)\over SO(1,4)}\times{SO(6)\over SO(5)}
, with
R
being the radius of the
AdS_{5}
and
S^{5}
spaces.

The
\eta
-deformation
[
176
,
46
]
and
\lambda
-deformation
[
175
]
can both be generalized to integrable deformations of semi-symmetric spaces.
The
\eta
-deformed
AdS_{5}\times S^{5}
model was problematically found to be scale- but not Weyl-invariant, with the background solving only the generalized supergravity equations
[
47
,
48
,
64
]
, and the perturbative light-cone S-matrix did not directly match the conjectured exact S-matrix
[
47
]
. It was then explained
[
184
]
that, to maintain Weyl invariance, one must in fact use the
purely fermionic
Dynkin diagram for the superalgebra
\mathfrak{psu}(2,2|4)
to construct a
unimodular

R
-matrix. In the recent paper
[
185
]
, an exact S-matrix was conjectured that is different from the previous attempt, but related by a so-called ‘twist’; this S-matrix indeed matches tree-level perturbative results.

Much remains to be
understood about these integrable-deformed string backgrounds.
For example one significant challenge is to understand the spectrum of the deformed theories
(a direction in which some progress was made for the
\eta
-deformation
[
49
]
through the TBA and Quantum Spectral Curve).
Examining the properties of the
bosonic
\eta
-model and
\lambda
-model
can provide
valuable insights into their superstring counterparts. For example, the
relation between the
\eta
- and
\lambda
-models by Poisson-Lie duality was first understood in the bosonic case
[
151
,
158
,
156
,
157
,
159
]
.
It is thus natural to first explore the question of quantum corrections and the higher-loop RG equations by
studying the bosonic models.
In Section
6.4
below, we shall comment on the possible applications of our approach to
the integrable-deformed string backgrounds.

It is also worth emphasizing that the bosonic models are of interest in their
own right in the general context of investigating integrable 2d theories.
The
\eta
-model has played an important role in generalizing the duality
[
101
,
102
,
103
]

between the deformed
O(3)
and
O(4)
sigma models and massive integrable QFTs
to higher-rank groups and cosets
[
104
,
105
,
108
]
and cosets of supergroups
[
135
]
. While the
dual theories are quantum-exact, the
\sigma
-model side of the duality is only
understood so far to leading order in the loop expansion. Thus, after
finding quantum corrections to integrable
\sigma
-models consistent with
renormalizability, it may then be interesting to study their compatibility with this duality.

There are also many other known examples of integrable string backgrounds, including those based on
lower-dimensional AdS spaces
[
172
,
173
]
and their integrable deformations
[
183
,
91
,
92
,
184
,
93
]
;

\beta
-deformations (or TsT-deformations)
[
174
,
94
]
; and more general homogeneous Yang-Baxter deformations
[
95
,
96
,
183
]
.

1.4.12
Quantum integrable S-matrices

The presence of higher conserved charges places strict constraints on the possible scattering behaviour of a theory
[
51
,
50
]
(see also the review
[
54
]
). Particle production is prohibited, i.e. the number of incoming and outgoing particles are equal; the set of initial momenta equals the set of final momenta; and the S-matrix must factorize into a product of
2\to 2
S-matrices. The consistency of this factorization implies that the
2\to 2
S-matrix satisfies the Yang-Baxter equation.

This special scattering behaviour is expected for integrable theories, and has been proven assuming the presence of at least two higher-spin conserved charges satisfying certain technical conditions
[
50
]
. S-matrices of this type may be seen as “almost free”, and their existence is a special feature of 2-dimensions since in higher dimensions the presence of higher-spin charges implies triviality of the S-matrix (i.e. a free theory) by the Coleman-Mandula theorem.

For certain theories there are various arguments for the preservation of integrability at the quantum level. For example, in the case of certain principal chiral models, it has been argued
[
252
]
using consistency with global symmetry that the higher-spin conservation laws (
1.23
) cannot be modifed at the quantum level by anything other than a divergence, i.e. a quantum-corrected form of the current must still conserved to all orders in perturbation theory (see also
[
52
]
in the context of the
O(n)
-model, i.e. the
\sigma
-model on a sphere
S^{n-1}={O(n)\over O(n-1)}
.). The non-local charges of the
O(n)
model were also argued to persist at the quantum level
[
53
]
and to imply factorization of the S-matrix with no particle production.

In the important paper
[
51
]
, Zamolodchikov and Zamolodchikov started by assuming quantum integrability and the dynamical generation of a mass-gap for the
O(n)
-model. Using also the model’s symmetries and assuming a ‘minimal’ solution for the phase ambiguity (with minimal number of poles on the physical sheet), they uniquely determined the exact S-matrix for non-perturbative quantum particles.

The
O(4)
-model, equivalent to the
S^{3}

\sigma
-model or
SU(2)
PCM, was later solved explicitly
[
40
]
by proving equivalence to a Thirring-type fermionic model with an infinite number of flavours, finding agreement with the Zamolodchikov-Zamolodchikov S-matrix. This exact solution was later generalized to the PCM on
SU(N)

[
55
]
and with arbitrary WZ term (PCM
k
)
[
41
]
.
19
19

19

See also
[
56
]
, where the exact ground state energy was computed in the large-
N
limit.

The Zamolodchikov-Zamolodchikov ‘rational’ S-matrices (with rational dependence on rapidities) are known to admit ‘trigonometric’ deformations
[
144
,
136
]
. The 2d “sausage model” was then found
[
101
]
as a semi-classical
\sigma
-model description of the trigonometric
O(3)
S-matrix, and this model was later understood
[
145
]
to be the
SU(2)\over U(1)
example of the
\eta
-model. More generally, similar trigonometric S-matrices are understood to be associated to inhomogeneous Yang-Baxter deformations and the associated
q
-deformed symmetries
[
57
]
(see also recent papers
[
58
]
on the light-cone S-matrix for
\eta
-deformed string backgrounds and refs. therein).

1.4.13
Factorized scattering vs. tree-level sigma model S-matrix

Given that integrable S-matrices are so constrained, one might hope to classify the integrable
\sigma
-models by (i) computing the S-matrix for a general
\sigma
-model in terms of the couplings
({\rm G},{\rm B})
(ii) imposing factorization and no particle production and (iii) systematically reading off the resulting constraints on
({\rm G},{\rm B})
from integrability.
Like the RG flow (which is the focus of the thesis), the S-matrix could thus serve as another ‘diagnostic tool’ for integrability.

Here we shall summarize the paper
[
1
]
where we considered such a procedure for the leading
tree-level
S-matrix,
whose factorization should correspond to
classical
integrability. The factorization of the loop-corrected S-matrix should then correspond to preservation of integrability at the quantum level. For example, for the complex sine-Gordon model, we recall that the tree-level 2-body S-matrix satisfies the Yang-Baxter equation but the 1-loop-corrected S-matrix only satisfies it if the theory is supplemented by certain quantum corrections
[
126
]
. These corrections may be traced to the leading quantum corrections to a gauged WZW model (see eq. (
2.4
) below) — of which the complex sine-Gordon model is a massive integrable deformation. The corrections are thus expected to correspond to a particular choice of finite counterterms consistent with quantum integrability, similar to those discussed below in the context of the RG flow.

To compute an S-matrix in perturbation theory, one must first decide on a choice of vacuum. In
[
1
]
we considered the ‘trivial’ vacuum
x^{m}=0
(with all fields vanishing) in an expansion of the metric and
{\rm B}
-field around flat space,
{\rm G}_{mn}+{\rm B}_{mn}=\delta_{mn}+{\rm h}_{mn,p}\,x^{p}+{\rm h}_{mn,pq}\,x^{p}\,x^{q}+\ldots
.
This is a natural choice since it is universal to any target space geometry. However, the fields
x^{m}
expanded around this vacuum are massless 2d scalars, which are known to suffer from IR divergences at the quantum level
[
66
]
. This is related to the fact that massless particles (moving at the speed of light) on a 1d spatial line do not separate asymptotically, so an S-matrix cannot have the usual physical interpretation. Still, massless 2d S-matrices were formally discussed for integrable theories in the context of a finite-density
TBA
[
67
]
. The S-matrix there retains the interpretation as the relative phase when one particle is moved past
another.

One might still hope to define a formal
tree-level
S-matrix for such massless particles (e.g., from the classical action evaluated on a solution with special scattering boundary conditions), but we found in
[
1
]
that such an object still has IR singularities. The singularities are not true divergences but instead ambiguous “0/0” contributions due to a vertex vanishing and an internal propagator blowing up at the same time when the scattering amplitude is taken on-shell. We classified the ambiguities into two types and considered some simple approaches to regularize them (the usual
i\varepsilon
prescription, or an alternative “massive regularization” where the particles are given a small mass). One can compute finite scattering amplitudes by regularizing the ambiguities, taking the amplitude on-shell, and finally taking the regulator to zero. However, we found that the resulting amplitudes do not have the usual connection to integrability: in various well-known integrable examples the amplitudes exhibit particle production.
