These findings were in agreement with earlier attempts to compute scattering amplitudes for massless 2d scalars in integrable theories
[
68
]
.

Opposite conclusions have been found for massless 2d S-matrices in non-renormalizable (non scale-invariant)
Nambu-like models
[
73
]
. For Nambu theory with a flat target space (but not for a curved one), each scalar appears
in the action only through its derivative and thus ambiguities related to IR poles appear to be
absent, not only at the tree level but also at the loop level. Here the (naively expected) relation
between factorization of the massless S-matrix and integrability does appear to hold (with the
Nambu action being integrable beyond tree level only in special critical dimensions).
Moreover, the flat-space Nambu theory is an example of a
T\bar{T}
-deformation
[
79
]
of a free theory, for which integrability apparently constrains the RG flow even in non-renormalizable cases, or rather dictates the fixing of the counterterms to be consistent with the integrable S-matrix
[
78
]
.

A similar S-matrix approach has been used
[
69
]
to rule out integrability of strings on certain (warped) AdS supergravity backgrounds by expanding around non-trivial classical solutions (BMN geodesic
[
70
]
, GKP spinning string
[
71
]
or ‘null cusp’
[
72
]
solutions) and fixing a light-cone gauge. These vacua give a mass to at least some fields and one may then consider particular worlsheet scattering amplitudes that do not contain any ambiguous contributions from massless fields. While this approach is not easily applied to generic backgrounds (where one does not have a good understanding of classical solutions), it would be interesting in future to consider a more general class of backgrounds than AdS ones, but still assuming enough isometries to allow a similar choice of vacuum.

1.4.14
Affine Gaudin models and local charges

There is a proposal to systematize integrable 2d theories by viewing them as realizations of affine Gaudin models
[
255
,
59
]
(see also
[
60
]
). It so happens that all of the integrable
\sigma
-models described above are examples of such theories. This is indeed suggestive they may describe a large portion of the integrable theories.

The affine Gaudin-type integrable models have flat Lax connections (
1.7
). They also have a systematic construction of local, higher-spin, classically conserved charges, generalizing those constructed in (
1.40
) for the PCM/symmetric space and models with Lax connections of those same form. The classical Poisson brackets of the spatial component of the Lax connection
\mathcal{L}\equiv L_{\sigma}=\tfrac{1}{2}(L_{+}-L_{-})\in{\rm Lie}(G)
take the Maillet
r/s
-form
[
228
]
(here
z_{1},z_{2}
are spectral parameters and
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
are spatial coordinates),

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \big\{\mathcal{L}_{\underline{1}}(z_{1};\sigma_{1}),\mathcal{L}_{\underline{2}}(z_{2};\sigma_{2})\big\}={}&{}\Big([r_{\underline{12}}(z_{1},z_{2}),\mathcal{L}_{\underline{1}}(z_{1};\sigma_{1})+\mathcal{L}_{\underline{2}}(z_{2};\sigma_{1})]\\
&+[s_{\underline{12}}(z_{1},z_{2}),\mathcal{L}_{\underline{1}}(z_{1};\sigma_{1})-\mathcal{L}_{\underline{2}}(z_{2};\sigma_{1})]\Big)\delta_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}-2s_{\underline{12}}(z_{1},z_{2})\,\delta_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}^{\prime}\ .\end{aligned}

(1.88)

Here the underlined
\underline{1},\underline{2}
indices in (
1.88
) denote the first and second factors in a tensor product
{\rm Lie}(G)\otimes{\rm Lie}(G)
, e.g.
\mathcal{L}_{\underline{1}}\equiv\mathcal{L}\otimes 1
,
\mathcal{L}_{\underline{2}}\equiv 1\otimes\mathcal{L}
. The Poisson bracket (
1.88
) is called ‘non-ultralocal’ because of the presence of the
\delta^{\prime}
term. The constant
r
- and
s
-matrices
r_{\underline{12}}(z_{1},z_{2})=-r_{\underline{21}}(z_{2},z_{1})
and
s_{\underline{12}}(z_{1},z_{2})=s_{\underline{21}}(z_{2},z_{1})
are respectively antisymmetric and symmetric, and they take the special ‘‘twist’’ form,
20
20

20

The form of (
1.89
) is slightly modified is so-called ‘cyclotomic’ examples involving twisting by a
Z_{T}
automorphism, e.g. the symmetric space
\sigma
-model with
T=2
; here we ignore such cases.

\displaystyle r_{\underline{12}}(z_{1},z_{2})+s_{\underline{12}}(z_{1},z_{2})=\frac{1}{(z_{2}-z_{1})\phi(z_{2})}\,T_{A}\otimes T^{A}\ ,

(1.89)

in terms of a model-dependent meromorphic
twist function

\phi(z)
of the spectral parameter. Here the ‘split Casimir’
T_{A}\otimes T^{A}
, built from any set of generators
T_{A}
for
G
and the dual generators
T^{A}
(with respect to
{\kappa}_{AB}\equiv{\rm Tr}[T_{A}T_{B}]
), is invariant under change of basis.

At each zero
z_{i}
of the twist function
\phi(z)
(or more properly zeros of the 1-form
\phi(z)dz
)
21
21

21

In particular the poles of
\mathcal{L}
will be zeros of the twist function.
, one can automatically construct from (
1.88
) an infinite tower of local, higher spin charges. Assuming for simplicity the Lax connection to be valued in a
B
,
C
or
D
type algebra (the
A
type case is slightly more complicated), the following charges
\{Q^{(2n)}(z_{i})\}_{n,i}
are all in involution
[
255
]
(assuming a well-defined limit of
\phi(z)\mathcal{L}(z)
at
z_{i}
),

\displaystyle\big\{Q^{(2n)}(z_{i}),Q^{(2m)}(z_{j})\big\}=0\ ,\qquad Q^{(2n)}(z_{i})=\int d\sigma\,{\rm Tr}\big[\big(\phi(z)\mathcal{L}(z)\big)^{2n}\big]\Big|_{z=z_{i}}\ .

(1.90)

For realizations of affine Gaudin models, the Hamiltonian is a linear combination of some of these charges, so all of the charges are conserved (commuting with the Hamiltonian).

For the group-based models above (PCM, PCM
k
, group
\eta
-model, group
\lambda
-model) with Lax connections of the form (
1.38
) in terms of a flat, conserved current
\mathcal{A}_{\pm}
, let us re-define the spectral parameter
z=\tfrac{1+w}{1-w}
for simplicity so that the Lax matrix
\mathcal{L}=L_{\sigma}
has simple poles at finite values
w=\pm 1
. These models’ twist functions all have simple zeros at these points
\pm 1
, and take the form
\phi(w)=\tfrac{(1-w)(1+w)}{p(w)}
where
p
is a polynomial not vanishing at
\pm 1
. The resulting charges at
w_{1}=1
and
w_{2}=-1
are proportional to the local charges obtained from the (anti-)holomorphic currents in (
1.40
) above,
22
22

22

We seem to have produced only half as many charges in (
1.91
) as in (
1.40
), but in fact all of the odd currents
J^{(2n+1)}
vanish for
B
,
C
and
D
type groups
[
253
]
. Formally this procedure applies to the complexifed Lie group and one specifies a real form by some reality conditions. This does not affect the charges in this case since the zeros
w=\pm 1
of the twist function are real and so the charges (
1.91
) are real.

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}Q^{(2n)}(\pm 1)=0\ ,\qquad Q^{(2n)}(\pm 1)\sim\int d\sigma\,\mathcal{J}^{(2n)}_{\pm}\ ,\qquad\mathcal{J}^{(2n)}_{\pm}={\rm Tr}[\mathcal{A}_{\pm}^{2n}]\ .

(1.91)

Here we have used the trace but, as in (
1.40
)(
1.41
) above, we could have instead used any symmetric, invariant tensor in (
1.90
).

Thus one learns from (
1.90
) that the higher-spin charges (
1.91
) are all in involution.
It also follows from the Poisson bracket (
1.88
) that these charges are in involution with the monodromy matrix and its non-local conserved charges
[
255
]
as constructed in Section
1.4.1
. A similar construction of local, conserved charges applies to models based on symmetric spaces (or other
Z_{T}
-cosets).

As well as re-interpreting known models, the affine Gaudin approach has the power to generate new integrable theories, essentially by picking new twist functions
\phi(z)
. For example, the
\eta
-deformations of symmetric space
[
137
]
and semi-symmetric space
[
176
]

\sigma
-models were discovered in this way (based on the previously known group space case); more recent examples include the integrable
G^{N}
models
[
217
]
, deformed
G^{N}
models
[
65
]
and
G\times G/H
models
[
218
]
(see Chapter
3
).

The affine Gaudin construction systematizes certain reformulations of these integrable
\sigma
-models. For a certain class of twist functions, the affine-Gaudin-type models are equivalent
[
61
]
to “E-models”
[
177
]
(a first order formulation with manifest Poisson-Lie symmetry), and can also be obtained
[
62
,
265
]
from 4d Chern-Simons theory
[
264
]
.

Finally we remark that the affine Gaudin formalism could lead to a more systematic understanding of the RG flow of integrable
\sigma
-models, or perhaps even a first-principles proof of their renormalizability. Along these lines, a recent paper
[
220
]
noted a pattern in the 1-loop RG flow for several integrable affine-Gaudin-type
\sigma
-models: in each case it may be written in the same universal way as a flow of the twist function. It would be interesting to try to derive this statement, or to check it beyond the leading 1-loop order. One complication is that, beyond the 1-loop order, we should expect the integrable theories to require a quantum deformation to remain RG-stable (see below); it is not yet clear how these quantum deformations would be encoded in the affine Gaudin language.

1.4.15
On scheme dependence

Under covariant scheme transformations, or redefinitions of the
\sigma
-model couplings
({\rm G},{\rm B})
(in terms of constants
c,d,e
),

\displaystyle{\rm G}_{mn}\to{\rm G}_{mn}+\alpha^{\prime}(c\,R_{mn}+d\,{\rm H}_{mpq}{\rm H}_{n}{}^{pq})+\ldots\ ,\quad{\rm B}_{mn}\to{\rm B}_{mn}+\alpha^{\prime}\,e\,\nabla^{p}{\rm H}_{pmn}+\ldots\ ,

(1.92)

the
\beta
-function
\beta_{mn}=\frac{d}{dt}({\rm G}+{\rm B})_{mn}
transforms as a contravariant vector in the space of couplings. Simple power counting implies that the leading 1-loop
\beta
-function is invariant while, in general, scheme dependence will begin at the 2-loop order.
23
23

23

For the special case of vanishing
{\rm B}=0
, the
\sigma
-model may be viewed as a “single-coupling” theory and its 2-loop
\beta
-function (
1.8
) is also invariant (see Appendix
B
).

In this thesis, we will generally use the
2-loop
\beta
-functions given in (
1.9
),
which were computed in a special 2-loop scheme
that effectively treats

{\rm G}_{mn}
and
{\rm B}_{mn}
as symmetrically as possible
[
197
,
163
]
(see also
[
170
,
221
]
).
Indeed, the

\beta
-functions for
{\rm G}
and
{\rm B}
in (
1.9
) are given by the symmetric and antisymmetric parts of a single tensor expression.
We shall refer to this
{\rm G}
-
{\rm B}
symmetric scheme as the
“
{\rm G}{\rm B}
scheme” (see the introduction to Chapter
3
for discussion of the properties of this scheme).

It is important to emphasize, however, that our conclusions will be scheme-independent. In particular, the statement that ‘a model is not renormalizable’ is understood to mean that it is not renormalizable in any covariant subtraction scheme. We shall find certain finite counterterms that restore higher-loop renormalizability and, while the form of these counterterms will change in different subtraction schemes, they cannot be eliminated by covariant scheme changes such as (
1.92
).

Chapter 2
Quantum corrections to geometry from RG flow

As discussed in the Introduction, it was observed for some years that integrable
\sigma
-models appear to be stable under the leading 1-loop RG flow, and our purpose here is to investigate what happens at higher-loop orders. The general finding is that the integrable theories remain stable under the higher-loop RG flow provided they are supplemented with particular finite counterterms, i.e. the classical target space geometry
({\rm G}+{\rm B})_{mn}^{(0)}
receives certain quantum corrections,

\displaystyle({\rm G}+{\rm B})_{mn}=({\rm G}+{\rm B})_{mn}^{(0)}+\alpha^{\prime}({\rm G}+{\rm B})_{mn}^{(1)}+\ldots\ .

(2.1)

One might first try to study the integrability–RG flow relationship for a canonical integrable example such as the PCM,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{{\rm PCM}}=-\tfrac{1}{2}{\rm h}{\rm Tr}[J_{+}J_{-}]\ .

(2.2)

However, the question trivializes for this simple example, because the renormalizability of the PCM follows immediately from its large
G\times G
global symmetry (and parity invariance)

\displaystyle g\to g_{L}\,g\,g_{R}\ ,\qquad(g_{L},g_{R})\in G_{L}\times G_{R}\ .

(2.3)

Only the curvature radius
\sqrt{h}
can run because the theory (
2.2
) does not admit any deformations preserving these symmetries.
This conclusion is consistent with the conjecture that integrable models are renormalizable, but it is not good evidence for it: we only used manifest global symmetry, without relying on the hidden integrable symmetries.

In order to really probe the role of integrability in constraining the RG flow, we shall consider integrable theories with less global symmetry. As we shall observe, integrability appears to be sufficient for renormalizability, even in cases without any manifest global symmetries.
In this chapter we shall focus on the integrable
\eta
- and
\lambda
-deformations introduced in Sections
1.4.9
and
1.4.10
, which
are classically integrable but have reduced global symmetry groups. In particular, these models are not fully constrained by their global symmetries, and renormalizability does not follow from global symmetries alone.

First in Section
2.2
, we shall consider the simplest
\eta
-deformed model
based on the coset
SU(2)\over U(1)
. Then in secton
2.3
, we shall consider
\lambda
-deformed models
based on the coset
SU(2)\over U(1)
and the group
SU(2)
. As we reviewed above, these models are already known to be stable under the leading 1-loop RG flow; we shall determine the quantum corrections
required to solve the 2- and higher-loop RG equation (
1.9
).

This chapter is based on the papers
[
2
,
3
]
and closely follows the presentation there.

2.1
On quantum corrections in sigma models

Before proceeding, let us provide justification for the presence of quantum corrections (
2.1
) in
\sigma
-models
by recalling some familiar examples where such corrections are known to occur.

One class of examples are
the special integrable
\sigma
-models (
1.66
) corresponding to gauged WZW conformal field theories.
The classical
\sigma
-models (
1.66
) are indeed scale-invariant, i.e. fixed-point solutions of the RG equation (
1.9
), at the leading 1-loop order.
However, to remain conformally invariant beyond 1-loop, the target space metric and
{\rm B}
-field
require non-trivial
\alpha^{\prime}
corrections at each order,
which may be derived exactly
the underlying quantum gWZW theory
(for discussion and examples see
[
119
,
120
,
121
]
).
The simplest example is provided by the exact counterpart
[
113
]
of the
familiar classical
SU(1,1)/U(1)
gWZW metric (or 2d Euclidean black hole)
[
122
,
123
]

\displaystyle ds^{2}=k(dr^{2}+\tanh^{2}r\,dy^{2})\ \ \to\ \ ds^{2}=(k-2)\Big[dr^{2}+\big(\coth^{2}r-\tfrac{2}{k}\big)^{-1}dy^{2}\Big]\penalty\ .

(2.4)

Here the inverse level
k^{-1}
(and later the inverse coupling
h^{-1}
) plays the role of the loop-counting parameter
\alpha^{\prime}
in (
1.6
)

As was checked directly in
[
124
]
, the leading
k^{-1}
correction in (
2.4
)
is required to solve the 2-loop scale invariance equation following from (
1.8
)
(see also
[
125
]
for a 4-loop test of (
2.4
)).
24
24

24

In this Weyl-invariant case
the diffeomorphism vector
\xi_{n}
can be written as
2\partial_{n}\Phi
where the exact dilaton field
\Phi
is given by

e^{-2\Phi}=\sinh 2r\ \big(\coth^{2}r-\tfrac{2}{k}\big)^{1/2}
.

The integrable cases that follow will be a direct generalization of this principle: instead of ensuring complete
scale-invariance, the quantum corrections will ensure invariance under the RG flow with just a few couplings running.

It is also well-known that the classical Buscher rules for abelian T-duality are subject to quantum corrections
[
109
,
110
,
203
,
204
]
, so geometries resulting from T-duality will contain non-trivial
\alpha^{\prime}
corrections. For example,
given the metric

ds^{2}={\rm h}[dx^{i}dx^{i}+\mathcal{M}(x)dy^{2}]
with an isometry direction
y
, the standard T-duality rule
\mathcal{M}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}=\mathcal{M}^{-1}
is known to be
modified at the 2-loop level
[
109
]
(see also
[
111
,
112
]
)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}=\mathcal{M}^{-1}\big(1+\tfrac{1}{2}{\rm h}^{-1}\,\partial_{i}\log\mathcal{M}\,\partial^{i}\log\mathcal{M}\big)\ .

(2.5)

In an example below in Section
2.3
, this quantum correction to the T-duality transformation will be reproduced by special limits of the corrections for the
\lambda
-model. We shall also produce similar corrections to the transformation rules of non-abelian duality in some special examples.

2.2
\eta
-model

The metric of the 2d ‘‘sausage’’ model
25
25

25

We note that
{\rm h}
in this chapter is equivalent to
4h
in
[
2
]
.

\displaystyle ds^{2}=\tfrac{1}{4}{\rm h}\Big[\frac{dr^{2}}{(1-r^{2})(1+\varkappa^{2}r^{2})}+\frac{1-r^{2}}{1+\varkappa^{2}r^{2}}d\phi^{2}\Big]\ ,

(2.6)

was originally written down in
[
101
]
as the leading semi-classical approximation to the
\sigma
-model corresponding to the massive integrable trigonometric S-matrix of
[
144
]
. It was discovered as a solution to the 1-loop RG equation (the leading Ricci flow term in (
1.8
)) and conjectured to be classically integrable. Its classical integrability was later shown in
[
103
]
. In
[
145
]
this model was identified as the
SU(2)\over U(1)
example of the
\eta
-deformation
[
131
,
137
]
, introduced in Section
1.4.9
.
26
26

26

It is interesting to note that the metric (
2.6
) is formally self T-dual, i.e. invariant under

\phi\to\tilde{\phi}
and
\tilde{\phi}\to i\varkappa^{-1}\phi
,
r\to i\varkappa^{-1}r^{-1}
.

The metric (
2.6
) is an integrable deformation of the round
S^{2}
(
\varkappa=0
) of radius
\sqrt{{\rm h}/4}
, with

\varkappa
being the deformation parameter.
The usual
\eta
-deformation correspnds to the case when
\varkappa
is real, however the authors of
[
101
]
considered another regime of interest with
\varkappa
imaginary, in which case the
\sigma
-model is UV stable with a free UV fixed point at
\varkappa^{2}=-1
.
Alternatively, we can send
\varkappa^{2}\to-1
while simultaneously expanding around
r^{2}=1
.
Taking the limit in this way, the metric (
2.6
) reduces to the classical metric of the
\frac{SU(1,1)}{U(1)}
gWZW model (cf (
2.4
)),

\displaystyle r^{2}=1-(1+\varkappa^{2})\sinh^{2}x\ ,\ \ \qquad\varkappa^{2}\to-1\ ,\qquad ds^{2}\to\tfrac{1}{4}{\rm h}(dx^{2}+\tanh^{2}x\,d\phi^{2})\ ,

(2.7)

with
{\rm h}/4
becoming the level.
Another useful limit is the maximal deformation limit
\varkappa\to\infty
,
{\rm h}\to\infty
with
{\rm h}^{\prime}\equiv\tfrac{{\rm h}}{\varkappa^{2}}
fixed, which yields

[
145
]
the undeformed hyperbolic space
H^{2}
with radius
\sqrt{{\rm h}^{\prime}/4}
(here we set
r=\tfrac{1}{\cosh{\theta}}
)

\displaystyle ds^{2}=\frac{{\rm h}^{\prime}}{4r^{2}}\Big[\frac{dr^{2}}{1-r^{2}}+(1-r^{2})d\phi^{2}\Big]=\tfrac{1}{4}{\rm h}^{\prime}\Big[d\theta^{2}+\sinh^{2}\theta\,d\phi^{2}\Big]\ .

(2.8)

The metric (
2.6
) solves the 1-loop RG equation (
1.8
) with
{\rm h}
and
\varkappa
running as (eq. (
1.76
) with
c_{{}_{SU(2)}}=2
)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}{\rm h}=4(1+\varkappa^{2})+{\cal O}({\rm h}^{-1})\ ,\qquad\qquad\frac{d}{dt}\varkappa=4{\rm h}^{-1}\varkappa(1+\varkappa^{2})+{\cal O}({\rm h}^{-2})\ .

(2.9)

There is an RG-invariant combination of couplings

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\nu=0\ ,\qquad\qquad\nu\equiv{{\rm h}\over 4\varkappa}+{\mathcal{O}}({\rm h}^{0})\ .

(2.10)

In agreement with the above comments,

\varkappa^{2}=-1
is a UV fixed point (corresponding to either a free theory or a gWZW model depending how the limit is taken).

However, including also the 2-loop term in the RG equations (
1.8
), the
\sigma
-model on the classical sausage geometry (
2.6
) appears not to be renormalizable: it does not solve the 2-loop RG equations with only
{\rm h}
and
\varkappa
running. This is true in any covariant subtraction scheme
27
27

27

As discussed in Appendix
B
, for 2d target spaces, the scheme dependence does not begin until 3-loops.
and with any diffeomorphism vector (here one only needs to consider diffeomorphism vectors of the form
\xi^{m}=\xi^{m}(r)
since
\phi
is an isometry direction).

The relation to the gWZW model suggests that,
for (
2.6
) to remain a solution to the RG equation at higher loops with only
{\rm h}
and
\varkappa
running,
it should similarly be modified by quantum (i.e.
1/{\rm h}
) corrections.
Inspired by the analogy with the form of the gWZW metric in (
2.4
) we propose
the following conjecture for the exact generalization of (
2.6
)

\displaystyle ds^{2}=(\tfrac{1}{4}{\rm h}-1+\varkappa^{2})\Big[\frac{dr^{2}}{(1-r^{2})(1+\varkappa^{2}r^{2})}+\big(\frac{1+\varkappa^{2}r^{2}}{1-r^{2}}+\frac{8\varkappa^{2}}{{\rm h}}\big)^{-1}d\phi^{2}\Big]\ .

(2.11)

The metric (
2.11
) is expected to solve the RG equations to all loop orders
(in a particular scheme and modulo coordinate redefinitions) with
{\rm h}
and
\varkappa
running
according to a generalization of (
2.9
).
The conjecture passes some obvious tests:

(i)

The metric (
2.11
) reduces to
S^{2}
when setting
\varkappa=0
, now with
shifted radius-squared coupling

{\tfrac{1}{4}{\rm h}-1}
, which is simply related to the classical one
\tfrac{1}{4}{{\rm h}}
by a finite coupling redefinition.
Alternatively, one may shift
{\rm h}\to{\rm h}+4(1-\varkappa^{2})
in (
2.11
) to obtain

ds^{2}=\tfrac{1}{4}{\rm h}\Big[\frac{dr^{2}}{(1-r^{2})(1+\varkappa^{2}r^{2})}+\big(\frac{1+\varkappa^{2}r^{2}}{1-r^{2}}+\frac{2\varkappa^{2}}{\tfrac{1}{4}{\rm h}+1-\varkappa^{2}}\big)^{-1}d\phi^{2}\Big],

so that the radius-squared of
S^{2}
remains
\tfrac{1}{4}{\rm h}
in the
\varkappa=0
limit.

(ii)

The metric remains flat for
\varkappa^{2}=-1
.

(iii)

In the non-trivial
\varkappa^{2}\to-1
limit (
2.7
) it reduces to the exact gWZW metric in (
2.4
) with level
k={\rm h}/4
.

(iv)

In the maximal deformation limit (
2.8
) it reduces to
H^{2}
, now with shifted radius-squared coupling
{\rm h}^{\prime}/4+1
.

More importantly,
one can directly check that (
2.11
) with the leading
h^{-1}
correction
included,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} ds^{2}&={\rm h}\Big[\frac{dr^{2}}{(1-r^{2})(1+\varkappa^{2}r^{2})}\Big(\frac{1}{4}-{{1-\varkappa^{2}}\over{{\rm h}}}\Big)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+\frac{1-r^{2}}{1+\varkappa^{2}r^{2}}\Big(\frac{1}{4}-{{1-\varkappa^{2}}\over{{\rm h}}}-{2\varkappa^{2}\over{{\rm h}}}\frac{1-r^{2}}{1+\varkappa^{2}r^{2}}+\ldots\Big)d\phi^{2}\Big]\ ,\end{aligned}

(2.12)

indeed solves the 2-loop RG equation
(
1.8
), which becomes in this case with 2d target space,
28
28

28

In 2 dimensions the curvature tensor can be written as

R_{mnkl}=\tfrac{1}{2}R({\rm G}_{mk}{\rm G}_{nl}-{\rm G}_{nk}{\rm G}_{ml})
. Note that any
{\rm B}
-field on a 2d space is trivial (with zero field strength
{\rm H}=d{\rm B}
) so we use the
\beta
-function (
1.8
) for the case of vanishing
{\rm B}
-field (also taking
\lambda_{m}=0
).

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}{\rm G}_{mn}=(\tfrac{1}{2}R+\tfrac{1}{4}R^{2}){\rm G}_{mn}+\ldots+\nabla_{(m}\xi_{n)}\ ,

(2.13)

for a particular diffeomorphism vector
\xi_{n}
with components,
29
29

29

We note that the diffeomorphism vector (
2.14
) begins at 2-loop order; the 1-loop diffeomorphism vanishes in these coordinates.

\displaystyle\xi_{\phi}=0\ ,\qquad\quad\xi_{r}=\frac{8\varkappa^{2}(1+\varkappa^{2})r}{{\rm h}(1+\varkappa^{2}r^{2})^{2}}\ .

(2.14)

The
\beta
-functions (
2.9
) receive the following 2-loop corrections

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}{\rm h}=(1+\varkappa^{2})

\displaystyle\big[4+16\,{\rm h}^{-1}(1-\varkappa^{2})+{\cal O}({\rm h}^{-2})\big]\ ,

(2.15)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\varkappa={\rm h}^{-1}\varkappa(1+\varkappa^{2})

\displaystyle\big[4+16\,{\rm h}^{-1}(1-\varkappa^{2})+{\cal O}({\rm h}^{-2})\big]\ .

(2.16)

The RG-invariant quantity in (
2.10
) remains RG-invariant without 2-loop corrections,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\nu=0\ ,\qquad\qquad\nu\equiv{{\rm h}\over 4\varkappa}+0+\mathcal{O}({\rm h}^{-1})\ .

(2.17)

Furthermore, the ansatz (
2.11
) also solves the 3-loop RG equation (
1.8
) with

\displaystyle\beta_{mn}=\Big[\tfrac{1}{2}R+\tfrac{1}{4}R^{2}+c_{1}R^{3}+c_{2}(\nabla R)^{2}+c_{3}R\nabla^{2}R\Big]{\rm G}_{mn}+c_{4}\nabla_{m}R\nabla_{n}R\ ,

(2.18)

in a particular “natural” renormalization scheme corresponding to

\displaystyle\qquad c_{1}=0\ ,\qquad c_{2}=\tfrac{1}{8}\ ,\qquad c_{3}=-\tfrac{1}{4}\ ,\qquad c_{4}=-\tfrac{1}{16}\ ,

(2.19)

in which the 3-loop
\beta
-functions for
{\rm h}
and
\varkappa
take the form

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}{{\rm h}}=(1+\varkappa^{2})

\displaystyle\Big[4+16\,{\rm h}^{-1}(1-\varkappa^{2})+64\,{\rm h}^{-2}(1-\varkappa^{2})^{2}+{\mathcal{O}}({\rm h}^{-3})\Big]\ ,

(2.20)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}{\varkappa}={\rm h}^{-1}\varkappa(1+\varkappa^{2})

\displaystyle\Big[4+16\,{\rm h}^{-1}(1-\varkappa^{2})+64\,{\rm h}^{-2}(1-\varkappa^{2})^{2}+{\mathcal{O}}({\rm h}^{-3})\Big]\ .

(2.21)

The diffeomorphism vector in (
2.14
) receives the following 3-loop correction

\displaystyle\xi_{\phi}=0\ ,\qquad\quad\xi_{r}=\frac{8\varkappa^{2}(1+\varkappa^{2})r}{{\rm h}(1+\varkappa^{2}r^{2})^{2}}\Big[1+\frac{8\varkappa^{2}(r^{2}-1)}{{\rm h}(1+\varkappa^{2}r^{2})}\Big]\ .

(2.22)

This scheme (
2.19
) is related to the minimal subtraction scheme in
[
146
,
147
,
148
]
,

\displaystyle\qquad c_{1}=\tfrac{5}{32}\ ,\qquad c_{2}=\tfrac{1}{16}\ ,\qquad c_{3}=0\ ,\qquad c_{4}=-\tfrac{1}{16}\ ,

(2.23)

by the covariant coupling redefinition,

\displaystyle{\rm G}_{mn}^{(\rm nat)}=\big[{\rm G}_{mn}+\tfrac{5}{8}\,\alpha^{\prime 2}({R}^{2})_{mn}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\alpha^{\prime 2}\nabla^{2}R_{mn}\big]^{(\rm min)}\ ,

(2.24)

where
{\rm G}_{mn}^{(\rm nat)}\equiv{\rm G}_{mn}
is the metric (
2.11
) in the “natural” scheme and
{\rm G}_{mn}^{(\rm min)}
is the corresponding metric in the minimal scheme (see Appendix
B
).

We call the scheme (
2.19
) “natural” because the RG-invariant quantity in (
2.17
) remains invariant with no 3-loop corrections:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\nu=0\ ,\qquad\qquad\nu\equiv{{\rm h}\over 4\varkappa}+0+0+\mathcal{O}({\rm h}^{-2})\ .

(2.25)

This prompts us to conjecture that, in a natural choice of
subtraction scheme at each loop order, the quantity
\nu\equiv\tfrac{{\rm h}}{\varkappa}
is an exact RG-invariant.
This suggests that
\nu
should be the parameter that appears in the exact quantum trigonometric S-matrix
that generalizes the non-perturbative massive S-matrix of the
O(3)
-invariant
S^{2}

\sigma
-model
[
51
]
.

Relatedly, we extrapolate from the obvious pattern in (
2.20
),(
2.21
) to conjecture that, in the same natural scheme, the all-loop
\beta
-functions of
h
and
\varkappa
are

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}{{\rm h}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1+\varkappa^{2}}{\tfrac{1}{4}{\rm h}-(1-\varkappa^{2})}\,{\rm h}\ ,

(2.26)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}{\varkappa}

\displaystyle=\frac{1+\varkappa^{2}}{\tfrac{1}{4}{\rm h}-(1-\varkappa^{2})}\,\varkappa\ .

(2.27)

Here
\varkappa^{2}=-1

remains a fixed point, as it should to all orders since it corresponds both to flat space and the gWZW limit.
For
\varkappa=0
we get
\frac{d}{dt}{R^{2}_{S^{2}}}=1+R^{-2}_{S^{2}}
for the
S^{2}
radius
R_{S^{2}}=\sqrt{\tfrac{1}{4}{\rm h}-1}
, which agrees with the 1-loop and 2-loop coefficients in the
\beta
-function of the

S^{2}
model. In the maximal deformation limit (
2.8
) we get
\frac{d}{dt}{R^{2}_{H^{2}}}=-1+R^{-2}_{H^{2}}
for the
H^{2}
radius
R_{H^{2}}=\sqrt{\tfrac{1}{4}{\rm h}^{\prime}+1}=\sqrt{\tfrac{1}{4}{\rm h}\varkappa^{-2}+1}
. The fact that this is correctly related to the
S^{2}

\beta
-function by the analytic continuation
R^{2}_{H^{2}}=-R^{2}_{S^{2}}
is a
check of the consistency of the conjectured exact metric (
2.11
) and exact
\beta
-functions (
2.26
),(
2.27
). Moreover,
if there is a natural scheme where (
2.26
),(
2.27
) are exact,
then the
S^{2}
and
H^{2}

\beta
-functions are 2-loop exact in this scheme
(this may be possible in a special non-minimal scheme since the 3- and higher-loop
\beta
-function coefficients are scheme-dependent).

Let us mention that
in the case of the
(1,1) supersymmetric generalization of the
\sigma
-model (
1.6
), the first potential correction to the 1-loop
\beta
-function
appears
at 4 loops (
\sim\zeta(3)R^{4}
in the minimal scheme
[
149
]
, with this particular invariant actually vanishing in the case of 2d target space).
There is also
no deformation of the super-gWZW metric (to all orders) and it is thus natural to expect that the same may apply to the
supersymmetric
\sigma
-model on the sausage geometry (
2.6
) (i.e. only
{\rm h}
and
\varkappa
running without adding corrections to the metric).

One may also repeat the above discussion for the

\eta
-deformation of hyperbolic space
H^{2}=\frac{SU(1,1)}{U(1)}

(or the Lorentzian signature symmetric spaces
dS_{2}
and
AdS_{2}
).
The corresponding metric is related to (
2.6
) by the
formal analytic continuation,

\displaystyle r\to ir\ ,\qquad\ \ \phi\to i\phi\ ,\ \qquad\ \varkappa\to i\varkappa\ ,\qquad\ \ {\rm h}\to-{\rm h}\ .

(2.28)

i.e. we find
30
30

30

Let us note that the metric (
2.29
) is self T-dual, i.e. invariant under

\phi\to\tilde{\phi}
and
\tilde{\phi}\to\varkappa^{-1}\phi
,
r\to\varkappa^{-1}r^{-1}
.

\displaystyle ds^{2}=\tfrac{1}{4}{\rm h}\Big[\frac{dr^{2}}{(1+r^{2})(1+\varkappa^{2}r^{2})}+\frac{1+r^{2}}{1+\varkappa^{2}r^{2}}d\phi^{2}\Big]\ .

(2.29)

This inhomogeneous Yang-Baxter deformation of
H^{2}
is based on the
split

R
-matrix of
{\rm Lie}(SU(1,1))
(see footnote
15
).
On the other hand, if we had not continued
\varkappa\to i\varkappa
in (
2.28
), we would have found Yang-Baxter deformation based on the non-split
R
-matrix.
For
S^{2}
only the latter case exists as a Yang-Baxter deformation in a strict sense since there is no split
R
-matrix of
{\rm Lie}(SU(2))
(see, e.g.,
[
150
]
).

For

\varkappa=0
(
2.29
) becomes the
H^{2}
metric, while for
\varkappa^{2}=1
it is flat.
The limit analogous to (
2.7
), i.e.

\varkappa\to 1
with
r^{2}\to-1+(1-\varkappa^{2})\sinh^{2}t
, is now a formal limit
and gives the metric

ds^{2}=\tfrac{1}{4}{\rm h}(-dt^{2}+\tanh^{2}t\,d\phi^{2})
, which is the classical metric of the

\frac{SU(1,1)}{\mathbb{R}}
gWZW model.

In
conformally-flat coordinates the metric (
2.29
) may be written as

\displaystyle ds^{2}={{\rm h}\over 4(p^{2}+\varkappa^{2}q^{2})}(dp^{2}+dq^{2})\ ,\qquad\qquad r=\frac{q}{p}\penalty\ ,\quad\phi=\tfrac{1}{2}\log({p^{2}+q^{2}})\ ,

(2.30)

where the scaling symmetry
(p,q)\to\lambda(p,q)
is the counterpart of

\phi
-shift isometry of (
2.29
).

The analog of the exact metric (
2.11
) is found by the analytic continuation (
2.28
),

\displaystyle ds^{2}=(\tfrac{1}{4}{\rm h}+1+\varkappa^{2})\Big[\frac{dr^{2}}{(1+r^{2})(1+\varkappa^{2}r^{2})}+\big(\frac{1+\varkappa^{2}r^{2}}{1+r^{2}}+\frac{8\varkappa^{2}}{{\rm h}}\big)^{-1}d\phi^{2}\Big]\ .

(2.31)

Again, formally taking
r^{2}\to-1+(1-\varkappa^{2})\sinh^{2}t
, the limit

\varkappa^{2}\to 1
is the exact metric of the
\frac{SU(1,1)}{\mathbb{R}}
gWZW model.

Interestingly the exact metric (
2.31
) may have different properties compared to the classical metric (
2.29
).
For example, analytically continuing further
(
\varkappa\to i\varkappa,\ r\to\rho,\ \phi\to it
)
to the non-split
\eta
-deformation of AdS
2
,
there is a singularity at

\rho^{2}={{\rm h}-8\varkappa^{2}\over\varkappa^{2}({\rm h}+8)}
that is always present in the classical limit
{\rm h}\gg 1

but absent for
{\rm h}<8\varkappa^{2}
.

Written in the conformally-flat coordinates as in (
2.30
), the quantum-corrected metric (
2.31
) is

\displaystyle ds^{2}=(\tfrac{1}{4}{\rm h}+1+\varkappa^{2})\Big[{dp^{2}+dq^{2}\over p^{2}+\varkappa^{2}q^{2}}-\frac{\varkappa^{2}}{2{\rm h}}\,\frac{\big[d(p^{2}+q^{2})\big]^{2}}{(p^{2}+\varkappa^{2}q^{2})^{2}(\tfrac{1}{4}+{2\varkappa^{2}\over{\rm h}}{p^{2}+q^{2}\over p^{2}+\varkappa^{2}q^{2}})}\Big]\ .

(2.32)

Expanding the metric (
2.32
) to first subleading order in small
{\rm h}^{-1}
one obtains

\displaystyle ds^{2}=\tfrac{1}{4}{\rm h}\,{dp^{2}+dq^{2}\over p^{2}+\varkappa^{2}q^{2}}+(1+\varkappa^{2}){dp^{2}+dq^{2}\over p^{2}+\varkappa^{2}q^{2}}-\frac{\varkappa^{2}}{2}\,\frac{\big[d(p^{2}+q^{2})\big]^{2}}{(p^{2}+\varkappa^{2}q^{2})^{2}}+{\cal O}({\rm h}^{-1})\ .

(2.33)

2.3
\lambda
-model

Let us now turn to the
\lambda
-deformed
\sigma
-models .
As we reviewed in Section
1.4.10
, the
\lambda
-model may be
constructed by starting with the
G/G
gauged WZW action and adding
a deformation quadratic in the gauge field, breaking some or all of the gauge symmetry,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{L}=k\,\Big(\mathcal{L}_{G}(g)+{\rm Tr}\big[g^{-1}\partial_{+}gA_{-}-A_{+}\partial_{-}gg^{-1}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+g^{-1}A_{+}gA_{-}-A_{+}A_{-}+(\lambda^{-1}-1)A_{+}PA_{-}\big]\Big)\ .\end{aligned}

(2.34)

One obtains a
\sigma
-model by integrating out the gauge field
A_{\pm}
(here
\mathcal{L}_{G}
is the WZW Lagrangian, see Section
1.4.7
)

\mathcal{L}=k\,\Big(\mathcal{L}_{G}(g)+{\rm Tr}[J_{+}\,M^{-1}\,K_{-}]\Big)\ ,\quad\qquad M=\operatorname{Ad}_{g}-I+(1-\lambda^{-1})P\ .

(2.35)

Here the integration over the gauge field was done classically so the resulting
\sigma
-model (
2.35
) should only be understood as a classical approximation that will be subject to quantum corrections (cf. the classical gWZW geometry in eq. (
2.4
)).
In what follows we shall construct such corrections in a manner consistent with 2-loop renormalizability in the simplest examples based on
SU(2)\over U(1)
and
SU(2)
.

2.3.1
SU(2)/U(1)
example

First let us consider the
\lambda
-model with 2d target space based on the symmetric space
{G\over H}={SU(2)\over U(1)}
.
We fix the
U(1)
gauge symmetry (generated by
\sigma_{2}
) by choosing the following parametrization of the coset element

\displaystyle\textstyle g=\exp(i\alpha\sigma_{3})\exp({i}\beta\sigma_{2})\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \cos\alpha=\sqrt{p^{2}+q^{2}}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \tan\beta=\frac{p}{q}\ .

(2.36)

The
\lambda
-model (
2.35
) then becomes

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}=\frac{k}{1-p^{2}-q^{2}}\big({\kappa}\,\partial_{+}p\partial_{-}p+{\kappa}^{-1}\partial_{+}q\partial_{-}q\big)\ ,\qquad\qquad{\kappa}\equiv\frac{1-\lambda}{1+\lambda}\ .

(2.37)

The model (
2.37
) again admits several useful limits.
In general, in a “boost” limit that infinitely rescales the Cartan directions, the
\lambda
-model gives the T-dual of the
\eta
-model on an analytically continued coset
[
151
]
(equivalent to the Poisson-Lie duality relating the
\eta
- and
\lambda
-models discussed in Section
1.4.9
).
In this special 2d case, the
\eta
-deformed
S^{2}
model is self T-dual (see footnote
26
) so the T-duality may be skipped:
infinitely rescaling the coordinates,
31
31

31

This limit takes the coordinates
(p,q)
out of their proper coordinate range
p^{2}+q^{2}<1
in (
2.37
). This may be avoided by instead considering the
\lambda
-model on the analytically continued coset
SU(1,1)\over U(1)
.

\displaystyle(p,q)\to\gamma(p,\kappa q)\ ,\qquad\gamma\to\infty

(2.38)

the metric becomes equivalent
[
151
]
to the
\eta
-model metric (
2.30
) on the analytically continued coset
SU(1,1)\over U(1)
.
Changing coordinates
(p,q)\to(r,\phi)=(q/p,\tfrac{1}{2}\log(p^{2}+q^{2}))

and analytically continuing
(r,\phi)\to(ir,i\phi)
, we obtain again the
\eta
-model (
2.6
) on
SU(2)\over U(1)
, with the couplings related by

\displaystyle{\rm h}=4k{\kappa}\ ,\qquad\varkappa=i{\kappa}\ .

(2.39)

The limit
{\kappa}=1
(or
\lambda=0
) corresponds to the classical metric of the
SU(2)\over U(1)
gWZW model:

\displaystyle ds^{2}=\frac{k}{1-p^{2}-q^{2}}({dp^{2}+dq^{2}})=k(d\alpha^{2}+\cot^{2}\alpha\,d\beta^{2})\ ,\ \ \quad(p,q)=\cos\alpha(\sin\beta,\cos\beta)\ .

(2.40)

As discussed in Section
2.1
, the gWZW metric (
2.40
) is known to be subject to quantum corrections at each order. Thus it is already clear that the
\lambda
-deformation (
2.36
) of this model should be subject to non-trivial quantum corrections.

The
\lambda
-model is known to be 1-loop renormalizable with only
{\kappa}
running
[
152
,
153
]
(eq. (
1.86
) with
c_{{}_{SU(2)}}=2
and
{\kappa}\equiv\tfrac{1-\lambda}{1+\lambda}
):

{\frac{d}{dt}}k=0+\mathcal{O}(k^{-1})\ ,\qquad\qquad\frac{d}{dt}{{\kappa}}=-k^{-1}(1-{\kappa}^{2})+\mathcal{O}(k^{-2})\ .

(2.41)

The main observation here is that one may ensure the 2-loop renormalizability of this model by modifying the classical action
(
2.37
) with a particular quantum correction.
A natural guess is that the necessary counterterms originate from a determinant of integration over the 2d gauge field, which appears quadratically in (
2.34
).
Such integrals are not well-defined
[
127
]
, with the resulting local terms depending on a choice of regularization. One particular prescription is to write the gauge field in terms of two scalars,
A=du+d*v
and integrate over
u,v
with measures
M

[
109
,
127
]
(see also
[
128
]
), obtaining the result

\displaystyle\int[dA]\,e^{i\int d^{2}\xi\,\sqrt{-h}\,(-M\,A^{\mu}A_{\mu})}=\exp\Big[\frac{i}{4\pi}\int d^{2}\xi\,\sqrt{-h}\,\big(\tfrac{1}{2}(\partial\log M)^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}R^{(2)}\log M\big)\Big]\ .

(2.42)

For generality we assumed a curved 2d background with curvature
R^{(2)}
, and we have ignored a trivial
M
-independent factor in (
2.42
).
The
R^{(2)}\log M
term in (
2.42
) is related to the familiar dilaton shift found in

[
129
]
(see also
[
130
]
), and vanishes on a flat 2d background.

However, the
(\partial\log M)^{2}
term remains non-trivial on a flat 2d background.
In this case
M=M(g)
is the matrix that appears in the part of the
\lambda
-model action (
2.34
) that is quadratic in the gauge field, i.e.
\mathcal{L}=\ldots+{\rm Tr}A_{+}M(g)A_{-}
. It generally depends on the group element
g
, taking the the form
M\propto 1+(\lambda^{-1}-1)P-\text{Ad}_{g}
(written as an operator
{\rm Lie}(G)\to{\rm Lie}(G)
). Hence the prescription (
2.42
)
suggests the
following
correction to the classical Lagrangian (
2.37
) of the
{SU(2)}\over{U(1)}

\lambda
-model,
32
32

32

Recall that the Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}={\rm G}_{mn}\partial_{+}x^{m}\partial_{-}x^{n}=-{\rm G}_{mn}\partial_{\mu}x^{m}\partial^{\mu}x^{n}
has a minus sign relative to the target space metric when written in covariant 2d notation. This explains the relative minus sign between the two lines in (
2.43
).

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\Delta\mathcal{L}=\tfrac{1}{2}(\partial\log\hbox{det}M)^{2}=\tfrac{1}{2}\big[\partial\log(1-p^{2}-q^{2})\big]^{2}\ ,\\
&\Delta(ds^{2})=-\tfrac{1}{2}\big[d\log(1-p^{2}-q^{2})\big]^{2}\ .\end{aligned}

(2.43)

Somewhat surprisingly, in this case the 1-loop correction (
2.43
) happens to be independent of the deformation parameter
\lambda
.

This leads to a 1-loop corrected metric for the
\lambda
-model (
2.37
) given by (cf. (
2.33
))

\displaystyle ds^{2}=k\,{{\kappa}\,dp^{2}+{\kappa}^{-1}dq^{2}\over 1-p^{2}-q^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\Big[\frac{d(1-p^{2}-q^{2})}{1-p^{2}-q^{2}}\Big]^{2}+{\cal O}(k^{-1})\ .

(2.44)

One can then check directly that the metric (
2.44
) indeed solves the 2-loop RG equations (
2.13
)
with (recall that
{\kappa}\equiv\tfrac{1-\lambda}{1+\lambda}

)

\displaystyle{\frac{d}{dt}}k=0+k^{-1}({\kappa}^{-1}-{\kappa})^{2}+\mathcal{O}(k^{-2})\ ,\qquad\qquad\frac{d}{dt}{{\kappa}}=k^{-1}(1-{\kappa}^{2})+0+\mathcal{O}(k^{-3})\ ,

(2.45)

and the components of the
diffeomorphism vector
\xi_{m}
given by

\displaystyle\xi_{p}

\displaystyle=\frac{2p}{1-p^{2}-q^{2}}\big[1+{1\over k}\frac{\kappa(p^{2}-1)-\kappa^{-1}p^{2}}{1-p^{2}-q^{2}}\big]\ ,

\displaystyle\xi_{q}

\displaystyle=\frac{2q}{1-p^{2}-q^{2}}\big[1+{1\over k}\frac{\kappa^{-1}(q^{2}-1)-\kappa q^{2})}{1-p^{2}-q^{2}}\big]\ .

(2.46)

The
\beta
-function for the deformation parameter
{\kappa}

does not receive a 2-loop correction.
Surprisingly, the parameter
k
(which has the interpretation of the level in the gWZW model) starts running
at 2-loop order.
Still, there is an RG invariant (cf. (
2.17
))

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}{\rm k}=0\ ,\ \ \ \ \qquad{\rm k}\equiv k+({\kappa}^{-1}+{\kappa})+\mathcal{O}(k^{-1})\ .

(2.47)

This suggests that it is
{\rm k}
rather than
k
that should be identified with level of the gWZW model in (
2.34
).
However, in this 2d case the WZ term vanishes upon gauge fixing (any
{\rm B}
-field is a total derivative in 2d) so the question of which is the WZ level becomes somewhat meaningless.

These results are consistent with the limits of the
\lambda
-model discussed above.
Setting (cf. (
2.36
),(
2.40
))

\displaystyle(p,q)=(1+{k}^{-1})\cos\alpha(\sin\beta,\cos\beta)

(2.48)

in (
2.44
) and taking the gWZW limit,
\kappa=1
, we find

\displaystyle ds^{2}=k(d\alpha^{2}+\cot^{2}\alpha\,d\beta^{2})+2(d\alpha^{2}+{\cot^{2}\alpha\over\sin^{2}\alpha}\,d\beta^{2})+\mathcal{O}(k^{-1})\ ,

(2.49)

which precisely matches the large
k
expansion of the exact gWZW metric (cf. (
2.4
)), i.e.

\displaystyle ds^{2}=(k+2)\Big(d\alpha^{2}+\frac{\cot^{2}\alpha}{1-\frac{2}{k}\cot^{2}\alpha}\,d\beta^{2}\Big)\ .

(2.50)

Note that in this limit the RG invariant in (
2.47
) becomes
{\rm k}=k+2
, i.e. the
shifted level of the
SU(2)/U(1)
gWZW model.
33
33

33

In the
{\kappa}=1
limit
the diffeomorphism vector (
2.46
) becomes a gradient of the exact dilaton of the gWZW model:

\xi_{n}=2\partial_{n}\Phi
,
\Phi=-\log(1-p^{2}-q^{2})-{1\over 2k}{1\over 1-p^{2}-q^{2}}+{\cal O}(k^{-2})
(cf.
footnote
24
).

In the
\eta
-model limit, the corrected
\lambda
-model metric (
2.44
) reduces to the leading correction in the
\eta
-model metric (
2.11
) if one modifies the scaling limit (
2.38
) and coupling relation (
2.39
) by certain quantum corrections,

\displaystyle p\to\gamma p\ ,\qquad\qquad q\to-i\gamma\varkappa\big[1-4{\rm h}^{-1}({1+\varkappa^{2}})\big]\,q\ ,\qquad\qquad\gamma\to\infty\ ,

(2.51)

\displaystyle{\kappa}=-i\varkappa\big[1-8{\rm h}^{-1}(1+\varkappa^{2})\big]\ ,\qquad\qquad\tfrac{1}{4}{\rm h}-1+\varkappa^{2}=-ik\varkappa\ ,

(2.52)

followed by again making the same change of variables
(r,\phi)=(q/p,\tfrac{1}{2}\log(p^{2}+q^{2}))
and continuing
(r,\phi)\to(ir,i\phi)
.
In this limit the RG equations (
2.45
) reproduce the corresponding ones for the
\eta
-model couplings
\varkappa
and
{\rm h}
in (
2.15
),(
2.16
)
with the RG invariant (
2.47
) being mapped to an analytic continuation of the
\eta
-model RG invariant (
2.17
),

\displaystyle{\rm k}=k+({{\kappa}}^{-1}+{{\kappa}})+{\cal O}(k^{-1})=i\frac{{\rm h}}{4\varkappa}+{\cal O}({\rm h}^{-1})=i\nu+{\cal O}(\nu^{-1})\ .

(2.53)

This may be viewed as a hint that a quantum deformation parameter
that appears
in the corresponding exact S-matrix should be
{\rm q}=e^{-{i\over{\rm k}}}=e^{-{1\over\nu}}
(cf.
[
151
]
),
which becomes
{\rm q}=e^{-{i\over k+2}}
at the gWZW point
{\kappa}=1
.

2.3.2
SU(2)
example

Now let us turn to the
\lambda
-model for the group
SU(2)

[
201
,
138
]
.
Parametrizing the group element as

\displaystyle g=\exp{\big[-i\arcsin\alpha\,\big(\cos{\beta}\,\sigma_{2}+\sin{\beta}(\cos{\gamma}\,\sigma_{3}-\sin{\gamma}\,\sigma_{1})\big)\big]}\ ,

(2.54)

we obtain from (
2.34
) the following 3-dimensional classical
\sigma
-model metric and
{\rm B}
-field
[
138
]

34
34

34

Note that, in these coordinates, WZ term
k\mathcal{L}_{{\rm WZ}}(g)
in (
2.34
) contributes
k(\arcsin\alpha-\alpha\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}})\sin{\beta}\,d\beta\wedge d\gamma
to the
{\rm B}
-field.

\displaystyle(ds^{2})^{(0)}

\displaystyle=k\big[\frac{d\alpha^{2}}{{\kappa}(1-\alpha^{2})}+\frac{\kappa\alpha^{2}}{\Delta}(d\beta^{2}+\sin^{2}\beta d\gamma^{2})\big]\ ,\quad\qquad\Delta(\alpha)\equiv{\kappa}^{2}+(1-{\kappa}^{2})\alpha^{2}\ ,

\displaystyle{\rm B}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=k\big(\arcsin\alpha-\frac{{\kappa}^{2}\alpha\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}}{\Delta}\big)\sin\beta\,d\beta\wedge d\gamma\ ,

(2.55)

\displaystyle{\rm H}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=d{\rm B}^{(0)}=\frac{k\alpha^{2}}{\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}\Delta^{2}}\big[2{\kappa}^{2}+(1-{\kappa}^{2})\Delta\big]\sin\beta\,d\alpha\wedge d\beta\wedge d\gamma\ .

As discussed in Section
1.4.10
,
\lambda
-models are known to be 1-loop renormalizable but, again, one can check that the classical background (
2.55
) does not solve the 2-loop RG equation (
1.9
) (with only
k,{\kappa}
running) in
any
covariant subtraction scheme and with
any

global symmetry acting on
\beta,\gamma
, the diffeomorphism vector must be of the form
\xi^{\alpha}=\xi^{\alpha}(\alpha)
,
\xi^{\beta,\gamma}=0
and the
{\rm B}
-gauge transformation must be trivial,
\partial_{[m}\lambda_{n]}=0
.)

However, we observe that it is possible to restore the 2-loop renormalizability of the model
by adding special
quantum
(1/k)
counterterms to this classical background,

\displaystyle ds^{2}

\displaystyle=(ds^{2})^{(0)}+\frac{2(1-{\kappa}^{2})^{2}\alpha^{4}}{{\kappa}^{2}(1-\alpha^{2})\Delta^{2}}d\alpha^{2}\ ,

(2.56)

\displaystyle{\rm B}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\rm k}}{k}{\rm B}^{(0)}-2\big(\arctan\frac{\alpha}{{\kappa}\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}}-\frac{{\kappa}\alpha\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}}{\Delta}\big)\sin\beta\,d\beta\wedge d\gamma\ ,

\displaystyle{\rm H}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\rm k}}{k}{\rm H}^{(0)}-\frac{4{\kappa}\alpha^{2}}{\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}{\Delta^{2}}}\sin\beta\,d\alpha\wedge d\beta\wedge d\gamma\ ,\qquad\qquad{\rm k}\equiv k+\frac{4+(1+{\kappa}^{2})^{2}}{4{\kappa}}\ .

This corrected background (
2.56
) indeed solves the 2-loop RG equation (
1.9
) with
35
35

35

The 1-loop terms in (
2.57
) match the general result (
1.86
) with
c_{{}_{SU(2)}}=2
and
{\kappa}\equiv\frac{1-\lambda}{1+\lambda}
.

\frac{d}{dt}k=0+\frac{(1-{\kappa}^{2})^{3}(5+3{\kappa}^{2})}{8k{\kappa}^{2}}\ ,\qquad\qquad\frac{d}{dt}{\kappa}=\frac{(1-{\kappa}^{2})^{2}}{2k}\Big[1-\frac{(1-{\kappa}^{2})^{2}}{k{\kappa}}\Big]\ .

(2.57)

The only non-zero component of the diffeomorphism vector
\xi^{m}
is

\begin{split}\xi^{\alpha}&=\frac{\alpha(1-\alpha^{2})(1-{\kappa}^{2})}{k\Delta}\Big[{\kappa}+\frac{2\alpha^{2}{\kappa}^{2}(1-{\kappa}^{2})-(3-2{\kappa}^{2}+{\kappa}^{4})\Delta^{2}}{k\Delta^{2}}\Big]\ ,\end{split}

(2.58)

and the B-gauge transformation in (

.

Note that the coupling
{\rm k}
defined in (
2.56
) does not run at 2-loop order,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}{\rm k}=0+\frac{1}{{\rm k}}\times 0+\ldots\ .

(2.59)

This RG invariant
{\rm k}
is the coefficient of
\arcsin\alpha
(present in
{\rm B}_{0}
in (
2.55
)) in the 1-loop corrected background (
2.56
). Choosing it
to be integer-valued removes the global ambiguities arising from the
\arcsin\alpha
term.
Furthermore, given that under a large transformation,

36
36

36

Note that
\arctan\frac{\alpha}{{\kappa}\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}}=\operatorname{sign}{\kappa}\,\arcsin\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{\Delta}}
and
\alpha=\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{\Delta}}
for
\alpha=0,\pm 1
.

\delta(\arcsin\alpha)=\operatorname{sign}{\kappa}\ \delta(\arctan\frac{\alpha}{{\kappa}\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}})\ ,

(2.60)

and that the coefficient of
\arctan\frac{\alpha}{{\kappa}\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}}
in (
2.56
) is integer-valued, the
\arctan
term does not lead to any additional ambiguities.
The quantization of the flux,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int{\rm H}^{(0)}=k\ ,\qquad\qquad\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int{\rm H}={\rm k}-2\ ,

(2.61)

also supports the identification of
{\rm k}
as integer-valued.

In the case of the
SU(2)\over U(1)

\lambda
-model
it was possible to write the 1-loop corrections
(
2.43
)
in terms of a simple
counterterm

\Delta\mathcal{L}=-\tfrac{1}{2}(\partial\log\hbox{det}M)^{2}=-\tfrac{1}{2}({\rm Tr}[M^{-1}\partial M])^{2}
in terms of the matrix
M
as defined in (
2.35
).
For this
SU(2)
case, we find however that this particular counterterm is not sufficient to restore 2-loop renormalizability (in any covariant subtraction scheme) — i.e. the required corrections (
2.56
) cannot be written in this form even after a covariant scheme change.
Instead,
let us also include
similar counterterms (with coefficients
c_{i}
)
built out of the quantity
M^{-1}\partial M
.
In addition, we may also include counterterms (with coefficients
d_{i}
)
proportional to the terms present in the classical Lagrangian (
2.35
) (and its image under parity). As a result, we are led to the following ansatz
37
37

37

In (
2.62
) and below,
\mathcal{L}_{\rm PCM}(a)\equiv-\tfrac{1}{2}{\rm Tr}[a^{-1}\partial_{+}a\,a^{-1}\partial_{-}a]
and
\mathcal{L}_{{\rm WZ}}
denote the principal chiral model and Wess-Zumino Lagrangian terms (see Section
1.4.6
).

\displaystyle\Delta\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=c_{1}(\partial_{+}\log\hbox{det}M)(\partial_{-}\log\hbox{det}M)+c_{2}\mathcal{L}_{{\rm PCM}}(M)+c_{3}\mathcal{L}_{{\rm WZ}}(M)

(2.62)

\displaystyle+d_{1}\mathcal{L}_{{\rm PCM}}(g)+d_{2}\mathcal{L}_{{\rm WZ}}(g)+d_{3}{\rm Tr}[J_{+}M^{-1}K_{-}]+d_{4}{\rm Tr}[J_{-}M^{-1}K_{+}]\ .

We find that this matches the required 1-loop corrected background
(
2.56
) provided the constants
c_{i}
and
d_{i}
take the following values

\displaystyle c_{1}=-\tfrac{3+{\kappa}}{2(1-{\kappa})}\ ,\qquad c_{2}=-\tfrac{2(1+{\kappa})}{1-{\kappa}}\ ,\qquad c_{3}=1\ ,

(2.63)

\displaystyle d_{1}=0\ ,\quad d_{2}={\rm k}-k-2\ ,\quad\ d_{3}=\tfrac{1}{2}\big[{\rm k}-k+(1+\kappa^{-1})\big]\ ,\quad d_{4}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\big[{\rm k}-k-3(1+\kappa^{-1})\big]\ .

Combining the quantum counterterms (
2.62
) with the classical Lagrangian (
2.35
) allows us to represent the
\sigma
-model corresponding to
the 1-loop corrected geometry (
2.56
) in the form

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=k\,\mathcal{L}_{{\rm PCM}}(g)+({\rm k}-2)\mathcal{L}_{{\rm WZ}}(g)

(2.64)

\displaystyle+\tfrac{1}{2}{\rm Tr}\Big[\big({\rm k}+k+(1+{\kappa}^{-1})\big)J_{+}M^{-1}K_{-}-\big({\rm k}-k-3(1+{\kappa}^{-1})\big)J_{-}M^{-1}K_{+}\Big]

\displaystyle-\tfrac{3+{\kappa}}{2(1-{\kappa})}(\partial_{+}\log\hbox{det}M)(\partial_{-}\log\hbox{det}M)-\tfrac{2(1+{\kappa})}{1-{\kappa}}{{\rm PCM}}(M)+{{\rm WZ}}(M)\

\displaystyle M(g)=\big(\tfrac{1-{\kappa}}{1+{\kappa}}\big)^{2}-\text{Ad}_{g}\ .

We note that coefficients of the WZ terms in (
2.64
) are RG invariants,
{\rm k}-2
(see eq. (
2.59
)) and
1
. This is consistent with usual expectations and suggests that the representation (
2.64
) is a natural one.

In the WZW limit
{\kappa}\to 1
when the RG invariant
{\rm k}
in (
2.56
) reduces to the usual shifted WZ level

{\rm k}\big|_{{\kappa}=1}=k+2\ ,

(2.65)

the corrections to the metric and
{\rm B}
-field in (
2.56

the expression in (
2.64

k\big[\mathcal{L}_{{\rm PCM}}(g)+\mathcal{L}_{{\rm WZ}}(g)\big]
. Indeed, even though the coefficients
\tfrac{3+{\kappa}}{2(1-{\kappa})}
and
\tfrac{2(1+{\kappa})}{1-{\kappa}}
in (

) blow up in the limit
{\kappa}\to 1
, the corresponding
M
-dependent expressions vanish faster and all
M
-dependence disappears in the
{\kappa}=1
limit.

\eta
-model limit

Like the
SU(2)\over U(1)
example discussed above, the
SU(2)

\lambda
-model (
2.55
) admits a formal “boost” scaling limit,

\alpha\to\sin(\alpha+i\zeta)\ ,\qquad\qquad\zeta\to\infty\ ,

(2.66)

when it becomes (dropping a closed 2-form contribution to the
{\rm B}
-field)

\begin{split}\widetilde{ds^{2}}&=k\Big[\frac{1}{\kappa}d\alpha^{2}+\frac{\kappa}{1-\kappa^{2}}(d\beta^{2}+\sin^{2}\beta d\gamma^{2})\Big]\ ,\qquad\widetilde{{\rm B}}=k\cos\beta\,d\alpha\wedge d\gamma\ .\end{split}

(2.67)

This geometry is related by abelian T-duality (in the
\alpha
direction) to the squashed 3-sphere
[
37
]
,

{ds^{2}}=k\Big[\kappa(d\tilde{\alpha}-\cos\beta d\gamma)^{2}+\frac{{\kappa}}{1-{\kappa}^{2}}(d\beta^{2}+\sin^{2}\beta d\gamma^{2})\Big]\ ,\qquad{\rm B}=0\ ,

(2.68)

which in turn has the interpretation as the
\eta
-deformation (
1.68
) of the
SU(2)
PCM (cf.
[
202
]
). The squashed 3-sphere geometry (
2.68
) is an anomalous case of an
\eta
-model that
is automatically renormalizable at 2-loops without needing quantum corrections (this statement was checked in App. B of
[
3
]
— and more generally for “squashed” models interpolating between a PCM and a symmetric space). As we shall now demonstrate, the quantum corrections derived above for the
SU(2)

\lambda
-model are consistent with this fact.

Starting from the 1-loop corrected
\lambda
-model background (
2.56
)
and taking the same scaling limit (
2.66
), we get a corrected version of (
2.67
),

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \widetilde{ds^{2}}&=\big(\frac{k}{\kappa}+\frac{2}{{\kappa}^{2}}\big)d\alpha^{2}+\frac{k\kappa}{1-\kappa^{2}}(d\beta^{2}+\sin^{2}\beta d\gamma^{2})\ ,\\
\widetilde{{\rm B}}&=\big[k+\frac{4+(1+{\kappa}^{2})^{2}}{4{\kappa}}\big]\cos\beta d\alpha\wedge d\gamma\ .\end{aligned}

(2.69)

The background (
2.69
) solves the 2-loop RG equations (
1.9
) with the same
\beta
-functions (
2.57
) as the
\lambda
-model and vanishing diffeomorphism vector and
{\rm B}
-gauge transformation (
\xi_{m}=\lambda_{m}=0
).
To extend the T-duality relation between (
2.67
) and (
2.68
) to the quantum level,
we use the 1-loop corrections to the T-duality transformation laws
of
[
203
]

(see also
[
204
]
), generalizing the quantum correction in (
2.5
).
38
38

38

We note that, in order to directly apply the results of
[
203
]
, one first has to perform a scheme change,
({\rm G}+{\rm B})_{\mu\nu}\to({\rm G}+{\rm B})_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{4}{\rm H}^{2}_{\mu\nu}
.

As a result, the T-dual of (
2.69
) is found to be

\displaystyle ds^{2}{}

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle=\big[k{\kappa}+\tfrac{1}{2}(1+{\kappa}^{2})^{2}\big]\big(d\tilde{\alpha}-\cos\beta d\gamma\big)^{2}+\big[\frac{k\kappa}{1-\kappa^{2}}+\tfrac{1}{2}(1-{\kappa}^{2})\big]\big(d\beta^{2}+\sin^{2}\beta d\gamma^{2}\big)\ ,

(2.70)

\displaystyle{\rm B}{}

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle=0\ ,

where we have rescaled
\tilde{\alpha}
.
Indeed this just the squashed 3-sphere geometry (
2.68
) with redefined parameters,

(k,{\kappa})\to(\hat{k},\hat{\kappa})\ ,\qquad\quad k=\hat{k}-\frac{3+\hat{{\kappa}}^{4}}{2\hat{{\kappa}}}\ ,\qquad{\kappa}=\hat{{\kappa}}+\frac{1-\hat{{\kappa}}^{2}}{\hat{k}}\ .

(2.71)

Thus the fact that the quantum corrected
\lambda
-model (
2.56
) is stable under the 2-loop RG flow is perfectly consistent, in the
\eta
-model or “boost” limit, with the fact that the squashed 3-sphere is automatically stable without quantum deformation.

We see in this example that
the known quantum corrections to the T-duality transformation rules
are naturally consistent with the
quantum deformations of integrable models for higher-loop renormalizability.
As we shall discuss in the next section, similar quantum corrections are expected for non-abelian duality.

2.3.3
Non-abelian dual limit

In a particular limit,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &k\to\infty\ ,\qquad\lambda=1-\tfrac{{\rm h}}{2k}\ ,\qquad g=e^{-\tfrac{h}{2k}v}\ ,\\
&v\in{\rm Lie}(G)\text{ and }{\rm h}\text{ fixed}\end{aligned}

(2.72)

the
\lambda
-model (
2.34
) becomes the non-abelian dual (NAD) of the PCM or symmetric space (see Section
1.4.5
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=-\tfrac{1}{2}{\rm h}\,{\rm Tr}[\partial_{+}v\,\frac{1}{P+{\rm ad}_{v}}\partial_{-}v]\ .

(2.73)

It was noted earlier in the literature
[
140
,
141
,
142
,
143
]
that the NAD theories (
2.73
) appear not to be renormalizable beyond the 1-loop order, prompting doubt about their inequivalence with the PCM and symmetric space
\sigma
-models . In this section, we shall resolve this confusion by observing that the classical NAD models (
2.73
) must be supplemented with particular finite counterterms to maintain renormalizability beyond 1-loop order.
We shall produce the necessary counterterms in the
SU(2)\over U(1)
and
SU(2)
examples by taking the NAD limit of the corresponding
\lambda
-model corrections constructed above.

NAD of
S^{2}
sigma model

For the
SU(2)\over U(1)

\lambda
-model in the coordinates (
2.37
), the NAD limit amounts to

\displaystyle p={\kappa}x\ ,\qquad q=1-{\kappa}^{2}y\ ,\qquad k\to\infty\ ,\quad{\kappa}\to 0\ ,\qquad{\rm h}\equiv 4k{\kappa}={\rm fixed}\ .

(2.74)

The resulting theory (
2.73
) is the NAD of the
\sigma
-model on
S^{2}
, with metric

\displaystyle ds^{2}=\frac{{\rm h}}{4}\,{dx^{2}+dy^{2}\over 2y-x^{2}}\ ,

(2.75)

and trivial
{\rm B}
-field since the target space is 2d. In the NAD limit (
2.74
), the leading quantum correction to the
\lambda
-model in (
2.44
) suggests a particular correction to the NAD metric (
2.75
),

\displaystyle ds^{2}=\frac{{\rm h}}{4}\,{dx^{2}+dy^{2}\over 2y-x^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\Big[\frac{d(2y-x^{2})}{2y-x^{2}}\Big]^{2}+{\cal O}(h^{-1})\ ,

(2.76)

which indeed solves the 2-loop RG equation (
2.13
) with only
{\rm h}
running,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}{\rm h}=4+\frac{16}{{\rm h}}+\mathcal{O}(h^{-2})\ ,

(2.77)

and matching the 2-loop
\beta
-function the dual undeformed
S^{2}={SU(2)\over U(1)}

\sigma
-model (eq. (
1.32
) with
c_{{}_{SU(2)}}=2
,

). Note that, since the coordinate redefinition in the NAD limit (

) depends on
{\kappa}={\kappa}(t)
,
the diffeomorphism vector gets an additional contribution compared to (
2.46
) and
its
components are given by

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\xi_{x}={4x\over 2y-x^{2}}-{1\over{\rm h}}{8x(1+3x^{2}-4y)\over(2y-x^{2})^{2}}+{\cal O}({\rm h}^{-2})\ ,\\
&\xi_{y}={2(2y-1)\over 2y-x^{2}}+{1\over{\rm h}}{8(1+2x^{2}-2y)\over(2y-x^{2})^{2}}+{\cal O}({\rm h}^{-2})\ .\end{aligned}

(2.78)

If one takes a further scaling limit,

\displaystyle x\to\gamma\cos\chi\ ,\qquad y\to\tfrac{1}{2}\gamma^{2}+\gamma y\ ,\qquad\gamma\to\infty\ ,

(2.79)

the NAD metric (
2.75
) degenerates to an abelian T-dual of
S^{2}
(in the
y
direction),
39
39

39

The T-dual metric (
2.80
) can also be found by directly taking the limit
k\to\infty
,
{\kappa}\to 0
with

p=\cos\chi
,
q={\kappa}y
and
{\rm h}\equiv 4k{\kappa}
fixed in the
\lambda
-model metric (
2.37
).

\displaystyle ds^{2}=\tfrac{1}{4}{\rm h}\,(d\chi^{2}+\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\chi}dy^{2})\ .

(2.80)

Taking a particular 1-loop modification of the limit (
2.79
) (cf. (
2.48
)),

\displaystyle x\to\gamma(1+4{\rm h}^{-1})\cos{\chi}\ ,\qquad y\to\tfrac{1}{2}\gamma^{2}+\gamma(1+4{\rm h}^{-1})y\ ,\qquad\gamma\to\infty\ ,

(2.81)

and shifting the coupling
{\rm h}\to{\rm h}-8
in the quantum-corrected NAD metric (
2.76
), we obtain a quantum-corrected T-dual metric (
2.80
),

\displaystyle ds^{2}=\tfrac{1}{4}{\rm h}\,\big[d\chi^{2}+\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\chi}\big(1+8{\rm h}^{-1}\cot^{2}\chi\big)dy^{2}\big]+{\cal O}({\rm h}^{-1})\ ,

(2.82)

which coincides with the known corrections (
2.5
) to the T-duality transformation rule
[
109
]

(with
{\cal M}=\sin^{2}{\chi}
and
{\rm h}\to\tfrac{1}{4}{\rm h}
so that (
2.5
) gives
\tilde{\cal M}=\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\chi}\big(1+2({\rm h}/4)^{-1}\cot^{2}\chi\big)
in this case).

NAD of
SU(2)
principal chiral model

In the case of the
SU(2)

\lambda
-model in the coordinates (
2.55
), the NAD limit (
2.72
) amounts to

\alpha\to{\kappa}\,\alpha\ ,\ \ \ \qquad\rm k\to\infty\ ,\quad{\kappa}\to 0\ ,\qquad{\rm h}\equiv 4k{\kappa}={\rm fixed}

(2.83)

where
{\rm h}
and the new coordinate
\alpha
are fixed.
Taking this limit in the 1-loop-corrected
\lambda
-model background (

) suggests a particular quantum correction to the
SU(2)
NAD model,

\displaystyle ds^{2}

\displaystyle=\Big[\frac{{\rm h}}{4}+\frac{2\alpha^{4}}{(1+\alpha^{2})^{2}}\Big]d\alpha^{2}+\frac{h}{4}\frac{\alpha^{2}}{(1+\alpha^{2})}(d\beta^{2}+\sin^{2}\beta d\gamma^{2})\ ,

\displaystyle{\rm B}

\displaystyle=\Big[\frac{{\rm h}+5}{4}\frac{\alpha^{3}}{(1+\alpha^{2})}+\frac{2\alpha}{1+\alpha^{2}}-2\arctan{\alpha}\Big]\sin\beta d\beta\wedge d\gamma\ ,

(2.84)

\displaystyle{\rm H}

\displaystyle=\Big[\frac{{\rm h}+5}{4}\frac{\alpha^{2}(3+\alpha^{2})}{4(1+\alpha^{2})^{2}}-\frac{4\alpha^{2}}{(1+\alpha^{2})^{2}}\Big]\sin\beta d\alpha\wedge d\beta\wedge d\gamma\ .

Indeed it solves the 2-loop RG equations (
1.9
) with

\beta
-function,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}{\rm h}=2+{2}{{\rm h}}^{-1}\ ,

(2.85)

matching that of the
SU(2)
PCM (i.e. (
1.20
) with
c_{{}_{SU(2)}}=2
) and diffeomorphism vector,

\displaystyle\xi^{\alpha}=\frac{2\alpha}{{\rm h}(1+\alpha^{2})}\Big[(1-\alpha^{2})-\frac{4(5+5\alpha^{2}+3\alpha^{4}+\alpha^{6})}{h(1+\alpha^{2})^{2}}\Big]\ ,\qquad\xi^{\beta,\gamma}=\lambda_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}=0\ ,

(2.86)

equalling the
\lambda

) in the NAD limit (

) plus an extra contribution due to the RG-dependent rescaling of
\alpha
.

We can also take the NAD limit (
2.72
) in the corrected Lagrangian written in the form (
2.64
),
getting

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=-(\tfrac{1}{2}{\rm h}+\tfrac{9}{4}){\rm Tr}\big[\partial_{+}v\,M^{-1}\partial_{-}v\big]-\tfrac{7}{4}{\rm Tr}\big[\partial_{-}v\,M^{-1}\partial_{+}v\big]\qquad\qquad

(2.87)

\displaystyle-\tfrac{3}{2}(\partial_{+}\log\hbox{det}M)(\partial_{-}\log\hbox{det}M)-2\,\mathcal{L}_{\rm PCM}(M)+\mathcal{L}_{\rm WZ}(M)\ ,

\displaystyle M\equiv 1+{\rm ad}_{v}\ .

This indeed matches with (
2.84
) upon choosing the coordinates,

v=-\tfrac{i}{2}\alpha\big[\cos{\beta}\,\sigma_{2}+\sin{\beta}(\cos{\gamma}\,\sigma_{3}-\sin{\gamma}\,\sigma_{1})\big]\ .

(2.88)

In a further limit of infinite shift of
\alpha

\alpha\to\alpha+\ell\ ,\qquad\ \ \ \ell\to\infty\ ,

(2.89)

the NAD background (

) degenerates to an abelian T-dual of the PCM on
SU(2)
(in the

{\rm B}

\displaystyle\widetilde{ds^{2}}=\tfrac{{\rm h}+8}{4}\,d\alpha^{2}+\tfrac{{\rm h}}{4}\big(d\beta^{2}+\sin^{2}\beta d\gamma^{2}\big)\ ,\qquad\ \ \ {\rm B}=\tfrac{{\rm h}+5}{4}\,\cos\beta\,d\alpha\wedge d\gamma\ .

(2.90)

This background (
\mathbb{R}\times S^{2}

SU(2)

\begin{split}{ds^{2}}={\rm h}\big(d\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta\,d\psi^{2}+\cos^{2}\theta\,d\chi^{2}\big)\ ,\end{split}

(2.91)

by (i) the 1-loop corrected T-duality transformation
\alpha\to\tilde{\alpha}
of
[
