]
, (ii) shifting the coupling
{\rm h}\to{\rm h}-2
, and (iii) changing coordinates
\tilde{\alpha}=\psi+\chi
,
\gamma=\psi-\chi
and
\beta=2\theta
.

Summary

These findings extend the previous conclusions
[
154
,
155
]
about the 1-loop quantum
equivalence of the models related by the non-abelian duality to the 2-loop level. As for abelian T-duality, NAD (properly modified by
quantum
\alpha^{\prime}
corrections) should also be a symmetry of the
\sigma
-model
\beta
-functions or the string effective action to all orders in
\alpha^{\prime}
.

Resolving earlier problems checking NAD at 2-loop level
[
140
,
141
,
142
,
143
]
,
we found that the preservation of quantum equivalence requires a non-trivial 1-loop correction to the
classical NAD model metric.
In the
SU(2)\over U(1)
and
SU(2)
cases, we explicitly constructed the corrections restoring
2-loop renormalizability, with the 2-loop
\beta
-functions matching the associated symmetric space and group models. This is a non-trivial result since these 2-loop
\beta
-functions are invariant under scheme changes (redefinitions of
h
) since the PCM/symmetric space and their NADs are
single-coupling
theories.

In further abelian T-dual limits, we obtained corrected versions of T-dual models matching the known quantum corrections to the T-duality rules (the simple case of (
2.5
) and the more general case of
[
203
]
). In these special cases, this approach serves as an alternate derivation of the leading correction to the T-duality transformation.

2.4
Discussion

We have demonstrated that, just as for gauged WZW models,
the invariance of integrable
\sigma
-models under the
two-loop (and higher) RG flow requires a specific quantum
deformation of the classical Lagrangian (i.e. of the target space metric and
B
-field).

In particular, we proposed an exact metric for the
\eta
-deformation of the
S^{2}
(and
H^{2}
)
\sigma
-model
that solves the 3-loop RG flow equations and is consistent with the gWZW limit.
We also found the leading-order deformation of the
\lambda
-model for
SU(2)\over U(1)
, which solves the
2-loop RG equations and is consistent with the gWZW and
\eta
-model limits.
For this example we identified the origin of the deformation as a finite counterterm
resulting from the determinant of integrating over the auxiliary 2d gauge field
(the same determinant that leads to the correction of the dilaton term on a curved 2d background).

We also found the leading-order deformation of the
\lambda
-model for
SU(2)
that solves the 2-loop RG equations,
but in that case the necessary counterterm does not take the same simple and identifiable form.
We checked the consistency of the corrections with the WZW model limit and the limit T-dual to the squashed 3-sphere (
\eta
-model).

In the non-abelian dual limit, the corrections for the
\lambda
-models generated predictions
for leading corrections to the
SU(2)\over U(1)
and
SU(2)
NAD models.
These corrections were indeed found to restore the NAD models’ 2-loop renormalizability,
resolving the earlier problems
[
140
,
141
,
142
,
143
]
in checking the consistency of non-abelian duality with the 2-loop RG flow.

The classical
\lambda
-model’s
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
transformation (
1.80
)
is respected by the leading corrections found in the above examples.
In explicit coordinates, this is seen as invariance of the counterterm in (
2.44
) (in the
SU(2)\over U(1)
case) under
k\to-k
,
{\kappa}\to-{\kappa}
,
p\to-p
; and invariance of the counterterms in (
2.56
) (in the
SU(2)
case) under
k\to-k
,
{\kappa}\to-{\kappa}
,
\alpha\to-\alpha
.
The resulting 2-loop
\beta
-functions (
2.45
) and (
2.57
) are thus invariant under
k\to-k
,
{\kappa}\to-{\kappa}
. As we shall see later in Chapter
4
(see eq. (
4.33
)), the preservation of this
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
invariance requires a particular formulation of the quantum theory, and in generic subtraction schemes it would require a certain coupling redefinition to become manifest.

In the
SU(2)\over U(1)
case, the
\lambda
-model (
2.37
) is invariant under an additional
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
transformation:
p\leftrightarrow q
,
{\kappa}\to{\kappa}^{-1}
(i.e.
\lambda\to-\lambda
), associated with the subgroup
H=U(1)
being abelian.
40
40

40

I thank B. Hoare for a discussion of this point.
Indeed this
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is also respected by the counterterm in (
2.44
), and the resulting 2-loop
\beta
-functions (
2.45
) are then invariant under
{\kappa}\to{\kappa}^{-1}
.

In summary, while some models (e.g., on groups or symmetric spaces) have sufficient
manifest
global symmetry to ensure renormalizability to all orders, general integrable models may not have any manifest symmetries. However, the
hidden
integrable symmetries still seem to constrain the RG flow. This pattern is found to persist at higher-loop orders, and in such cases the hidden symmetries seem to require a particular choice of finite counterterms for their preservation at the quantum level.

A general open question is how to systematically construct the necessary finite counterterms. One natural approach would be to try to derive them by imposing “Ward identities” for the hidden integrable symmetries
at the quantum level, i.e. requiring the choice of finite counterterms to be consistent with quantum integrability, and assuming that such counterterms would also be consistent with renormalizability.
Another approach, assuming a UV fixed point and a known underlying quantum S-matrix,
would be to try to systematically reconstruct the corrected
\sigma
-model from a dual massive model, as considered in
[
102
,
108
,
135
]
.

An interesting open problem would be to construct an
exact generalization of the 1-loop-corrected metric (
2.44
) for the
SU(2)\over U(1)

\lambda
-model reducing in the appropriate limits to the exact gWZW
metric (
2.50
) and the proposed exact
\eta
-model metric (
2.31
),(
2.32
). This is non-trivial given the lack of isometries in the
\lambda
-model metric
and that the effective action approach used for the gWZW model
[
118
]
does not appear to apply directly to non-conformal theories.

It would be interesting to see whether the exact metric (
2.11
) of the
SU(2)\over U(1)

\eta
-model,
translated to the Hamiltonian framework, would have a natural interpretation in terms of a deformation of
the classical integrable structure.

A case that was not yet considered is the higher-loop RG flow of the
\eta
-deformation of the PCM (rather than the symmetric space). For the simplest
SU(2)
case, the
\eta
-model happens to coincide
[
131
]
with the
\sigma
-model on a ‘squashed 3-sphere’
[
37
]
and is automatically stable due to manifest symmetries. This is because the
\eta
-deformation breaks the PCM’s symmetry to
G_{L}\times U(1)^{r}
, i.e.
SU(2)_{L}\times U(1)_{R}
in the
SU(2)
case, with
U(1)_{R}
happening to be a subgroup of
SU(2)
such that
SU(2)\over U(1)
is a symmetric space. This implies that the
SU(2)

\eta
-model must coincide with the “squashed” model related to
SU(2)\over U(1)
— part of a general family built on symmetric spaces
G/H
considered in App. B of
[
3
]
. (Note that general examples from that family are renormalizable due to manifest
G_{L}\times H_{R}
symmetry but
not
classically integrable: the
SU(2)\over U(1)
example is atypically integrable.) For
\eta
-models based on general groups, this coincidence does not happen, so the theory is not protected by manifest symmetries and one may expect to require
additional finite local counterterms to get higher-loop renormalizability.

Finally,
the
\eta
-model and the
\lambda
-model are, in general, related
[
156
,
151
,
157
,
158
,
159
]

by Poisson-Lie (PL) duality
[
160
]
(and analytic continuation).
Since the scaling limits between the
\lambda
-models and (T-duals of)
\eta
-models revealed that our 2-loop results for the two classes of models are consistent,
then the same observations made above for NAD are likely to apply also to PL duality.
Namely, PL duality (with suitable quantum corrections) should be a symmetry not only at 1-loop order
[
161
,
162
]
, but also at higher loops.
This expectation
was recently confirmed by several papers
[
267
]

proposing 1-loop corrections to the classical PL duality rules (including NAD as a special case
41
41

41

See also
[
87
]
on the leading quantum corrections to homogeneous Yang-Baxter deformations (equivalent to particular NAD transformations
[
96
,
97
]
) in order for the deformation to preserve 2-loop conformal invariance.
).
It would be interesting to check those results against the corrections (
2.76
),(
2.87
) found here for particular NAD models.
In a related approach, it has recently been proposed
[
238
]
that there is a particular 2-loop ‘‘subtraction scheme’’
42
42

42

The PL-symmetric subtraction scheme of
[
238
]
is not a covariant
subtraction scheme in the usual sense as it is related to the
{\rm G}{\rm B}
scheme (
1.9
) by a Lorentz-non-covariant transformation involving the spin connection.
in which PL symmetry becomes manifest. The
\lambda
- and
\eta
-models were claimed to be automatically renormalizable in that PL-scheme, suggesting the possibility to derive the corresponding quantum corrections in the standard scheme by reversing the scheme transformation.

Chapter 3
G\times G
and
G\times G/H
sigma models

It is
expected that classically integrable 2d
\sigma
-models should be stable under the renormalization group flow,
the intuition being that hidden symmetries
will constrain
the RG evolution.
Constraints on coupling constants required for integrability should thus be RG-invariant.

The aim of this chapter is to explore the connection between integrability and the RG flow on
another class of examples — integrable
G\times G
and
G\times G/H
models that were derived from the affine Gaudin construction
[
217
,
218
]
. These models may be viewed as generalizations of the PCM
k
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{{}_{{\rm PCM}_{k}}}={{\rm h}}\,\mathcal{L}_{{}_{{\rm PCM}}}+k\,\mathcal{L}_{{}_{\rm WZ}}(g)\ ,\qquad\mathcal{L}_{{}_{{\rm PCM}}}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\,{\rm Tr}[J_{+}J_{-}]\ ,\qquad J\equiv g^{-1}dg\ ,\ \ g\in G\ ,

(3.1)

i.e. the principal chiral model (with inverse coupling
{\rm h}
) with the WZ term (with level
k
).
The conformal WZW model is obtained at the special points
{\rm h}=\pm k
(see Secrtions
1.4.6
-
1.4.7
).

The PCM
k
admits various integrable deformations (see, e.g.,
[
131
,
133
,
134
,
201
,
137
,
219
]
and Section
1.4.9
above), which have been interpreted

[
217
]
as particular cases of integrable affine Gaudin models.
The affine Gaudin construction also produces natural generalizations of the PCM
k
to integrable models on products of group spaces
G^{N}=G\times\ldots\times G

[
217
]
.

Here we shall consider a subclass of such models defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\rho_{ij}\,{\rm Tr}[J^{(i)}_{+}J^{(j)}_{-}]+k_{i}\,\mathcal{L}_{{}_{\rm WZ}}(g^{(i)})\ ,\qquad\qquad

(3.2)

\displaystyle J^{(i)}\equiv{g^{(i)}}^{-1}dg^{(i)}\ ,\qquad\qquad g^{(i)}\in G^{N}\ ,\qquad i=1,\ldots,N\ .

We denote by
J^{(i)}
the Maurer-Cartan 1-form corresponding to
i
-th copy of
G
and

\rho_{ij}
is a
constant
coupling matrix (summation over repeated
i,j
is assumed).

The PCM
k
(
3.1
) corresponds to the special case
N=1
(with
\rho_{11}={\rm h}
and
k_{1}=k
), and is integrable for any values of its couplings. However, for
N>1
, the model (
3.2
) is classically integrable only for
special
couplings
(\rho_{ij},k_{i})

that correspond to
the affine Gaudin models
[
217
]
.
These are
selected as the solutions
of certain polynomial equations.
We will focus on the first non-trivial case of
N=2
, i.e. on
G\times G
models.

As we shall find in Section
3.1
,
the
classical integrability condition for

G\times G
theories (
3.2
)
is
automatically stable
under the
2-loop
RG flow
in
a particular subtraction scheme (extending the 1-loop results of
[
220
]
).
Here the 2-loop stability is obtained without the need for any finite counterterms.

The model (
3.2
)
is a special case of the 2d

\sigma
-model
(
1.6
),
whose 2-loop
\beta
-functions are given in the “
{\rm G}{\rm B}
scheme” in eq. (
1.9
) (see also Section
1.4.15
on subtraction schemes).
This special 2-loop scheme
effectively treats

{\rm G}_{mn}
and
{\rm B}_{mn}
as symmetrically as possible, with the respective

\beta
-functions being the symmetric and antisymmetric parts of a single tensor expression
[
197
,
163
]
(see also
[
170
,
221
]
). We note that in the context of this chapter, the diffeomorphism (
L_{\xi}({\rm G}+{\rm B})_{mn}
) and B-gauge transformation (
\partial_{[m}\lambda_{n]}
) terms in (
1.9
) may be ignored
since they
automatically
vanish in the examples considered below
due to manifest global
G_{L}\times G_{L}
symmetry.

Applied to the case of the PCM
k
in (
3.1
), the 2-loop
\beta
-function in
{\rm G}{\rm B}
scheme (
1.9
) gives (here we set
\alpha^{\prime}=1
)
43
43

43

In this chapter we denote
\tau=\log\mu
(instead of
t
) since we will use a coupling constant called
t
.

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}{\rm h}={{\rm c}_{{}_{G}}}\,(1-\tfrac{k^{2}}{{\rm h}^{2}})\,\Big[1+\tfrac{{{\rm c}_{{}_{G}}}}{2{\rm h}}(1-\tfrac{3\,k^{2}}{{\rm h}^{2}})\Big]\ ,\qquad\qquad\frac{d}{d\tau}{k}=0\ ,

(3.3)

so that the position of the WZW fixed point
{\rm h}=\pm k
remains unchanged at the 2-loop order.
The 2-loop PCM
k

\beta
-function (
3.3
) was
found in
[
197
]

using a scheme equivalent this one at the 2-loop level.
To recall, part of the scheme freedom
comes from the prescription of how one treats the antisymmetric 2d tensor
\varepsilon^{\mu\nu}
appearing in the
{\rm B}
-term in (
1.6
)
in dimensional regularization. Ref.
[
197
]
used a ’t Hooft-Veltman prescription of treating
\varepsilon^{\mu\nu}
as effectively 2-dimensional.
In
[
163
]
it was assumed that, in
d=2+\epsilon
dimensions,
\varepsilon^{\mu\nu}\varepsilon_{\rho\sigma}=f(\epsilon)(\delta^{\mu}_{\rho}\delta^{\nu}_{\sigma}-\delta^{\mu}_{\sigma}\delta^{\nu}_{\rho})
where
f=1+f_{1}\epsilon+...
, and then the
{\rm G}{\rm B}
scheme (
1.9
) corresponds to
the choice
f_{1}=-1
. As noted in
[
222
]
, the scheme used in
[
197
]

is equivalent (at least at the 2-loop level) to

f(d)=\frac{1}{d-1}=1-\epsilon+...
, i.e. to the choice
f_{1}=-1

[
163
]

of the
{\rm G}{\rm B}
scheme.

The
{\rm G}{\rm B}
scheme is naturally “adapted” to the vicinity of the WZW conformal point:
the derivative
\partial_{\rm h}\beta_{\rm h}\big|_{{\rm h}=k}
of the
\beta
-function for
{\rm h}
at the fixed point
correctly reproduces
[
197
]
the anomalous dimension of the
{\rm Tr}(J_{+}J_{-})
operator (PCM Lagrangian)
as computed
[
223
]
using the underlying infinite dimensional
Kac-Moody symmetry of the WZW model. Thus this scheme is
apparently
consistent with the preservation of the KM symmetry in
the vicinity of
the conformal point.

It is then natural to expect that this scheme should also play a special role in a more general class of
integrable models (
3.2
) containing WZW models as special limits,
44
44

44

Similar logic was recently used
in
[
224
]
in the discussion of the 2-loop RG evolution of a “squashed”
SU(2)
variant of
PCM
k
.

and should facilitate preservation of the hidden integrable structure of these models at the quantum level.
We will indeed see evidence for this below: the
classical integrability conditions for the
G\times G
model (
3.2
)
will be automatically preserved by the 2-loop RG evolution
provided one uses the
\beta
-functions in the
{\rm G}{\rm B}
scheme (
1.9
).

We shall also study,
in Section
3.2
,
a gauged analog of the models (
3.2
) defined on a coset space
G\times G/H
.
This theory,
which
was recently derived from affine Gaudin models in
[
218
]
,
may be viewed as a
generalization of the standard
G/H
symmetric space
\sigma
-model,
also including
WZ terms.
For these
G\times G/H
theories to be gauge invariant, the corresponding
couplings must satisfy certain linear relations. In addition, for a gauge invariant model to be classically integrable,
the couplings should further satisfy certain polynomial relations
[
218
]
.

We will
compute the RG flow for these
integrable
G\times G/H
theories, finding that they are stable under the
1-loop RG flow.
However, at the 2-loop level,
RG stability
does not
automatically
arise and, in general,
requires certain
finite redefinitions of the couplings. These are
equivalent to adding specific finite counterterms,
which may be interpreted as required for preservation of integrability
at the quantum level
(analogous to what was observed on other examples in Chapter
2
).
There are still a few special cases, in particular the integrable
T^{1,1}
model of
[
218
]
, that are automatically stable
under the 2-loop RG flow (see Section
3.2.2
).

In Section
3.3
we shall present a new integrable
\sigma
-model with
target space metric

{T^{1,q}=SU(2)\times SU(2)/U(1)}

[
225
]

and a particular
B
-field. The model admits as a special limit
the conformal GMM model with unequal levels
[
226
,
227
]
.
Our central observation is that, in the case of the subgroup
H
in
G\times G/H
being
abelian, the gauge invariance conditions of
[
218
]
are too restrictive and there is also a second
“branch”
of gauge invariant theories. This allows a natural generalization of the integrable
T^{1,1}
model of
[
218
]
to
T^{1,q}

with a general parameter

q
. We demonstrate that the resulting
T^{1,q}
model is classically integrable, admitting a Lax representation. We observe
that the
T^{1,q}
model is self-dual under T-duality in one isometry direction, and argue that this property
forces it to be stable under the RG flow.
We verify this fact explicitly by computing the corresponding 2-loop RG flow of the two coupling constants.

A few concluding remarks will be made in Section
3.4
.
Appendix
C
contains discussion of the integrability conditions for the
G^{N}
model (
3.2
) relevant to this chapter.
The explicit formulae for the 2-loop
\beta
-functions of the
G\times G
and
G\times G/H
models used in this chapter, and their derivations, are presented in Appendix
D
.

This chapter is based on the paper
[
5
]
and we closely follow the presentation there.

3.1
G\times G
models

As
mentioned above, the
G^{N}
model (
3.2
) is classically integrable for
special
values of its couplings
(\rho_{ij},k_{i})
satisfying certain polynomial relations, which originate from the affine Gaudin construction

[
217
]
. For such values of the couplings the model admits a Lax connection of the form

\displaystyle L_{+}=\alpha_{i}\,J^{(i)}_{+}\ ,\qquad\qquad L_{-}=\beta_{i}\,J^{(i)}_{-}\ ,

(3.4)

whose flatness condition,
F_{+-}(L)\equiv\partial_{+}L_{-}-\partial_{-}L_{+}+[L_{+},L_{-}]=0
, is equivalent to the equations of motion
following from (
3.2
). Moreover, the affine Gaudin construction
guarantees
the existence of an infinite tower of conserved, commuting higher-spin charges

[
229
]
, as reviewed above in Section
1.4.14
.

Below we shall consider the simplest
N=2
case of the
G^{N}
model (
3.2
) for a simple Lie group
G
.
We shall parametrize the
2\times 2
matrix
\rho_{ij}
in (
3.2
) in terms of the 4 components
s,t,u,b
as follows

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\begin{pmatrix}s&t+b\\
t-b&u\end{pmatrix}_{ij}\,{\rm Tr}[J^{(i)}_{+}J^{(j)}_{-}]+k_{i}\,\mathcal{L}_{{}_{\rm WZ}}(g^{(i)})\ .

(3.5)

Then the affine Gaudin condition for integrability is the vanishing of a cubic polynomial
[
217
,
220
]
,

\displaystyle f(s,t,u,b,k_{1},k_{2})\equiv-t\,(s+t)\,(t+u)+b^{2}\,(s+t+u)+t\,k_{1}\,k_{2}+b\,(u\,k_{1}-s\,k_{2})=0\ .

(3.6)

Let us note that the
affine Gaudin conditions for integrability
(e.g. (
3.6
) in the
N=2
case) are certainly sufficient for integrability. However, it is not a priori clear if they are necessary,
since there could also be integrable theories of the form (
3.2
) that are unrelated to the affine Gaudin construction
of
[
217
]
.
In Appendix
C
we presented a check that the condition (
3.6
) is also
necessary
for the integrability
of the
G\times G
model (
3.5
), assuming the natural ansatz (
3.4
) for the corresponding Lax connection.

3.1.1
RG flow in
G\times G
models

The general
G^{N}
model (
3.2
) has global
(G_{L})^{N}\times G_{R}
symmetry acting as

\displaystyle g^{(i)}\to u_{L}^{(i)}g^{(i)}u_{R}\ ,\qquad\qquad(u_{L}^{(i)},u_{R})\in(G_{L})^{N}\times G_{R}\ .

(3.7)

In fact, (
3.2
) is the most general 2-derivative local Lagrangian having this symmetry.
This implies that only
\rho_{ij}
can run under the RG flow
(with the WZ parameters
k_{i}
not renormalized as usual, since WZ terms do not have a local 2d representation that could be produced by the beta functions (
1.9
)).

Starting with the
\sigma
-model couplings
({\rm G}_{mn},{\rm B}_{mn})
corresponding to the
G\times G
model
(
3.5
) and computing the
corresponding 2-loop
\beta
-functions in the
{\rm G}{\rm B}
scheme (
1.9
),
we find

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\qquad\qquad\frac{d}{d\tau}\rho_{ij}=\alpha^{\prime}\,\beta_{ij}^{(1)}+\alpha^{\prime 2}\,\beta_{ij}^{(2)}+\ldots\ ,\\
\beta_{ij}^{(1)}={}&{}{{\rm c}_{{}_{G}}}\,F^{(4)}_{ij}(s,t,u,b,k_{1},k_{2})\ ,\qquad\beta_{ij}^{(2)}={\rm c}_{{}_{G}}^{2}\,(su-t^{2})^{-5}\,F^{(9)}_{ij}(s,t,u,b,k_{1},k_{2})\ ,\end{aligned}

(3.8)

where the matrices
F^{(4)},F^{(9)}
are homogeneous polynomials of degrees 4 and 9 in their arguments
and
{{\rm c}_{{}_{G}}}
is the dual Coxeter number of the group
G
, as in the PCM
k
case in (
3.3
).
The explicit expressions
for
F^{(4)},F^{(9)}

are given in Appendix
D.1
and also in an example below.

Remarkably, despite the complicated expressions for the
\beta
-functions, one is able to verify that
the integrability condition (
3.6
) is, in fact, preserved by the 2-loop RG flow:

\displaystyle{df\over d\tau}\Big|_{f=0}=\big(\alpha^{\prime}\,\beta_{ij}^{(1)}+\alpha^{\prime 2}\,\beta_{ij}^{(2)}+...\big)\,{\partial f\over\partial{\rho_{ij}}}\Big|_{f=0}=\alpha^{\prime}\times 0+\alpha^{\prime 2}\times 0+...\ .

(3.9)

The vanishing of the 1-loop
{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha^{\prime})
term in (
3.9
) was already established in
[
220
]
, and the vanishing of the 2-loop
term is a new non-trivial result. Let us stress that
this property of
the integrability condition (
3.6
)
not being deformed at the 2-loop level is specific to the

{\rm G}{\rm B}
scheme (
1.9
).

3.1.2
Example with
k_{1}=k_{2}=0

Let us consider the special case of the integrable
G\times G
models (
3.5
),(
3.6
)
with vanishing WZ levels,
k_{1}=k_{2}=0
.
In that case
the integrability condition (
3.6
) implies that

\displaystyle b=b(s,t,u)\equiv\Big[{t(t+s)(t+u)\over t+s+u}\Big]^{1/2}\ .

(3.10)

We thus obtain
from (
3.5
)
an integrable
G\times G
model with 3 independent couplings
s,t,u
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}s&t+b(s,t,u)\\
t-b(s,t,u)&u\end{pmatrix}_{ij}{\rm Tr}[J^{(i)}_{+}J^{(j)}_{-}]\ .\

(3.11)

Since
k_{i}
do not run, this special case of the model (
3.5
),(
3.6
) should also be stable under the RG flow, i.e. (
3.11
) should be renormalizable with only
s,t,u
running.
Indeed,
using for convenience the redefined
couplings
(s,t,u)\to(x,y,z)
with

\displaystyle x=s+t+u\ ,\qquad y=\frac{s}{t}\ ,\qquad z=\frac{u}{t}\ ,

(3.12)

the 2-loop
\beta
-functions (
3.8
) become

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}x=2{{\rm c}_{{}_{G}}}-\frac{{{\rm c}^{2}_{{}_{G}}}}{2x(yz-1)^{3}}\ \Big[16+32(y+z)+16(y^{2}+z^{2})+88yz+68yz(y+z)

(3.13)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+12yz(y^{2}+z^{2}+5yz)+8y^{2}z^{2}(y+z)-y^{2}z^{2}(y+z)^{2}\Big]\ ,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}y=F(x;y,z)\ ,\qquad\qquad\frac{d}{d\tau}z=F(x;z,y)\ ,

(3.14)

\displaystyle F(x;y,z)\equiv\frac{\,y(y+1)(y+2)}{\,(zy-1)^{2}}\ \bigg(\frac{{{\rm c}_{{}_{G}}}}{x}\big[1-zy-3(z+1)^{2}\big]

(3.15)

\displaystyle\qquad\ \ -\frac{{{\rm c}^{2}_{{}_{G}}}}{2x^{2}}\frac{1}{(zy-1)^{3}}\Big[-z^{6}y^{2}-y^{6}(3zy-38z-44)+2z(y+1)(26zy+101y+58)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\ \ \ +20(y+1)^{2}-z^{4}(y(3y((y-14)y-100)-296)-38)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\ \ \ -z^{3}(y(y(y((y-4)y-178)-728)-708)-152)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\ \ \ +2z^{2}(y+1)(y(y(5y+89)+262)+105)\Big]\bigg)\ .

The obvious symmetry between
s
and
u
in (
3.11
) is translated into the symmetry of the RG equations under

y\leftrightarrow z
.

The fact that
these 2-loop
\beta
-functions are much simpler than
the general (not necessarily integrable) case of (
3.8
)
(see also
Appendix
D.1
)
suggests that a substantial simplification happens
upon specifying the
couplings to be at the integrable locus
f=0

in
(
3.6
)
(this was already observed at the 1-loop order in
[
220
]
).

3.2
G\times G/H
models

Let
H
be a subgroup of
G
such that
G/H
is a symmetric space (we assume that both
G
and
H
are simple real Lie groups). Then the
Lagrangian for the gauged
G\times G/H
model of
[
218
]
takes the form
45
45

45

Our conventions in
(
3.16
) are related to the ones of
[
218
]
by
r_{ij}\to 2\rho^{(0)}_{ij}
,
\rho_{ij}\to 2\rho_{ij}^{(1)}
and
the opposite sign for
the WZ terms, i.e.
k_{i}\to-k_{i}
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\rho_{ij}\,{\rm Tr}[P^{(i)}_{+}P^{(j)}_{-}]-\tfrac{1}{2}\,r_{ij}\,{\rm Tr}[{B}^{(i)}_{+}{B}^{(j)}_{-}]+k_{i}\,\mathcal{L}_{{}_{\rm WZ}}(g^{(i)})\ ,

(3.16)

\displaystyle P^{(i)}=P_{{G/H}}J^{(i)}\ ,\qquad{B}^{(i)}=P_{H}J^{(i)}\ ,\ \ \qquad J^{(i)}={g^{(i)}}^{-1}dg^{(i)}\ .

(3.17)

Here
P_{{G/H}}
and
P_{H}
are projectors to the corresponding parts of
the algebra of
G
, and

\rho_{ij}
,
r_{ij}
are
constant
2\times 2
matrices.
The global symmetry
consists of
left multiplication
G_{L}\times G_{L}
, as well as the discrete
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
corresponding to
the symmetric space structure of
G/H
.
The action for (
3.16
)
is required to be gauge invariant under the local right action by an element of
H
(acting
the
same
on both
g^{(i)}
)

\displaystyle g^{(i)}\to g^{(i)}{\rm w}\ ,\qquad\qquad{\rm w}(\xi^{+},\xi^{-})\in H\ .

(3.18)

For general choices of
G
and
H
,
gauge invariance imposes the
linear
constraints
[
218
]
46
46

46

The special case of abelian
H
will be
discussed below in Section
3.3
.

\displaystyle k_{1}=-k_{2}\equiv k\ ,\qquad\qquad r_{ij}=\begin{pmatrix}r&-r-k\\
-r+k&r\end{pmatrix}\ .

(3.19)

The remaining free parameters of the gauge invariant model
are then
r
,
k
and the
2\times 2
matrix
\rho_{ij}
.

Requiring integrability imposes further constraints which,
as for the

G^{N}
models (
3.2
),
can be obtained from the affine Gaudin construction.
The following parametrization of the 6 constants
r,k,\rho_{ij}

in terms of 4 parameters
K,x,\zeta_{+},\zeta_{-}
was shown in
[
218
]
to be sufficient for integrability

\displaystyle r=r_{11}=r_{22}=K\frac{\zeta_{-}^{2}-\zeta_{+}^{2}}{(1-x^{2})^{2}}\ ,\ \ \qquad\qquad r_{12}=2K\frac{(1-\zeta_{+}^{2})(x^{2}-\zeta_{-}^{2})}{(1-x^{2})^{3}}\ ,

(3.20)

\displaystyle r_{21}=-2K\frac{(1-\zeta_{-}^{2})(x^{2}-\zeta_{+}^{2})}{(1-x^{2})^{3}}\ ,

(3.21)

\displaystyle\rho_{11}=K\frac{1-2\zeta_{+}^{2}+\zeta_{-}^{2}\zeta_{+}^{2}}{(1-x^{2})^{2}}\ ,\ \ \qquad\qquad\rho_{12}=x\,r_{12}=2K\frac{x(1-\zeta_{+}^{2})(x^{2}-\zeta_{-}^{2})}{(1-x^{2})^{3}}\ ,

(3.22)

\displaystyle\rho_{21}=x^{-1}\,r_{21}=-2K\frac{(1-\zeta_{-}^{2})(x^{2}-\zeta_{+}^{2})}{x(1-x^{2})^{3}}\ ,\qquad\qquad\rho_{22}=K\frac{x^{4}-2\zeta_{+}^{2}x^{2}+\zeta_{-}^{2}\zeta_{+}^{2}}{x^{2}(1-x^{2})^{2}}\ ,

(3.23)

\displaystyle k=k_{1}=-k_{2}=-K\frac{2x^{2}+2\zeta_{-}^{2}\zeta_{+}^{2}-(1+x^{2})(\zeta_{-}^{2}+\zeta_{+}^{2})}{(1-x^{2})^{3}}\ .

(3.24)

This parametrization is simply equivalent to the gauge invariance conditions (
3.19
)
combined with the two extra polynomial integrability conditions

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} f_{1}\equiv{}&{}r^{2}-k^{2}-\rho_{12}\rho_{21}=0\ ,\\
f_{2}\equiv{}&{}(r-k)^{4}\rho_{12}+(r-k)^{2}(r-k-2\rho_{11})(r-k+2\rho_{22})\rho_{21}\\
&{}\qquad\qquad\ \ \ -2(r-k)(\rho_{11}+\rho_{22})\rho_{12}\rho_{21}^{2}+(\rho_{12}+\rho_{21})\rho_{12}\rho_{21}^{3}=0\ .\end{aligned}

(3.25)

Two simple solutions of these conditions are found by setting

r=k
(i.e.
r_{21}=0
in (
3.19
)) and
either

\rho_{21}=0
or
\rho_{12}=0
.

We note that the integrable
G\times G/H
models satisfying (
3.16
)(
3.19
),(
3.16
) were recently found to be obtainable
[
98
]
from 4d Chern-Simons theory
[
264
]
.

3.2.1
RG flow in
G\times G/H
models

The structure of the gauge invariant
G\times G/H
action (
3.16
),(
3.19
) is protected
by the right
H
gauge symmetry (
3.18
) and the global
G_{L}\times G_{L}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry.
This rules out
all
counterterms
except those
corresponding to renormalizations
of the 6 couplings
r,k,\rho_{ij}
(of which
k
is not renormalized as usual).
Let us parametrize
\rho_{ij}
as in (
3.5
),

\displaystyle\rho_{ij}=\begin{pmatrix}s&t+b\\
t-b&u\end{pmatrix}\ .

(3.26)

Computing the
\beta
-functions (
1.9
) corresponding to the
\sigma
-model couplings
({\rm G}_{mn},{\rm B}_{mn})
for the model (
3.16
),(
3.19
),(
3.26
),
we find for the 1-loop
\beta
-functions of the 5 running couplings

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}{\rm h}_{p}=\alpha^{\prime}\beta_{{\rm h}_{p}}^{(1)}\ ,\qquad\qquad{\rm h}_{p}\equiv(r,s,t,b,u)\ ,

(3.27)

\displaystyle\beta_{r}^{(1)}=\frac{{{\rm c}_{{}_{G}}}-{{\rm c}_{{}_{H}}}}{(t^{2}-su)^{2}}\Big(r^{2}s^{2}-2b^{2}t^{2}-2r^{2}t^{2}+2t^{4}-2b^{2}su-2st^{2}u+r^{2}u^{2}

(3.28)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+4bstk+4btuk-s^{2}k^{2}-2t^{2}k^{2}-u^{2}k^{2}\Big)+{{\rm c}_{{}_{H}}}\big(1-\tfrac{k^{2}}{r^{2}}\big)\ ,

\displaystyle\beta_{s}^{(1)}=\frac{{{\rm c}_{{}_{G}}}}{r(t^{2}-su)}\big[b^{2}s-st^{2}+r^{2}u+2r(t^{2}-su)-2btk+uk^{2}\big]\ ,

(3.29)

\displaystyle\beta_{t}^{(1)}=\frac{{{\rm c}_{{}_{G}}}}{r(t^{2}-su)}\big[-b^{2}t+b(s+u)k+t(r^{2}-su-k^{2})\big]\ ,

(3.30)

\displaystyle\beta_{b}^{(1)}=\frac{{{\rm c}_{{}_{G}}}}{r(t^{2}-su)}\big[-b(t^{2}+su)+t(s+u)k\big]\ ,

(3.31)

\displaystyle\beta_{u}^{(1)}=\frac{{{\rm c}_{{}_{G}}}}{r(t^{2}-su)}\big[r^{2}s+b^{2}u-t^{2}u+2r(t^{2}-su)-2btk+sk^{2}\big]\ .

(3.32)

We observe that the integrability conditions (
3.25
) are
stable under the 1-loop RG flow (
3.27
)–(
3.32
),

\displaystyle{df_{a}\over d\tau}\Big|_{f_{1}=f_{2}=0}=\alpha^{\prime}\beta_{{\rm h}_{p}}^{(1)}{\partial f_{a}\over\partial{\rm h}_{p}}\Big|_{f_{1}=f_{2}=0}+{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha^{\prime 2})=0+{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha^{\prime 2})\ ,\qquad\qquad a=1,2\ .

(3.33)

However, it turns out that (as for the examples discussed in Chapter
2
)
this property of RG stability does not automatically extend to the 2-loop order.
Computing the 2-loop
\beta
-functions for the model (
3.16
),(
3.19
) in the
{\rm G}{\rm B}
scheme (
1.9
) (given explicitly in Appendix
D.2
),
we find that
the subleading correction to (
3.33
) is non-zero at general values of the couplings,

\displaystyle\beta_{{\rm h}_{p}}^{(2)}{\partial f_{a}\over\partial{\rm h}_{p}}\Big|_{f_{1}=f_{2}=0}\not=0\ ,\qquad\qquad a=1,2\ .

(3.34)

Moreover, we checked that
(
3.34
) is also non-vanishing in
arbitrary covariant 2-loop subtraction schemes.
47
47

47

More precisely, we considered arbitrary subtraction schemes related to the
{\rm G}{\rm B}
scheme (
1.9
) by covariant redefinitions of
{\rm G}_{mn}
and
{\rm B}_{mn}
(see Section
1.4.15
).

As in other examples
[
2
,
3
]
, one may expect to restore the property of RG stability at the 2-loop order by adding certain finite quantum
\alpha^{\prime}
-corrections to the target space geometry.
Because of the global and local symmetries, the only possible corrections would
correspond to redefinitions
{\rm h}_{p}\to\bar{\rm h}_{p}
of the couplings
{\rm h}_{p}=(r,s,t,b,u)
,

\displaystyle{\rm h}_{p}=\bar{\rm h}_{p}+\alpha^{\prime}Q_{p}(\bar{\rm h})+\ldots\ \ .

(3.35)

Such redefinitions may be interpreted as quantum corrections to the integrability conditions (
3.25
):
if the original couplings
{\rm h}_{p}
satisfied

f_{a}({\rm h})=0
, then the corrected ones
\bar{\rm h}_{p}
would satisfy a
corrected
version of the integrability conditions,

\displaystyle\bar{f}_{a}(\bar{\rm h})=0\ ,\qquad\bar{f_{a}}\equiv f_{a}+\alpha^{\prime}\,Q_{p}\,\partial_{{\rm h}_{p}}f_{a}+\ldots\ \ .

(3.36)

3.2.2
Example: integrable deformation of GMM model on
G\times G/H
and
T^{1,1}
model

There are still special exceptional cases of the integrable
G\times G/H
model (
3.16
),(
3.19
),(
3.25
) that are automatically stable under the 2-loop RG flow
in the
{\rm G}{\rm B}
scheme. As we shall demonstrate below, one of them is the
particular solution of the integrability conditions (
3.25
) that was studied in
[
218
]
,

\displaystyle r=k\ ,\qquad\qquad\rho_{12}=\rho_{21}=0\ ,\ \ \ {\rm i.e.}\ \ t=b=0\ .

(3.37)

The Lagrangian of the corresponding theory (
3.16
),(
3.19
) is given by

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathcal{L}={}&-\tfrac{1}{2}\,{\rm Tr}\big[{\rm h}\,P_{+}P_{-}+\widetilde{{\rm h}}\,\widetilde{P}_{+}\widetilde{P}_{-}\big]-\tfrac{1}{2}k\,{\rm Tr}\big[{B}_{+}{B}_{-}+\widetilde{{B}}_{+}\widetilde{{B}}_{-}-2{B}_{+}\widetilde{B}_{-}\big]\\
&{}\quad+k\,\big[\mathcal{L}_{{}_{\rm WZ}}(g)-\mathcal{L}_{{}_{\rm WZ}}(\widetilde{g})\big]\ ,\end{aligned}

(3.38)

where we have set
48
48

48

Ref.
[
218
]
used the notation
(k,\,{\rm h},\,\widetilde{\rm h})\equiv(\lambda^{2},\,\lambda_{2}^{2},\,\lambda_{1}^{2})
.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &(g^{(1)},g^{(2)})\equiv(g,\widetilde{g})\ ,\quad\widetilde{P}_{\pm}=P_{\pm}(\widetilde{g})\ ,\quad\widetilde{B}_{\pm}={B}_{\pm}(\widetilde{g})\ ,\\
&{\rm h}\equiv\rho_{11}=s\ ,\quad\widetilde{\rm h}\equiv\rho_{22}=u\ .\end{aligned}

(3.39)

This is an integrable deformation of the special point
{\rm h}=\widetilde{\rm h}=k
that corresponds to the conformal GMM model
[
226
]
on the homogeneous space
G\times G/H
with equal levels.

Specializing the 1-loop and 2-loop
\beta
-functions in (
3.27
)–(
3.32
) and Appendix
D.2
to this case,
we find that the model (
3.38
) is automatically stable under 2-loop renormalization
with only
{\rm h}
and
\widetilde{\rm h}
running,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\frac{d}{d\tau}{\rm h}=2{{\rm c}_{{}_{G}}}\big(1-\tfrac{k}{{\rm h}}\big)\Big(1+\tfrac{1}{{\rm h}}\Big[2({{\rm c}_{{}_{G}}}-{{\rm c}_{{}_{H}}})-(3{{\rm c}_{{}_{G}}}-2{{\rm c}_{{}_{H}}})\tfrac{k}{{\rm h}}\Big]\Big)\ ,\qquad\\
&\frac{d}{d\tau}\widetilde{\rm h}=2{{\rm c}_{{}_{G}}}(1-\tfrac{k}{\widetilde{\rm h}}\big)\Big(1+\tfrac{1}{\widetilde{\rm h}}\Big[2({{\rm c}_{{}_{G}}}-{{\rm c}_{{}_{H}}})-(3{{\rm c}_{{}_{G}}}-2{{\rm c}_{{}_{H}}})\tfrac{k}{\widetilde{\rm h}}\Big]\Big)\ ,\quad\qquad\frac{d}{d\tau}k=0\ .\end{aligned}

(3.40)

Remarkably, the RG evolution of
{\rm h}
and
\widetilde{\rm h}
is decoupled. Note that the structure of their
\beta
-functions is similar to the one in the PCM
k
case (
3.3
).
As expected, the GMM model
{\rm h}=\widetilde{\rm h}=k
is a fixed point.

Let us consider the simplest example of this theory (
3.38
) with
G=SU(2)
and
H=U(1)
and
choose the parametrization

\displaystyle g=e^{\phi_{1}T_{1}}e^{\theta_{1}T_{2}}e^{\psi T_{3}}\ ,\qquad\qquad\widetilde{g}=e^{-\phi_{2}T_{1}}e^{-\theta_{2}T_{2}}e^{-\widetilde{\psi}T_{3}}\ ,

(3.41)

where the
SU(2)
generators are

T_{A}=\tfrac{i}{2}\sigma_{A}

and the generator of
H=U(1)

is
T_{3}
. We shall fix the
H
gauge freedom by setting
\widetilde{\psi}=0
. As a result, we get an integrable
5-dimensional
\sigma
-model (cf. (
1.6
))

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}={}

\displaystyle(G_{mn}+B_{mn})\partial_{+}x^{m}\partial_{-}x^{n}=\tfrac{1}{4}k\,\Big[\partial_{+}\psi\partial_{-}\psi+\cos^{2}{\theta_{1}}\,\partial_{+}\phi_{1}\partial_{-}\phi_{1}+\cos^{2}{\theta_{2}}\,\partial_{+}\phi_{2}\partial_{-}\phi_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+2\cos{\theta_{1}}\,\partial_{+}\phi_{1}\partial_{-}\psi+2\cos{\theta_{2}}\,\partial_{+}\psi\partial_{-}\phi_{2}+2\cos{\theta_{1}}\cos{\theta_{2}}\,\partial_{+}\phi_{1}\partial_{-}\phi_{2}\Big]

(3.42)

\displaystyle\qquad+\tfrac{1}{4}{\rm h}\,\big[\partial_{+}\theta_{1}\partial_{-}\theta_{1}+\sin^{2}{\theta_{1}}\,\partial_{+}\phi_{1}\partial_{-}\phi_{1}\big]+\tfrac{1}{4}\widetilde{\rm h}\,\big[\partial_{+}\theta_{2}\partial_{-}\theta_{2}+\sin^{2}{\theta_{2}}\,\partial_{+}\phi_{2}\partial_{-}\phi_{2}\big]\ .

The resulting target space geometry
corresponds to
the
T^{1,1}
metric
and a particular
B
-field
[
218
]
49
49

49

Due to
differing conventions, the
B
-field here is opposite in sign to that in
[
218
]
.
This difference is not significant, and can be removed by a parity transformation.

\displaystyle ds^{2}_{T^{1,1}}={}

\displaystyle G_{mn}dx^{m}dx^{n}=\tfrac{1}{4}k\,(d\psi+\cos{\theta_{1}}\,d\phi_{1}+\cos{\theta_{2}}\,d\phi_{2})^{2}

(3.43)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\ \ +\tfrac{1}{4}{\rm h}\,(d\theta_{1}^{2}+\sin^{2}{\theta_{1}}\,d\phi_{1}^{2})+\tfrac{1}{4}\widetilde{\rm h}\,(d\theta_{2}^{2}+\sin^{2}{\theta_{2}}\,d\phi_{2}^{2})\ ,

\displaystyle B={}

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}B_{mn}dx^{m}\wedge dx^{n}=\tfrac{1}{4}k\,(d\psi+\cos{\theta_{1}}\,d\phi_{1})\wedge(d\psi+\cos{\theta_{2}}\,d\phi_{2})\ .

(3.44)

The 3 parameters
{\rm h},\widetilde{\rm h},k
of (
3.38
) are thus mapped to the 3 parameters of the
T^{1,1}
metric in
[
225
]
. To recall, the
T^{1,1}
metric
(
3.43
)
is
an Einstein space
if
{\rm h}=\widetilde{\rm h}={3\over 2}k
.
It then serves as a base of a Ricci flat 6d conifold with metric
dr^{2}+r^{2}ds^{2}_{T^{1,1}}
if we formally
set

k=\tfrac{1}{9}

so that
R_{ij}=4g_{ij}
.
In general, the non-zero components of the Ricci tensor of the
cone geometry

ds^{2}=G_{mn}(X)dX^{m}dX^{n}=dr^{2}+r^{2}g_{ij}(x)dx^{i}dx^{j}
(with
i=1,...,d
)
are
{R_{ij}(G)=R_{ij}(g)-(d-1)g_{ij}}
. Thus it vanishes if
g_{ij}
is an Einstein metric with a particular value of the scalar curvature

R(g)=d(d-1)
(this condition is satisfied, e.g., for a unit-radius sphere
S^{d}
when
G_{mn}
is flat).

For the integrable
T^{1,1}
model (
3.42
), the 2-loop RG equations (
3.40
) become (putting
{{\rm c}_{{}_{G}}}=2,\ {{\rm c}_{{}_{H}}}=0
)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \frac{d}{d\tau}{\rm h}=4\big(1-\tfrac{k}{{\rm h}}\big)\big[1+\tfrac{4}{{\rm h}}\big(1-\tfrac{3\,k}{2\,{\rm h}}\big)\big]\ ,\qquad\qquad\ &\frac{d}{d\tau}\widetilde{h}=4\big(1-\tfrac{k}{\widetilde{\rm h}}\big)\big[1+\tfrac{4}{\widetilde{\rm h}}\big(1-\tfrac{3\,k}{2\,\widetilde{\rm h}}\big)\big]\ .\end{aligned}

(3.45)

As we shall discuss in Section
3.3.2
, the 2-loop RG stability of this
T^{1,1}
model may be understood
as consequence
of the fact
that the
\sigma
-model (
3.42
) is self-dual under T-duality in the
\psi
-direction.

3.3
Integrable
T^{1,q}
model

Let us now introduce a new integrable
\sigma
-model with
target space metric

T^{1,q}

and a particular
B
-field, which is a
one-parameter generalization of the
T^{1,1}
model (
3.42
) of
[
218
]
.
Its special conformal case will be the
SU(2)\times SU(2)/U(1)
GMM model,
now
with
unequal

WZ levels
[
226
,
227
]
(with their ratio related to
the parameter
q
).

Our central observation is that, starting with the
G\times G/H
model (
3.16
) and considering
the case when the subgroup
H
is
abelian
, the gauge invariance condition (
3.19
) of
[
218
]
is too restrictive.
At the particular point
\rho_{12}=\rho_{21}=0
, there is also a second
“branch”
of gauge invariant models,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{L}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\rho_{ij}\,{\rm Tr}[P^{(i)}_{+}P^{(j)}_{-}]-\tfrac{1}{2}\,r_{ij}\,{\rm Tr}[{B}^{(i)}_{+}{B}^{(j)}_{-}]+k_{i}\,\mathcal{L}_{{}_{\rm WZ}}(g^{(i)})\ ,\\
&\rho_{12}=\rho_{21}=0\ ,\qquad r_{ij}=\begin{pmatrix}r&q(-r-k_{1})\\
q(-r+k_{1})&q^{2}r\end{pmatrix}\ ,\qquad q^{2}\equiv-k_{2}/k_{1}\ ,\end{aligned}

(3.46)

where
k_{1}
,
k_{2}
are assumed to be of opposite sign.
The theory (
3.46
)
is invariant under a modified gauge transformation

\displaystyle(g^{(1)},\,g^{(2)})\to(g^{(1)}{\rm w}^{q},\,g^{(2)}{\rm w})\ ,\qquad\qquad{\rm w}={\rm w}(\xi^{+},\xi^{-})\in H\ .

(3.47)

Here
{\rm w}^{q}
is the
q
-th power of the abelian group element
{\rm w}
.
In the case when the abelian
H
is compact
then,
to make
{\rm w}^{q}
single-valued, one should
assume that

q=\sqrt{|{k_{2}\over k_{1}}|}
is an integer.
50
50

50

More generally, one could consider a “twisted” action of the abelian subgroup,
(g^{(1)},\,g^{(2)})\to(g^{(1)}{\rm w}^{q},\,g^{(2)}{\rm w}^{p})

characterized by integers
p,q
satisfying
q^{2}/p^{2}=-k_{2}/k_{1}
.
In the
SU(2)\times SU(2)/U(1)
example discussed below, that would lead to the
T^{p,q}
model.

The reason for the
restriction of
H
to be abelian if
q\neq 1
is that the variation of the Lagrangian (
3.16
) under (
3.47
) with

{\rm w}\in H
will be proportional to
(q-1)\mathcal{L}_{{}_{\rm WZ}}({\rm w})
, which vanishes for abelian
H
for any
q
. We also need to assume
\rho_{12}=\rho_{21}
to prevent mixing between
P^{(1)}
and
P^{(2)}
terms, which are rotated differently by
{\rm w}^{q}
and
{\rm w}
respectively.
At the value
q=1
(i.e.
k_{1}=-k_{2}
), this model
intersects with
the first “branch” of gauge-invariant
G\times G/H
theories (
3.16
),(
3.19
) considered above.

Note that, in generic cases, WZ terms present a topological obstruction to gauging
[
232
]
. There are, however, special “anomaly-free” subgroups of the WZ term’s global symmetry
G_{L}\times G_{R}
that can be gauged
[
233
]
, satisfying
{\rm Tr}_{L}[T_{A}T_{B}]-{\rm Tr}_{R}[T_{A}T_{B}]=0
. This condition is satisfied here by the gauge transformations (
3.18
) and (
3.47
) on both “branches” of theories, due to cancellation between the two copies of
G
in
G\times G/H
.

We
claim
that model (
3.46
) is integrable (admitting a Lax representation)
if
51
51

51

The case
r=-k
is also integrable since it is related to (
3.48
) by parity.

\displaystyle r=k\ .

(3.48)

In this case it becomes a
generalization of (
3.38
) to
the case of
unequal levels
k,\widetilde{k}
,

\displaystyle\hskip-5.69046pt\mathcal{L}={}

\displaystyle{}-\tfrac{1}{2}\,{\rm Tr}\big[{\rm h}\,P_{+}P_{-}+\widetilde{\rm h}\,\widetilde{P}_{+}\widetilde{P}_{-}\big]-\tfrac{1}{2}\,{\rm Tr}\big[k\,{B}_{+}{B}_{-}+\widetilde{k}\,\widetilde{{B}}_{+}\widetilde{{B}}_{-}-2\sqrt{k\widetilde{k}}\,{B}_{+}\widetilde{B}_{-}\big]

(3.49)

\displaystyle\quad+k\mathcal{L}_{{}_{\rm WZ}}(g)-\widetilde{k}\mathcal{L}_{{}_{\rm WZ}}(\widetilde{g})\ ,

where we have set (cf. (
3.39
))

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &(g^{(1)},g^{(2)})\equiv(g,\widetilde{g})\ ,\qquad\widetilde{P}_{\pm}=P_{\pm}(\widetilde{g}),\qquad\widetilde{B}_{\pm}={B}_{\pm}(\widetilde{g}),\ \\
&{\rm h}\equiv\rho_{11},\qquad\widetilde{\rm h}\equiv\rho_{22}\ ,\qquad k\equiv k_{1},\quad\widetilde{k}\equiv-k_{2}\ ,\qquad q=\sqrt{\tfrac{\widetilde{k}}{k}}\ .\end{aligned}

(3.50)

The fact that the
\widetilde{k}=k
limit (
3.38
) is an integrable theory provides a first check of the integrability of (
3.49
):
its Lax connection was obtained
[
218
]
by taking the limit
r=k
,
\rho_{12}=\rho_{21}=0
of the affine Gaudin Lax connection for the general integrable
G\times G/H
model (
3.16
),(
3.19
),(
3.25
).
It was found that certain components of the
\operatorname{Lie}(G)
-valued Lax connection degenerate to zero and thus the flatness condition of the resulting
connection
L_{\pm}
does not imply all of the equations of motion.
However, one can consider a generalized limiting procedure
by infinitely rescaling the spectral parameter while taking this limit, thus
obtaining a second connection
\widetilde{L}_{\pm}
that “misses” a different subset of equations of motion.
The flatness conditions of the two Lax connections together encode the full set of the equations
of motion
[
218
]
,

\displaystyle L_{+}(z)={B}_{+}+z^{-1}P_{+}\ ,\qquad\ \ \ \ L_{-}(z)=\frac{1}{kz^{2}-{\rm h}}\Big[(k-{\rm h})({B}_{-}+zP_{-})+k(z^{2}-1)\widetilde{B}_{-}\Big]\ ,

(3.51)

\displaystyle\widetilde{L}_{-}(z)=\widetilde{B}_{-}+z\widetilde{P}_{-}\ ,\quad\ \ \ \,\widetilde{L}_{+}(z)=\frac{1}{kz^{-2}-\widetilde{\rm h}}\Big[(k-\widetilde{\rm h})(\widetilde{B}_{+}+z^{-1}\widetilde{P}_{+})+k(z^{-2}-1){B}_{+}\Big]\ .

(3.52)

The fact of having two separate Lax connections may seem unusual but should be sufficient for the
integrability applications: for example, each Lax connection will lead to its own family of conserved charges.
They may also be regarded as a single block-diagonal connection valued in
\operatorname{Lie}(G)\oplus\operatorname{Lie}(G)
. Indeed for
k=0
the two Lax connections (
3.51
),(
3.52
) become the familiar ones (
1.33
)
of the two decoupled
G/H

\sigma
-models.
52
52

52

I thank B. Hoare for this comment.

We observe that the Lax connections (
3.51
),(
3.52
) naturally generalize to the new model (
3.49
) with unequal levels by replacing certain factors of
k\to\widetilde{k}
,

\displaystyle L_{+}(z)={B}_{+}+z^{-1}P_{+}\ ,\qquad L_{-}(z)=\frac{1}{kz^{2}-{\rm h}}\Big[(k-{\rm h})({B}_{-}+zP_{-})+\sqrt{k\widetilde{k}}(z^{2}-1)\widetilde{B}_{-}\Big]\ ,

(3.53)

\displaystyle\widetilde{L}_{-}(z)=\widetilde{B}_{-}+z\widetilde{P}_{-}\ ,\ \ \,\widetilde{L}_{+}(z)=\frac{1}{\widetilde{k}z^{-2}-\widetilde{\rm h}}\Big[(\widetilde{k}-\widetilde{\rm h})(\widetilde{B}_{+}+z^{-1}\widetilde{P}_{+})+\sqrt{k\widetilde{k}}(z^{-2}-1){B}_{+}\Big]\ .

(3.54)

Assuming the simplest case
G=SU(2)
,
H=U(1)
,
using the same coordinate parametrization of this
SU(2)\times SU(2)/U(1)
model as in (
3.41
),
and fixing again the
H=U(1)
gauge as
\widetilde{\psi}=0
, we find the following generalization of (
3.42
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}={}

\displaystyle(G_{mn}+B_{mn})\partial_{+}x^{m}\partial_{-}x^{n}=\tfrac{1}{4}k\,\Big[\partial_{+}\psi\partial_{-}\psi+\cos^{2}{\theta_{1}}\,\partial_{+}\phi_{1}\partial_{-}\phi_{1}+q^{2}\cos^{2}{\theta_{2}}\,\partial_{+}\phi_{2}\partial_{-}\phi_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+2\cos{\theta_{1}}\,\partial_{+}\phi_{1}\partial_{-}\psi+2q\cos{\theta_{2}}\,\partial_{+}\psi\partial_{-}\phi_{2}+2q\cos{\theta_{1}}\cos{\theta_{2}}\,\partial_{+}\phi_{1}\partial_{-}\phi_{2}\Big]

(3.55)

\displaystyle+\tfrac{1}{4}{\rm h}\,\big[\partial_{+}\theta_{1}\partial_{-}\theta_{1}+\sin^{2}{\theta_{1}}\,\partial_{+}\phi_{1}\partial_{-}\phi_{1}\big]+\tfrac{1}{4}\widetilde{\rm h}\,\big[\partial_{+}\theta_{2}\partial_{-}\theta_{2}+\sin^{2}{\theta_{2}}\,\partial_{+}\phi_{2}\partial_{-}\phi_{2}\big]\ ,\qquad q=\sqrt{\tfrac{\widetilde{k}}{k}}\ .

The resulting target space metric is that of the
T^{1,q}
space
[
225
]

and the
B
-field is a natural generalization of the one in (

),

\displaystyle ds^{2}_{T^{1,q}}={}

\displaystyle G_{mn}dx^{m}dx^{n}=\tfrac{1}{4}k\,(d\psi+\cos{\theta_{1}}\,d\phi_{1}+q\cos{\theta_{2}}\,d\phi_{2})^{2}

(3.56)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\tfrac{1}{4}{\rm h}\,(d\theta_{1}^{2}+\sin^{2}{\theta_{1}}\,d\phi_{1}^{2})+\tfrac{1}{4}\widetilde{\rm h}\,(d\theta_{2}^{2}+\sin^{2}{\theta_{2}}\,d\phi_{2}^{2})\ ,

\displaystyle B={}

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}B_{mn}dx^{m}\wedge dx^{n}=\tfrac{1}{4}k\,(d\psi+\cos{\theta_{1}}\,d\phi_{1})\wedge(d\psi+q\cos{\theta_{2}}\,d\phi_{2})\ .

(3.57)

Like the
q=1
case
[
218
]
in (
3.42
),
the presence of the
B
-field is crucial here for integrability
(the
T^{1,q}

\sigma
-model without
B
-field is not integrable
[
235
,
236
]
).
The coordinate form of the Lax connections (
3.53
),(
3.54
) is (
T_{A}

are the
SU(2)
generators in (
3.41
))

\displaystyle{L^{\prime}}_{+}(z)=\cos{\theta_{1}}\,\partial_{+}\phi_{1}\,T_{3}+z^{-1}\big(\partial_{+}\theta_{1}\,T_{2}+\sin\theta_{1}\,\partial_{+}\phi_{1}\,T_{1}\big)\ ,

(3.58)

\displaystyle{L^{\prime}}_{-}(z)=\tfrac{1}{kz^{2}-{\rm h}}\Big[(k-{\rm h})\big(\cos\theta_{1}\,\partial_{-}\phi_{1}\,T_{3}+z(\partial_{-}\theta_{1}\,T_{2}+\sin\theta_{1}\,\partial_{-}\phi_{1}\,T_{1})\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-(z^{2}-1)\big(\sqrt{k\widetilde{k}}\,\cos\theta_{2}\,\partial_{-}\phi_{2}+k\,\partial_{-}\psi\big)\Big]\ ,

\displaystyle\widetilde{L}_{+}(z)=\tfrac{1}{\widetilde{k}z^{-2}-\widetilde{h}}\Big[(\widetilde{k}-\widetilde{\rm h})\big(-\cos\theta_{2}\,\partial_{-}\phi_{2}\,T_{3}+z^{-1}(-\partial_{+}\theta_{2}\,T_{2}+\sin\theta_{3}\,\partial_{+}\phi_{3}\,T_{1})\big)

(3.59)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\ +\sqrt{k\widetilde{k}}(z^{-2}-1)\big(\cos\theta_{1}\,\partial_{+}\phi_{1}+\partial_{+}\psi\big)\Big]\ ,

\displaystyle\widetilde{L}_{-}(z)=-\cos{\theta_{2}}\,\partial_{-}\phi_{2}\,T_{3}+z\big(-\partial_{-}\theta_{2}\,T_{2}+\sin\theta_{2}\,\partial_{-}\phi_{2}\,T_{1}\big)\ .

To simplify the expressions we followed
[
218
]
here in replacing
L_{\pm}
by its gauge transformed version

{L^{\prime}}_{\pm}={\rm w}^{-1}L_{\pm}{\rm w}+{\rm w}^{-1}\partial_{\pm}{\rm w}
, with
{\rm w}=\exp(-\psi T_{3})
.

At the special point

{\rm h}=k,\ \widetilde{\rm h}=\widetilde{k}=q^{2}k
, the model (
3.55
)
becomes the
SU(2)\times SU(2)/U(1)
case of the conformal GMM model with levels
k_{1}=k,\ k_{2}=-\widetilde{k}
.
It was pointed out in
[
227
]
that the
SU(2)\times SU(2)/U(1)
GMM model corresponds to the

T^{1,q}

metric and
a particular
B
-field, and its 2-loop conformality was explicitly checked (see also
[
234
]
).
The general GMM model has a current algebra symmetry
[
226
]
and is also integrable in the Lax connection sense
[
188
]
.
What we have shown above is that
it admits an integrable extension (
3.55
)
away from the conformal point
{\rm h}=k,\ \widetilde{\rm h}=\widetilde{k}
.

3.3.1
Stability under the 2-loop RG flow

Let us now show that the integrable

T^{1,q}
model (
3.55
) is stable under the 2-loop RG flow.

The general gauge invariant model (
3.46
) (with the
r=k

condition (
3.48
) relaxed) must be
stable under the RG with only
(r,{\rm h},\widetilde{\rm h})
running, due to its
H
gauge invariance and global
G_{L}\times G_{L}
symmetry. Even at the point
q=1
or
\widetilde{k}=k
where the two
“branches”
of gauge invariant theories (
3.46
),(
3.19
) intersect, the RG flow cannot ‘turn on’ the extra couplings

\rho_{12}
,
\rho_{21}
since this is forbidden by an extra global “center” symmetry
[
225
,
218
]
,

(g,\,\widetilde{g})\to(g\,z,\,\widetilde{g}),\ z\in{\rm Z}(H)\subset H
when
H
is abelian.
53
53

53

Note, however, that the center symmetry alone is not sufficient to explain the stability of the
T^{1,q}
model since it does not prevent
r
from running.

We shall see that the integrable
T^{1,q}
model, obtained by fixing
r=k
, is a “fixed line” of the RG flow.
Relaxing
r=k
has the effect of replacing
k\to r
in the metric,
54
54

54

It was recently argued by observing chaotic behaviour
[
263
]
that relaxing the
r=k
condition indeed destroys classical integrability, as least in the equal level case
q=1
(i.e.
\widetilde{k}=k
). This implies that, with
q=1
, the fixed line
r=k
and its parity conjugate
r=-k
are the only integrable models among the
\sigma
-models on the class of backgrounds (
3.60
).

with
k
still appearing in the
B
-field (cf. (
3.56
),(
3.57
)),
55
55

55

Rescaling
r\to r^{\prime}k
and
\psi\to{1\over\sqrt{k}}\psi^{\prime}
the background (
3.60
) can be put into the form
symmetric under

k\leftrightarrow\widetilde{k},\ {\rm h}\leftrightarrow\widetilde{\rm h}
:

\qquad\ ds^{2}=\tfrac{1}{4}r^{\prime}\,(d\psi^{\prime}+\sqrt{k}\cos{\theta_{1}}\,d\phi_{1}+\sqrt{\widetilde{k}}\cos{\theta_{2}}\,d\phi_{2})^{2}+\tfrac{1}{4}{\rm h}\,(d\theta_{1}^{2}+\sin^{2}{\theta_{1}}\,d\phi_{1}^{2})+\tfrac{1}{4}\widetilde{\rm h}\,(d\theta_{2}^{2}+\sin^{2}{\theta_{2}}\,d\phi_{2}^{2})\ ,

\qquad\ \ \ B=\tfrac{1}{4}\,(d\psi^{\prime}+\sqrt{k}\cos{\theta_{1}}\,d\phi_{1})\wedge(d\psi^{\prime}+\sqrt{\widetilde{k}}\cos{\theta_{2}}\,d\phi_{2})\ .

\displaystyle ds^{2}={}

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{4}r\,(d\psi+\cos{\theta_{1}}\,d\phi_{1}+q\cos{\theta_{2}}\,d\phi_{2})^{2}+\tfrac{1}{4}{\rm h}\,(d\theta_{1}^{2}+\sin^{2}{\theta_{1}}\,d\phi_{1}^{2})+\tfrac{1}{4}\widetilde{\rm h}\,(d\theta_{2}^{2}+\sin^{2}{\theta_{2}}\,d\phi_{2}^{2})\ ,

\displaystyle B={}

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{4}k\,(d\psi+\cos{\theta_{1}}\,d\phi_{1})\wedge(d\psi+q\cos{\theta_{2}}\,d\phi_{2})\ ,\qquad\ \ q=\sqrt{\tfrac{\widetilde{k}}{k}}\ .

(3.60)

The corresponding
2-loop
\beta
-functions in the
{\rm G}{\rm B}
scheme following from (
1.9
) are
(
k,\widetilde{k}
do not run)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}r=2(r^{2}-k^{2})\Big[\tfrac{1}{{\rm h}^{2}}+\tfrac{\widetilde{k}}{k}\tfrac{1}{{\widetilde{\rm h}}^{2}}+\tfrac{r^{2}-3k^{2}}{r}\big(\,\tfrac{1}{{\rm h}^{4}}+\tfrac{{\widetilde{k}}^{2}}{k^{2}}\tfrac{1}{\widetilde{\rm h}^{4}}\big)\Big]\ ,

(3.61)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}{\rm h}=4\big[1-\tfrac{r}{2{\rm h}}(1+\tfrac{k^{2}}{r^{2}})\big]+\tfrac{2}{{\rm h}^{3}r^{2}}\Big[8{\rm h}^{2}r^{2}-8{\rm h}k^{2}r-12{\rm h}r^{3}+4r^{2}k^{2}+3k^{4}+5r^{4}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\tfrac{\widetilde{k}}{k}\tfrac{{{\rm h}}^{2}}{\widetilde{\rm h}^{2}}(r^{2}-k^{2})(3r^{2}-k^{2})\Big]\ ,

(3.62)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}{\widetilde{\rm h}}=4\big[1-\tfrac{\widetilde{k}}{k}\tfrac{r}{{2\widetilde{\rm h}}}(1+\tfrac{k^{2}}{r^{2}})\big]+\tfrac{2}{{\widetilde{\rm h}}^{3}r^{2}}\Big[8{\widetilde{\rm h}}^{2}r^{2}-8{\widetilde{\rm h}}{\widetilde{k}}kr-12\tfrac{\widetilde{k}}{k}{\widetilde{\rm h}}r^{3}+4r^{2}{\widetilde{k}}^{2}+3{\widetilde{k}}^{2}k^{2}+5\tfrac{{\widetilde{k}}^{2}}{k^{2}}r^{4}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\tfrac{\widetilde{k}}{k}\tfrac{{\widetilde{\rm h}}^{2}}{{\rm h}^{2}}(r^{2}-k^{2})(3r^{2}-k^{2})\Big]\ .

(3.63)

Thus

r=\pm k
are fixed lines
of
(
3.61
),
both at 1-loop and 2-loop order.
The couplings
(r,{\rm h},{\widetilde{\rm h}})
grow linearly with
\tau\to\infty
in the UV (reflecting asymptotic freedom),
while they decrease to the GMM fixed point
(r,{\rm h},{\widetilde{\rm h}})=(k,k,\widetilde{k})
in the IR. In fact, as argued in
[
226
]
, the GMM model on
G\times G^{\prime}/H
is an exact CFT,
assuming at least one of the cosets
G/H
or
G^{\prime}/H
is a symmetric space (as is indeed the case for

SU(2)\times SU(2)/U(1)
).

Specialising to the fixed line
r=k
of (
3.61
),
the expressions (
3.62
),(
3.63
) simplify, giving the
2-loop
\beta
-functions of the integrable
T^{1,q}
model (
3.49
),(
3.55
),

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}{\rm h}=4\big(1-\tfrac{k}{{\rm h}}\big)\Big[1+\tfrac{4}{{\rm h}}\big(1-\tfrac{3\,k}{2\,{\rm h}}\big)\Big]\ ,\qquad\qquad\frac{d}{d\tau}\widetilde{h}=4\big(1-\tfrac{\widetilde{k}}{\widetilde{\rm h}}\big)\Big[1+\tfrac{4}{\widetilde{\rm h}}\big(1-\tfrac{3\,\widetilde{k}}{2\,\widetilde{\rm h}}\big)\Big]\ .

(3.64)

These are a natural generalization of the
\beta
-functions for the
T^{1,1}
model in (
3.45
) to the case of
\widetilde{k}\not=k
.
Like in (
3.45
), the RG evolution of
{\rm h}
and
\widetilde{\rm h}
happens to be decoupled (while
this is not the case for
r\not=k

in (
3.62
),(
3.63
)).

We note that, in addition to the
r=k
case of the
T^{1,q}
model,
the
\sigma
-model corresponding to (
3.60
) admits
another
integrable limit,

\widetilde{k}=0
.
In this case it factorizes into
a squashed
S^{3}
with WZ term and a round
S^{2}
.
The
\beta
-functions
(
3.61
) and (
3.62
)
become those of this
squashed
S^{3}
WZ-model
in

[
224
]
(for 1-loop
\beta
-functions see
[
237
,
106
]
)
56
56

56

The relation to the
notation used in
[
224
]
is

\eta=k{\rm h}^{-1},\ \lambda^{2}={2\pi}{\rm h}^{-1}
,
\kappa=1-r{\rm h}^{-1}
.
and the

\beta
-function (
3.63
) for the coefficient
\widetilde{\rm h}
becomes that of the
S^{2}={SU(2)\over U(1)}

\sigma
-model (eq. (
1.32
) with
{{\rm c}_{{}_{G}}}=2,\ {{\rm c}_{{}_{H}}}=0
).
Taking further limits, the
\beta
-functions (
3.61
),(
3.62
),(
3.63
) agree with
other previously known expressions:

(i)

Setting
\widetilde{k}=0

and
r={\rm h}
, we get from (
3.60
)
the direct sum of the PCM
k
(round
S^{3}
with a WZ term)
and the
S^{2}

\sigma
-model. In this case (
3.61
),(
3.62
) are indeed equivalent to the
\beta
-function
of PCM
k
, i.e. (
3.3
) with
{{\rm c}_{{}_{G}}}=2
.

(ii)

Setting
\widetilde{k}=0
(i.e.
q=0
)
and then
k=0
, we
instead
get the direct sum of a squashed
S^{3}
(with no WZ term) and a round
S^{2}
.
The
\beta
-functions for
r
and
{\rm h}
agree with those of the “squashed” PCM in
[
3
]

(with
G=SU(2)

and the “squashing” parameter
\varepsilon={r\over{\rm h}}
):

\qquad\frac{d}{d\tau}r={2r^{2}}{{\rm h}^{-2}}+{r^{3}}{{\rm h}^{-4}}\ ,\qquad\frac{d}{d\tau}{\rm h}=4\big(1-\tfrac{1}{2}r{\rm h}^{-1}\big)+{2}{{\rm h}^{-3}}\big(8{\rm h}^{2}-12{\rm h}r+5r^{2}\big)\ .

3.3.2
Covariance under T-duality

One can argue that the RG stability of the integrable
T^{1,q}
model (
3.55
), i.e. the presence of the fixed line
r=k
of (
3.61
), is related to
its property of being self-dual under T-duality in the isometric
\psi
-direction.
To see this, let us write the Lagrangian (
3.55
)
in the following form
57
57

57

Note that
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}

becomes simply quadratic in the fields
at the GMM point
{\rm h}=k,\ \widetilde{\rm h}=\widetilde{k}=q^{2}k
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\tfrac{1}{4}k\,\Big[(\partial_{+}\psi+U_{+})(\partial_{-}\psi+V_{-})-\tfrac{1}{2}U_{+}V_{-}\Big]+\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\ ,

(3.65)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad U_{\pm}\equiv 2\cos{\theta_{1}}\,\partial_{\pm}\phi_{1}\ ,\qquad\qquad V_{\pm}\equiv 2q\cos{\theta_{2}}\,\partial_{\pm}\phi_{2}\ ,

(3.66)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\equiv\tfrac{1}{4}{\rm h}\Big[\partial_{+}\theta_{1}\partial_{-}\theta_{1}+(\sin^{2}{\theta_{1}}+\tfrac{k}{{\rm h}}\cos^{2}{\theta_{1}})\partial_{+}\phi_{1}\partial_{-}\phi_{1}\Big]

(3.67)

\displaystyle\qquad+\tfrac{1}{4}\widetilde{\rm h}\Big[\partial_{+}\theta_{2}\partial_{-}\theta_{2}+(\sin^{2}{\theta_{2}}+\tfrac{kq^{2}}{\widetilde{\rm h}}\cos^{2}{\theta_{2}})\,\partial_{+}\phi_{2}\partial_{-}\phi_{2}\Big]\ .

Starting from the interpolating Lagrangian (obtained by
\partial_{\pm}\psi\to A_{\pm}
and adding the condition
{\partial_{+}A_{-}-\partial_{-}A_{+}=0}
with a Lagrange multiplier
\bar{\psi}
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\rm int}=\tfrac{1}{4}k\Big[(A_{+}+U_{+})(A_{-}+V_{-})-\tfrac{1}{2}U_{+}V_{-}-{\bar{\psi}}(\partial_{+}A_{-}-\partial_{-}A_{+})\Big]+\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\ ,

(3.68)

and integrating out
A_{\pm}
, we obtain the following T-dual Lagrangian

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{L}}=\tfrac{1}{4}k\Big[(\partial_{+}\bar{\psi}+U_{+})(\partial_{-}\bar{\psi}-V_{-})+\tfrac{1}{2}U_{+}V_{-}\Big]+\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\ .

(3.69)

This is the same as
the original theory
(
3.65
),
with
\psi\to\bar{\psi}
and a coordinate redefinition
\phi_{2}\to-\phi_{2}
(under which
V_{-}\to-V_{-}
).

To appreciate the special structure of
(
3.65
),
let us relax the condition
r=k
and go back to the
general non-integrable model (
3.46
)
corresponding to the background (
3.60
).
Using again the notation (
3.66
),
we find the following generalization of (
3.65
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=

\displaystyle\tfrac{r+k}{8}\Big[(\partial_{+}\psi+U_{+})(\partial_{-}\psi+V_{-})-\tfrac{1}{2}U_{+}V_{-}\Big]

(3.70)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\tfrac{r-k}{8}\Big[(\partial_{-}\psi+U_{-})(\partial_{+}\psi+V_{+})-\tfrac{1}{2}U_{-}V_{+}\Big]+\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\ .

Applying the T-duality
\psi\to\bar{\psi}
to (
3.70
) we get, instead of (
3.69
),

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{L}}={}

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{4}r\Big[\Big(\partial_{+}\bar{\psi}+\tfrac{r+k}{2r}U_{+}+\tfrac{r-k}{2r}V_{+}\Big)\Big(\partial_{-}\bar{\psi}-\tfrac{r-k}{2r}U_{-}-\tfrac{r+k}{2r}V_{-}\Big)

(3.71)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\tfrac{r+k}{4r}U_{+}V_{-}+\tfrac{r-k}{4r}U_{-}V_{+}\Big]+\widehat{\mathcal{L}}\ .

For general values of
r
and
k
,
(
3.71
) is different from (
3.70
); the only self-dual theory where (
3.70
) and (
3.71
) coincide is the
T^{1,q}
model (
3.65
) corresponding to
r=k
(or its parity-conjugate
r=-k
).

By the standard path integral argument, the T-dual models (
3.70
),(
3.71
) should be quantum-equivalent. In general, the
T-duality transformation rules are
subject to quantum
\alpha^{\prime}
corrections
[
109
,
111
,
203
]

that may be attributed to extra finite counterterms resulting from
integration over the auxiliary gauge field
A_{\pm}
(see eq. (
2.42
) above). If
the kinetic term of the isometric coordinate is non-trivial, i.e. the
term
quadratic in
A_{\pm}

is
A_{+}M(x)A_{-}
, then the leading quantum correction to the effective Lagrangian
is represented by the term
\Delta\mathcal{L}\sim\alpha^{\prime}\,\partial_{+}\log M\,\partial_{-}\log M
(as well as a shift of the dilaton
[
129
,
127
]
).
In the case of (
3.70
) we have
M=1
and thus this correction is absent.

Since the model (
3.70
) is stable
under the RG
due to its symmetries,
with the 3 running couplings
r,{\rm h},\widetilde{\rm h}
,
its T-dual (
3.71
) must also be stable.
Given that the self-dual points
{r=\pm k}
are part of both RG-stable families
(
3.70
) and (
3.71
) then they must also
remain in both families after the renormalization. Since they are the
only
such self-dual models, then
r=\pm k
must be fixed lines of the RG flow.
This was indeed confirmed above
by the explicit computation of the
\beta
-functions leading to
(
3.61
).

3.4
Discussion

In this chapter we discussed further
instances
of a close connection between
the conditions of integrability and a consistent restriction of the RG flow to a subspace of couplings.

We have found the 2-loop
\beta
-functions of the 6-parameter
G\times G
model (
3.5
)
and have shown that its integrability condition (
3.6
) is automatically preserved by the RG flow.
In
[
220
]
, the 1-loop
\beta
-functions for this integrable
model were written in a universal form in terms of the twist function, revealing a hidden simplicity.
It would be interesting to see if the complicated expressions we have found
for the 2-loop
\beta
-functions (see Appendix
D.1
)
simplify on the
“integrable surface”
once expressed in terms of the
twist function. Indeed, one may try to extend the method of
[
220
]
from 1-loop to 2-loop order, i.e. computing the Riemann tensor and thus the 2-loop
\beta
-function in terms of the twist function.
It would also be interesting to investigate the connection
to the “doubled” approach of
[
238
]
which studied the model (
3.5
),(
3.6
) with
additional integrable
\eta
- or
\lambda
-deformation parameters turned on.

We also studied the 6-parameter gauged
G\times G/H
model (
3.16
),(
3.19
),
which is integrable under the conditions (
3.25
).
The latter were found
to be stable under the 1-loop RG flow, but generally require a certain deformation
(i.e. the addition of finite counterterms) at the 2-loop level
to preserve renormalizability.

There are still some special cases in which integrable
G\times G/H
models
are automatically stable under the 2-loop RG flow. One simple example is the
T^{1,1}
model of
[
218
]
.
We also constructed a new class of integrable
G\times G/H
models (
3.49
) in the case when the subgroup
H

is abelian (see (
3.46
),(
3.48
),(
3.49
)).
For
G=SU(2)
and
H=U(1)
, this led to
an integrable
T^{1,q}

model generalizing the
T^{1,1}
model,
which
we also found to be stable under the 2-loop RG flow
for general values of the parameter
q
.
This model
may be interpreted as
an integrable deformation of the
conformal
GMM model
with unequal levels
[
226
]
.
An open question is whether the integrable
T^{1,q}
model admits a description in terms of affine Gaudin models (like the
T^{1,1}
case) or if it is outside of that formalism.

Since the GMM model admits a
G\times G^{\prime}/H
generalization (with
G\neq G^{\prime}
),
this raises the question of whether there is a larger class of integrable
G\times G^{\prime}/H

models that flow to
such
conformal
theories. One obvious possibility is to consider some analytic continuations, e.g.,
take
G^{\prime}
to be a different real form of the complexification of
G
.
For example, the counterpart of the
SU(2)\times SU(2)/U(1)
model
would be
SL(2,\mathbb{R})\times SU(2)/U(1)
.

Another interesting open question is why the integrable
G\times G
model was automatically stable under the 2-loop RG flow, while the gauged
G\times G/H
analog requires a non-trivial quantum deformation of the “integrable surface” in the space of couplings. This is analogous to WZW models, which are conformal without any
\alpha^{\prime}
-corrections, and gauged WZW models, which require a non-trivial tower of
\alpha^{\prime}
corrections (e.g. in eq. (
2.4
)) to remain conformal beyond 1-loop.
One possible resolution, which we shall consider below in Section
4.5
, is to reformulate the
G\times G/H
model as a theory with a gauge field and redefine the gauge field in terms of
H
-valued scalars; as for the “tripled” formulation of the
\lambda
-model in Chapter
4
, such a reformulation could
restore higher-loop renormalizability without needing any quantum corrections to the geometry.

Chapter 4
“Tripled” formulation of the
\lambda
-model

We have been studying a close link between
renormalizablility,
or stability under the RG flow,
and
integrability in 2d
\sigma
-models.
Having previously been observed

[
101
,
102
,
103
,
104
,
105
,
108
]
only at the 1-loop (Ricci flow) level, it
is important to study this relation also at higher loop orders.

This question of higher loops was addressed above in Chapters
2
and
3
,
where we found that, starting from 2 loops,
renormalizability typically requires a specific deformation of the classical target space
geometry, which may be interpreted as the result of adding
finite
local counterterms. In Chapter
2
, we considered the simplest examples of
integrable
\eta
- and
\lambda
-deformed bosonic
\sigma
-models with 2- and 3-dimensional target spaces.
In Chapter
3
, we looked at another class of integrable
\sigma
-models with target spaces
G\times G
and
G\times G/H
.

Here we shall concentrate on the
\lambda
-deformation
[
138
,
139
]
and address the
general case
where the model is based on an arbitrary
group

G
or symmetric space
G/H
. Its Lagrangian is (repeating eq. (
1.78
) for clarity)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=k\,\Big(\mathcal{L}_{G}(g)+{\rm Tr}\big[J_{+}A_{-}-A_{+}K_{-}+g^{-1}A_{+}gA_{-}-A_{+}A_{-}-(\lambda^{-1}-1)A_{+}PA_{-}\big]\Big),

(4.1)

\displaystyle J=g^{-1}dg\ ,\quad K=dg\,g^{-1}\ ,

\displaystyle P=\begin{cases}1\ ,\quad\ \ \ \ \ \text{group space }G\\
P_{G/H}\ ,\quad\text{symmetric space }G/H\ ,\end{cases}

(4.2)

where
\mathcal{L}_{G}=\mathcal{L}_{\rm PCM}+\mathcal{L}_{\rm WZ}
is the WZW Lagrangian,
g\in G
,
A_{\pm}\in{\rm Lie}(G)
and
P_{G/H}
is the projector onto the orthogonal complement of
{\rm Lie}(H)
in
{\rm Lie}(G)
.
Instead of
\lambda
it is often convenient to use
a redefined parameter,

{\kappa}=\frac{1-\lambda}{1+\lambda}\ .

(4.3)

The limit
\lambda\to 0
(or
{\kappa}\to 1
)
yields the conformal WZW model on
G
(or gauged WZW model on
G/H
)
with level
k
,
which should thus be a fixed point of the RG flow.
The
\lambda
-model’s
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
transformation (
1.80
) then implies that the opposite limit
\lambda\to\infty
(or
{\kappa}\to-1
) should also be a fixed point.
Indeed, in the group case the
\lambda\to\infty
limit of (
4.1
) is conformal:
it is the
G/G
chiral gauged WZW model
[
167
]
, which, on
integrating out
A_{\pm}
, gives the
G
WZW model at level
-k
.
Similarly, in the coset case we find in this limit the
G/H
gauged WZW model at level
-k
.

Integrating out the 2d gauge field
A_{\pm}
in (
4.1
), i.e. reducing the model to the standard (or “physical”) configuration space,
one finds a
\sigma
-model with parameters
k
and
\lambda
(see eq. (
2.35
)).
As we found in Chapter
2
, to preserve renormalizability of the
\lambda
-model
at the 2-loop level,
one must make a non-trivial modification of
the classical target space geometry.

In this chapter our central observation will be that,
before
integrating out
A_{\pm}
,
the
\lambda
-model (
4.1
) is renormalizable without any deformation.
As was done in the context of gWZW models
[
116
,
115
,
164
,
165
]
, we shall change variables from
A_{\pm}
to scalar fields
h,\bar{h}\in G
as

\displaystyle A_{+}=h\,\partial_{+}h^{-1},\ A_{-}=\bar{h}\,\partial_{+}\bar{h}^{-1}\ ,

(4.4)

resulting in a
\sigma
-model on the extended or “tripled” (
G\times G\times G
) configuration space
(g,h,\bar{h})
.
It may be interpreted as the sum of two decoupled parts: (i) a WZW model on
G
and (ii) a deformation of a
WZW model on
G\times G
by a particular left-right current interaction.
This special decoupling is expected to be a manifestation of integrability and, as a result,
the
manifest
global symmetries become sufficient to protect
the model
under the RG flow
without the need for any additional finite counterterms.

The rest of the chapter is organized as follows. In Section
4.1
we shall review the previous application of the change of variables (
4.4
) to gauged and chiral gauged WZW models.

In Sections
4.2
and
4.3
, we consider the
\lambda
-models, respectively based on groups and symmetric spaces, on the configuration space
(g,A_{+},A_{-})
, i.e. with the gauge field unintegrated.
By using the change of variables (
4.4
) to write the
\lambda
-models as certain “tripled”
\sigma
-models
with extra manifest symmetries, we shall argue that they are automatically renormalizable to all orders and explicitly compute their 2-loop
\beta
-functions.
In the
non-abelian dual (NAD)
limit (
2.72
), these will correctly reproduce the (scheme-invariant) 2-loop
\beta
-functions of the PCM and symmetric space
\sigma
-model, suggesting that the interpolating models for NAD are also automatically renormalizable without needing corrections.

In Section
4.4
we consider some simple examples of the models obtained in the “tripled” formulation of the
\lambda
-models based on
SU(2)\over U(1)
and
SU(2)
. In Section
4.5
we construct analogous “tripled” or “extended” space formulations of some other integrable
\sigma
-models (a variant of the
\lambda
-model built on
G^{N}

[
65
]
and the
G\times G/H
models of
[
218
]
).
Some concluding remarks are made in Section
4.6
.

This chapter is based on the paper
[
3
]
and we closely follow the presentation there.

4.1
Review of gWZW model

First let us review the original use of the change of variables (
4.4
) in the context of the gauged WZW models
[
116
,
115
,
164
,
165
]
. The gauged WZW model is closely related to the
\lambda
-model (
4.1
) but with the gauge field being valued in some subalgebra
{\rm Lie}(F)\subset{\rm Lie}(G)
(see Section
1.4.8
),

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{L}=k\,\Big(\mathcal{L}_{G}(g)+{\rm Tr}\big[J_{+}A_{-}-A_{+}K_{-}+g^{-1}A_{+}gA_{-}-A_{+}A_{-}]\Big)\ ,\\
&g\in G\ ,\ \ A_{\pm}\in{\rm Lie}(F)\ .\end{aligned}

(4.5)

Changing variables
(A_{+},A_{-})\to(h,\bar{h})
as in (
4.4
), now with with
h,\bar{h}\in F
, and using the Polyakov-Wiegmann identity
58
58

58

The Polyakov-Wiegmann identity is a multiplicative identity for the WZW Lagrangian
[
40
]
,

\qquad\mathcal{L}_{G}(uv)=\mathcal{L}_{G}(u)+\mathcal{L}_{G}(v)-{\rm Tr}[u^{-1}\partial_{+}u\,\partial_{-}vv^{-1}]\ .

[
40
]
,
the Lagrangian (
4.5
) can be written as a combination of WZW models,

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}&=k\Big(\mathcal{L}_{G}(h^{-1}g\bar{h})-\mathcal{L}_{F}(h^{-1}\bar{h})\Big)\ .\end{split}

(4.6)

The change of variables
in (
4.4
) results in a Jacobian contributing to the action as

[
40
,
186
]

\displaystyle\Delta\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=-2c_{{}_{F}}\Big(\mathcal{L}_{F}(h^{-1}\bar{h})+{\rm q}{\rm Tr}[A_{+}A_{-}]\Big)

(4.7)

\displaystyle=-2c_{{}_{F}}\Big(\mathcal{L}_{F}(h^{-1}\bar{h})+{\rm q}{\rm Tr}[\partial_{+}h\,h^{-1}\,\partial_{-}\bar{h}\,\bar{h}^{-1}]\Big)\ ,

(4.8)

where
c_{{}_{F}}
is the dual Coxeter number
59
59

59

More precisely,
c_{{}_{F}}
denotes here a rescaled version of the dual Coxeter number of
F
by an index factor relating to the embedding of
F
into
G
(see discussion in the final paragraph of App.
A
for subgroups
H\subset G
). If
G
and
F
are both classical groups with the same index for their fundamental representation (e.g. both
A
-type) then this index factor is trivial and
c_{{}_{F}}
is the dual Coxeter number of
F
.
of
F
and
an arbitrary coefficient
{\rm q}
parametrizes the ambiguity of adding a finite local counterterm
{\rm Tr}[A_{+}A_{-}]
.
Combining (
4.8
) and the classical Lagrangian (
4.6
) gives

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=k\mathcal{L}_{G}(h^{-1}g\bar{h})-(k+2c_{{}_{F}})\mathcal{L}_{F}(h^{-1}\bar{h})-2{\rm q}c_{{}_{F}}\big[\mathcal{L}_{F}(h^{-1}\bar{h})-\mathcal{L}_{F}(h^{-1})-\mathcal{L}_{F}(\bar{h})\big]\ .\

(4.9)

Now one can choose the coefficient
q=0
to obtain the sum of two decoupled WZW models (e.g. after defining
\tilde{g}=h^{-1}gh
,
\tilde{h}=h^{-1}\bar{h}
)

\displaystyle L=k\mathcal{L}_{G}(h^{-1}g\bar{h})-(k+2c_{{}_{F}})\mathcal{L}_{F}(h^{-1}\bar{h})

(4.10)

Expressing the gWZW model as this sum of decoupled WZW models makes manifest its properties of (i) exact conformal invariance and (ii) gauge invariance under
g\to ugu^{-1}
,
h\to uh
,
\bar{h}\to u\bar{h}
.

The exact form of the gWZW metric and
{\rm B}
-field can be derived
[
115
]
from (
4.10
) (agreeing with
[
113
,
114
]
) by considering the quantum effective action. The WZW model’s effective action is just the classical one with a shifted level (respectively
k\to k+c_{{}_{G}}
or
k\to k+c_{{}_{F}}
), leading to

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathcal{L}_{\rm eff}{}&{}=(k+c_{{}_{G}})\mathcal{L}_{G}(h^{-1}g\bar{h})+[-(k+2c_{{}_{F}})+c_{{}_{F}}]\mathcal{L}_{F}(h^{-1}\bar{h})\\
&{}=(k+c_{{}_{G}})\mathcal{L}_{G}(h^{-1}g\bar{h})-(k+c_{{}_{F}})\mathcal{L}_{F}(h^{-1}\bar{h})\ .\end{aligned}

(4.11)

Transforming back to
A_{\pm}
, the resulting theory is not local but the “local part” gives a prescription for a corrected version of (
4.5
) in which
A_{\pm}
can be integrated out
classically
to get an exactly conformal
\sigma
-model (of which the
SL(2,R)/U(1)
example is given in (
2.4
)).

Now let us briefly consider the related
G/F

chiral gauged WZW model
(cWZW)
[
167
]
, for which a similar argument was carried out in
[
164
]
,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{L}=k\,\Big(\mathcal{L}_{G}(g)+{\rm Tr}\big[J_{+}A_{-}-A_{+}K_{-}+g^{-1}A_{+}gA_{-}]\Big)\ ,\\
&g\in G\ ,\ \ A_{\pm}\in{\rm Lie}(F)\ .\end{aligned}

(4.12)

This theory differs from the gWZW model (
4.5
) by the addition of
k\,{\rm Tr}[A_{+}A_{-}]
. Again changing variables as in (
4.4
), the extra classical term in (
4.12
) is proportional to the ambiguous local term with coefficient
{\rm q}
in (
4.7
). Thus the result for the cWZW model is obtained by replacing
-2{\rm q}c_{{}_{F}}\to k-2{\rm q}c_{{}_{F}}
in (
4.9
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=k\mathcal{L}_{G}(h^{-1}g\bar{h})-(k+2c_{{}_{F}})\mathcal{L}_{F}(h^{-1}\bar{h})+(k-2{\rm q}c_{{}_{F}})\big[\mathcal{L}_{F}(h^{-1}\bar{h})-\mathcal{L}_{F}(h^{-1})-\mathcal{L}_{F}(\bar{h})\big]\ .\

(4.13)

Again in this case, there is a choice
{\rm q}=-1
that leads to cancellations and gives a sum of decoupled WZW models
[
164
]
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=k\mathcal{L}_{G}(h^{-1}g\bar{h})+(k+2c_{{}_{F}})\big[\mathcal{L}_{F}(h^{-1})+\mathcal{L}_{F}(\bar{h})\big]\ ,

(4.14)

illustrating that the cWZW model is also exactly conformal.

4.2
“Tripled”
\lambda
-model based on a group

Now let us turn to the
\lambda
-model based on a group
G
(putting
P=1
in eq. (
4.1
)),

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=k\,\Big(\mathcal{L}_{G}(g)+{\rm Tr}\big[J_{+}A_{-}-A_{+}K_{-}+g^{-1}A_{+}gA_{-}-\lambda^{-1}A_{+}A_{-}\big]\Big),

(4.15)

\displaystyle g\in G\ ,\quad A_{\pm}\in{\rm Lie}(G)\ .

This model interpolates between the gWZW model (
4.5
) (
\lambda=1
) and the cWZW model (
4.12
) (
\lambda=\infty
) in the particular case
F=G
. Hence, upon making the same change of variables
(A_{+},A_{-})\to(h,\bar{h})
as in (
4.4
), the resulting theory is just the weighted average of the gWZW and cWZW results (
4.9
),(
4.13
) above,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathcal{L}=&k\,\mathcal{L}_{G}(h^{-1}g\bar{h})-(k+2c_{{}_{G}})\mathcal{L}_{G}(h^{-1}\bar{h})\\
&\qquad\qquad-(k(\lambda^{-1}-1)+2{\rm q}c_{{}_{G}})\big[\mathcal{L}_{G}(h^{-1}\bar{h})-\mathcal{L}_{G}(h^{-1})-\mathcal{L}_{G}(\bar{h})\big]\ ,\ \end{aligned}

(4.16)

with the new scalar fields
h,\bar{h}
now valued in the full group
G
. This expression includes the Jacobian contribution (
4.8
), with the coefficient
{\rm q}
parametrizing the local term ambiguity. In this case
{\rm q}
just specifies a particular redefinition of the coupling
\lambda
, so the choice of
{\rm q}
is not important and we shall pick
{\rm q}=-1
(as in the cWZW case above).

Again using the Polyakov-Wiegmann identity (see footnote
58
), we may rewrite (
4.16
) as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=k\mathcal{L}_{G}(\tilde{g})+\mathcal{L}^{\prime}({\hat{h}},\bar{h})\ ,\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\tilde{g}\equiv h^{-1}g\bar{h}\in G\ ,\qquad{\hat{h}}\equiv h^{-1}\in G\ ,

(4.17)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\prime}=-(k+2c_{{}_{G}})\big[\mathcal{L}_{G}({\hat{h}})+\mathcal{L}_{G}(\bar{h})\big]+k\lambda^{-1}{\rm Tr}[{\hat{h}}^{-1}\partial_{+}{\hat{h}}\,\partial_{-}\bar{h}\bar{h}^{-1}]\ .

(4.18)

This is a
\sigma
-model with fields valued in the extended configuration space
(\tilde{g},{\hat{h}},\bar{h})\in G\times G\times G
.
As for the gauged WZW models considered above,
the first WZW term
k\mathcal{L}_{G}(\tilde{g})
still decouples from the other fields in (
4.17
). This decoupling is a special property expected to be associated with integrability: indeed if one deformed the
\lambda
-model (
4.15
) in an arbitrary way preserving its global symmetry but breaking integrability, the decoupling would be lost.
60
60

60

I thank N. Gromov for a discussion of this point.
Since the decoupled WZW term is conformal on its own, then its coefficient
k
will not run, and the RG flow of
\lambda
is determined by the other “truncated” part
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
.

Redefining the couplings as

\displaystyle{\tilde{k}}\equiv k+2c_{{}_{G}}\ ,\qquad\qquad{\tilde{\lambda}}\equiv\frac{k}{{\tilde{k}}}\lambda^{-1}\ ,

(4.19)

then the truncated part,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\prime}({\hat{h}},\bar{h})=-{\tilde{k}}\Big(\mathcal{L}_{G}({\hat{h}})+\mathcal{L}_{G}(\bar{h})-{\tilde{\lambda}}{\rm Tr}[{\hat{h}}^{-1}\partial_{+}{\hat{h}}\,\partial_{-}\bar{h}\bar{h}^{-1}]\Big)\ ,\qquad\quad{\hat{h}}\equiv h^{-1},\ \bar{h}\in G\ ,

(4.20)

may be interpreted as the sum of
two WZW models for the group
G

with the same level
-{\tilde{k}}

perturbed by a current-current interaction.
Classical integrability of the model (
4.20
) (implying integrability of the group space
\lambda
-model)
was first shown in
[
188
]
(see also
[
189
]
).
In fact (
4.20
) is a particular instance of the integrable
G\times G
models
[
217
]
in (
3.5
),(
3.6
) above, with the couplings and fields related by

\displaystyle(k_{1},k_{2},s,u,t,b)=(-{\tilde{k}},+{\tilde{k}},-{\tilde{k}},-{\tilde{k}},{\tilde{k}}{\tilde{\lambda}}^{-1},{\tilde{k}}{\tilde{\lambda}}^{-1})\ ,\qquad(g^{(1)},g^{(2)})=({\hat{h}},\bar{h}^{-1})\ .

(4.21)

At the value
\widetilde{\lambda}=1
of the coupling, the model (
4.20
) becomes a special case (
G\times G/G
with equal levels) of the conformal GMM model
[
187
]
.
A generalization of the model (
4.20
) was also considered in

[
230
,
231
]
,
where it was interpreted as a special case of a “doubly
\lambda
-deformed”
\sigma
-model.
Our path-integral relation
between the
\lambda
-model (
4.1
) and the truncated model (
4.20
)
should be equivalent (at least at the classical and 1-loop level)
to the canonical equivalence between the doubly
\lambda
-deformed model
and two copies of the
\lambda
-model found in
[
191
]

(upon setting one of the two
\lambda
-parameters to zero).
The leading order in
1/k
(1-loop) renormalization of similar models
was also studied earlier in
[
193
,
194
]
.

Our central observation is that the “truncated” model (
4.20
)
is automatically renormalizable to all orders with only
{\tilde{\lambda}}
(or equivalently
\lambda
) running.
This is because, thanks to the decoupling of the first WZW term in (
4.17
), the truncated model is fully protected under the RG flow by its manifest symmetries:
the global
G
symmetry (present in the usual formulation (
4.15
) of the
\lambda
-model),

\displaystyle{\hat{h}}\to{\hat{h}}\,g_{0}\ ,\qquad\bar{h}\to g_{0}^{-1}\,\bar{h}\ ,\qquad\qquad g_{0}\in G\ ,

(4.22)

and chiral gauge symmetries (cf. the symmetry (
1.61
) of the WZW model),

\displaystyle{\hat{h}}\to u(\sigma^{-})\,{\hat{h}}\ ,\qquad\bar{h}\to\bar{h}\,w(\sigma^{+})\ ,\qquad\qquad u,w\in G\ ,

(4.23)

arising ubiquitously from the change of variables (
4.4
). These symmetries uniquely protect the structure of (
4.20
), with
{\tilde{\lambda}}
and
{\tilde{k}}
being the only free parameters.
In particular, the chiral symmetries (
4.23
