5.2
RG flow from condition of integrability of time-dependent theory

As we have found above on several examples,
if the couplings of an integrable
\sigma
-model are promoted to functions of time that
solve the 1-loop RG equations,
h_{\alpha}\to h_{\alpha}(\tau),\ \partial_{\tau}h_{\alpha}=\beta_{\alpha}(h)
,
then the Lax connection of the original model
\mathcal{L}(h_{\alpha})
admits a natural generalization
to a classical Lax connection for the time-dependent model
\mathcal{L}(h_{\alpha}(\tau))
.
Here we shall argue that the converse is also true:
demanding the existence of a Lax pair for the time-dependent theory implies that
h_{\alpha}(\tau)
must solve
the 1-loop RG equations.

It is useful to start with a more general theory with local couplings
72
72

72

We generally expect integrable models to have a finite number of couplings
h_{\alpha}
.
A natural way to identify these couplings is
as the parameters that run under the RG flow.

h_{\alpha}(\tau,\sigma)
depending on both
\tau
and
\sigma
, i.e.

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{L}}=\mathcal{L}(h_{\alpha}(\tau,\sigma))\ ,

(5.25)

and demand the existence of a Lax representation for this
theory.
For the examples in Table
1
, the original Lax connection
takes a particular form
(which is generic to many integrable
\sigma
-models built on groups
G
or symmetric spaces
G/H
):

\displaystyle\hskip-71.13188ptG\ :\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle L_{\pm}=\tfrac{1}{2}(1+z^{\pm 1})\mathcal{A}_{\pm}\ ,

(5.26)

\displaystyle\hskip-71.13188ptG/H\ :\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle L_{\pm}=\mathcal{B}_{\pm}+z^{\pm 1}\mathcal{P}_{\pm}\ .

(5.27)

Here the connection components
\mathcal{A}_{\pm}\in\operatorname{Lie}(G)
,

\mathcal{B}_{\pm}\in\operatorname{Lie}(H)
,
\mathcal{P}_{\pm}\in\operatorname{Lie}(G)/\operatorname{Lie}(H)
depend implicitly on the fields and their derivatives
and the couplings
h_{\alpha}
(e.g. for PCM,
\mathcal{A}_{\pm}=J_{\pm}
, cf. (
5.20
)), while the dependence on the
spectral parameter
z
is indicated explicitly.
Let us now take the following natural ansatz for the Lax connection
\widehat{L}_{\pm}

corresponding to the
(\tau,\sigma)
-dependent theory (
5.25
)

\displaystyle G\ :\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\widehat{L}_{\pm}=p_{\pm}(w;\tau,\sigma)\,\mathcal{A}_{\pm}\ ,

(5.28)

\displaystyle G/H\ :\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\widehat{L}_{\pm}=q_{\pm}(w;\tau,\sigma)\,\mathcal{B}_{\pm}+r_{\pm}(w;\tau,\sigma)\,\mathcal{P}_{\pm}\ ,

(5.29)

where
p_{\pm},q_{\pm},r_{\pm}
are some functions, and
\mathcal{A}_{\pm}
,
\mathcal{B}_{\pm}
,
\mathcal{P}_{\pm}
are assumed to take the same form as in (
5.26
),(
5.27
), but now depending on the coupling
functions

h_{\alpha}(\tau,\sigma)
in place of the couplings, i.e.

\mathcal{A}_{\pm}=\mathcal{A}_{\pm}(x,\partial x;h_{\alpha}(\tau,\sigma))
, etc.

Demanding that the flatness of the connection (
5.28
),(
5.29
)
is equivalent to the equations of motion of the ‘local coupling’ theory (
5.25
),
we shall first prove that it is sufficient to use just
a single function

z(w;\tau,\sigma)
, so that the Lax connections (
5.28
),(
5.29
) become the same as the original ones (
5.26
),(
5.27
)
with the replacement
z\to z(w;\tau,\sigma)

as in (
5.11
),

\displaystyle\widehat{L}_{\pm}=L_{\pm}\big(h_{\alpha}(\tau,\sigma),z(w;\tau,\sigma)\big)\ .

(5.30)

Indeed, the equations of motion for the theory (
5.25
) contain
two types of terms:
the “original” terms (not depending on
\partial_{\pm}h_{\alpha}
), and “new” terms (depending on
\partial_{\pm}h_{\alpha}
).
To match the “original” terms with the corresponding terms in the zero-curvature condition for
\widehat{L}_{\pm}
, one needs
to impose the following conditions on
p_{\pm}
,
q_{\pm}
,
r_{\pm}

(see Appendix
E.1
for details)

\displaystyle G\ :\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle p_{+}^{-1}+p_{-}^{-1}=2\ ,

(5.31)

\displaystyle G/H\ :\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle q_{\pm}=1\ ,\qquad r_{+}r_{-}=1\ .

(5.32)

Substituting (
5.31
),(
5.32
) into the Lax ansatz (
5.28
),(
5.29
) then gives (
5.26
),(
5.27
) but with
z\to z(w;\tau,\sigma)
, where we set

z(w;\tau,\sigma)=2p_{+}-1
(for models on
G
) and

z(w;\tau,\sigma)=2r_{+}
(for models on
G/H
).

All of our examples are single-coupling theories
73
73

73

We do not promote the WZ level
k
in PCM
k
or
\lambda
-models to
a function of
(\tau,\sigma)
since the resulting model
would not be well-defined: starting with the
3d representation of the WZ term and replacing
k{\rm H}\to k(\tau,\sigma){\rm H}

would no longer give a local 2d action
while
k{\rm B}\to k(\tau,\sigma){\rm B}
in the 2d action would not be globally well-defined, or locally consistent with global symmetry
(invariance of the 2d action under global symmetry transformations requires dropping total derivatives).

except for the group
\eta
-model. Here we shall
specialize to the simplest case with one coupling
h(\tau,\sigma)

but the same result will also be true for the group
\eta
-model (see Appendix
E.3
).
To reproduce the “new” terms (proportional to
\partial_{\pm}h
) in the
equations of motion for (
5.25
) from the flatness condition for the generalized Lax connection (
5.30
), one needs to additionally impose certain constraints on both
h(\tau,\sigma)
and
z(w;\tau,\sigma)
.
For the models in Table
1

this leads to the following two first-order differential equations for the function
z(w;\tau,\sigma)

(here
h=h(\tau,\sigma)
)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}z=V_{\tau}(z,h)\ ,\qquad\qquad\partial_{\sigma}z=V_{\sigma}(z,h)\ ,

(5.33)

where
V_{\tau,\sigma}(z,h)
are model-dependent functions. For all five single-coupling examples in Table
1
, the consistency condition (
\partial_{\tau}V_{\sigma}-\partial_{\sigma}V_{\tau}=0
) for the system (
5.33
) takes the
remarkable form,

\displaystyle\partial_{+}\partial_{-}h-\frac{\beta^{\prime}(h)}{\beta(h)}\,\partial_{+}h\partial_{-}h=0\ ,

(5.34)

where
\beta(h)
is precisely the 1-loop
\beta
-function for the coupling
h
in the original model.
In addition
to the appearance (in this purely classical context)
of the 1-loop
\beta
-functions, a remarkable feature of (
5.34
)
that it does not depend on
z
(which completely factors out of the consistency condition).

The condition (
5.34
) may be written simply as
\partial_{-}(\frac{\partial_{+}h}{\beta(h)})=0
or

\partial_{+}(\frac{\partial_{-}h}{\beta(h)})=0
and thus leads to two first-order equations,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{+}h}{\beta(h)}=s_{+}(\xi^{+})\ ,\qquad\qquad\frac{\partial_{-}h}{\beta(h)}=s_{-}(\xi^{-})\ ,

(5.35)

where
s_{\pm}(\xi^{\pm})
are arbitrary functions of the 2d light-cone coordinates
\xi^{\pm}=\tfrac{1}{2}(\tau\pm\sigma)
.
By applying a conformal transformation (i.e. redefining
\tau
and
\sigma
), one can absorb
s_{\pm}
into
\partial_{\pm}
, i.e. replace

s_{\pm}\to 1
,
so that in terms of the redefined coordinates the first-order equations (
5.35
)
take the form of the 1-loop RG equation in
\tau

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}h=\beta(h)\ ,\qquad\qquad\partial_{\sigma}h=0\ .

(5.36)

Thus, modulo a conformal transformation,
the 1-loop RG solution is the only choice
of local coupling

h(\tau,\sigma)

for which the Lax connection can be uplifted to the
(\tau,\sigma)
-dependent theory according to (
5.30
).
This argument (in eqs. (
5.33
)-(
5.36
)) is demonstrated explicitly for the example of the PCM in Appendix
E.2
.

The same conclusion is reached of course if one starts directly with the theory where
the couplings
h_{\alpha}
depend only on time (giving

\partial_{\pm}\to\partial_{\tau}

and
s_{+}=s_{-}={\rm const}
in eqs. (
5.34
),(
5.35
)):
using the same ansatz for the Lax connection
one finds that the only functions
h_{\alpha}(\tau)
consistent with preserving integrability
are the solutions of the 1-loop RG flow — modulo
a rescaling of time, i.e. the part of the conformal group that does not introduce spatial dependence.
By performing a classical conformal transformation, one could instead assign a preferred role to space
\sigma
rather than time
\tau
, and in that case the RG equation (
5.36
) will hold with
\tau\to\sigma
.

To finish the construction of the generalized Lax pair let us now explain how to obtain the explicit form of the spectral parameter function

z(w;\tau,\sigma)
in (
5.30
).
Starting with
h=h(\tau)
that satisfies the 1-loop RG equation in (
5.36
)
(so that the consistency conditions (
5.35
) of (
5.33
)
are satisfied) one can solve the second equation in (
5.33
) as

\displaystyle\partial_{\sigma}z=V_{\sigma}(z,h(\tau))\qquad\rightarrow\qquad\int\frac{dz}{V_{\sigma}(z,h_{\alpha}(\tau))}=\sigma+\ell(\tau)\ .

(5.37)

The function
\ell(\tau)
is then fixed by substituting this solution into the first equation in (
5.33
).
The solution for
\ell(\tau)
leaves one free integration constant, which we call

w
. Finally, eq. (
5.37
) becomes an algebraic equation for
z(w;\tau,\sigma)

that can be explicitly solved in some simple cases (see Table
1
).
Note that since the parameter
w
appears as an integration constant in the function
\ell(\tau)
,
it always appears in the combination
w+\sigma
with the spatial coordinate,
implying that constant shifts of
\sigma
can be compensated by shifts of
w
.

Although we do not have a proof that the 1-loop RG flow always follows from requiring
integrability of the time-dependent generalizations of integrable models, the examples
in Table
1
reveal a highly non-trivial pattern.
In particular,
some of the 1-loop RG equations obtained in this way are quite non-trivial, not admitting simple closed form solutions.

5.3
Non-local conserved charges in time-dependent integrable models

We have shown in the previous sections that some well-known examples of classically integrable
\sigma
-models admit generalizations of their Lax connections to models with time-dependent couplings under the condition that the latter solve
the 1-loop RG equation in
\tau
.
However, one may question the usefulness of the resulting Lax connections since they have several unusual properties: they depend on
\tau
and
\sigma
explicitly; the spectral parameter only enters as a ‘constant piece’ of the spatial coordinate
\sigma
; and they
have branch cuts in the spectral plane (whose positions depend on
\tau
and
\sigma
).

In this section we shall argue that, nevertheless, the Lax connections can still be used to construct the ‘non-local’ charges typical of integrable models. However, the space-time dependence of the Lax connection renders these charges difficult to compute explicitly. We shall focus here on the case of the PCM, while the other examples from Table
1
are discussed in Appendix
F
.

5.3.1
Non-local charges

The derivation of non-local conserved charges from the monodromy matrix
{\cal M}=\mathcal{P}\,\exp\,\int^{b}_{a}d\sigma\,L_{\sigma}
discussed in Section
1.4.1
still applies for Lax connections with explicit space-time dependence. Indeed, the zero-curvature condition
\partial_{\sigma}L_{\tau}-\partial_{\tau}L_{\sigma}+[L_{\sigma},L_{\tau}]=0
implies that

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}{\cal M}={\cal M}\,L_{\tau}(b)-L_{\tau}(a)\,{\cal M}\ .

(5.38)

Hence, if we assume a periodicity condition on the spatial domain
a<\sigma<b
,

\displaystyle L_{\tau}(a)=L_{\tau}(b)\ ,

(5.39)

then we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}{\cal M}=[{\cal M},L_{\tau}(a)]\ ,

(5.40)

and so the eigenvalues of
{\cal M}
are conserved (
\partial_{\tau}{\rm Tr}[{\cal M}^{n}]=0
). If also the value at the endpoints vanishes,
L_{\tau}(b)=L_{\tau}(a)=0
, then all of the components of
{\cal M}
are conserved (
\partial_{\tau}{\cal M}=0
).

We shall focus on the time-dependent PCM on an infinite spatial interval
(a,b)=(-\infty,\infty)
.
74
74

74

It seems much harder to satisfy the periodicity condition (
5.39
) on a spatial circle due to the explicit non-periodic
\sigma
dependence in the Lax connections for the time-dependent models.

Picking the ‘negative’ branch
75
75

75

We note that it is consistent to ‘pick a branch’ here: e.g., by assuming
that
w
has an imaginary part, it then follows that the sign of the square root does not change from
\sigma=-\infty
to
\sigma=\infty
. The choice of a branch is arbitrary but this negative choice is more useful for satisfying the periodicity condition and constructing conserved charges. Also, for the group
\eta
-model and PCM
k
, which are deformations of PCM, the function

{z}
is single-valued at
\sigma=\pm\infty
, with
z_{\infty}\equiv z\big|_{\sigma=\infty}=-1
. It is then natural to obtain
z_{\infty}=-1

for the PCM as a limit of these models.
of the square root,
then the spectral function
{z}(w;\tau,\sigma)
in (
5.21
) has the finite limit
{z}=\sqrt{\frac{w+\sigma+\tau}{w+\sigma-\tau}}\to z_{\infty}=-1
at
\sigma\to\pm\infty
.
Hence, assuming
J_{\pm}
is bounded for large
|\sigma|
, the Lax connection
\widehat{L}_{\pm}=\tfrac{1}{2}(1+{z}^{\pm 1})J_{\pm}

vanishes at spatial infinity. The periodicity condition (
5.39
) is satisfied with
\widehat{L}_{\tau}
vanishing at spatial infinity,
so it follows that all the components of
{\cal M}
are conserved.

The boundary condition (that
J_{\pm}
is bounded), needed above for the conservation of
{\cal M}
, is quite weak. A further, more stringent boundary condition comes from the requirement that
{\cal M}
converges
as
(a,b)\to(-\infty,+\infty)

to produce well-defined charges.

Although the monodromy is conserved, it is only defined implicitly by the first-order ordinary differential equation (
1.14
), i.e.

\displaystyle{\cal M}^{-1}\partial_{\sigma}{\cal M}=\widehat{L}_{\sigma}=\tfrac{1}{4}\big(1+\big[\tfrac{w+\sigma-\tau}{w+\sigma+\tau}\big]^{1/2}\big)J_{+}-\tfrac{1}{4}\big(1+\big[\tfrac{w+\sigma-\tau}{w+\sigma+\tau}\big]^{-1/2}\big)J_{-}\ ,\qquad{\cal M}\big|_{\sigma=-\infty}=I\ .

(5.41)

Such an equation generally admits a solution but it is hard to find its explicit form. One possible approach
is to develop an expansion in the spectral parameter around a point where
\widehat{L}_{\sigma}
vanishes (and thus the monodromy is trivial,
{\cal M}=I
).

In the usual time-independent PCM case (obtained by replacing
\sqrt{\tfrac{w+\sigma-\tau}{w+\sigma+\tau}}
by a constant
z
in (
5.41
)), such an expansion around
z=-1
yields the conserved ‘multi-local’ Yangian charges in eq. (
1.22
).
Each term in the expansion around
z=-1
is individually conserved because each term in the corresponding expansion of
L_{\tau}
(and hence the right hand side of (
5.38
)) vanishes at spatial infinity.

For the time-dependent theory, the only zero around which to expand
\widehat{L}_{\sigma}
in (
5.41
) is
w=\infty
(again taking the negative branch of the square root). But, due to its explicit spatial dependence, the corresponding expansion of the Lax connection
is a sum of terms
w^{-n}P_{n-1}
, where
P_{n-1}
is a polynomial of degree
n-1
in
\sigma
. Then for any polynomial decaying boundary conditions on the fields at spatial infinity, the periodicity condition (
5.39
) on
\widehat{L}_{\tau}
will be broken at sufficiently high orders in this expansion.

Hence, while we have shown the formal existence of conserved non-local charges defined implicitly by (
5.41
), it appears to be hard to compute the monodromy matrix
{\cal M}
explicitly: due to the explicit
\sigma
dependence in the Lax connection, the usual expansion trick (
1.22
) does not easily work.
It is not clear at the moment how
to verify
if the resulting conserved charges are infinite in number (and independent)
as they should be for an integrable 2d theory.

5.3.2
1d reductions

One useful test of 2d integrability is to check the integrability of various 1d mechanical theories obtained as
consistent reductions of the 2d equations of motion.

Assuming an ansatz for a classical solution of an integrable 2d model
yielding a 1d system of equations (say with
\tau
as the remaining independent variable), one may expect
the resulting 1d system also to be integrable in the sense of admitting a Lax pair,
\tfrac{d}{d\tau}A=[B,A]
. One should further demand that the conserved charges are in involution.

In general, if the solution satisfies the periodicity condition (
5.39
), then the flatness of the 2d Lax connection leads to a 1d Lax pair
given by
(A,B)=({\cal M},-L_{\tau}(\infty))
in (
5.40
), evaluated on the reduction ansatz. For example, in
[
251
]
, such a reduction was performed for a certain ‘spinning string’ ansatz in
AdS_{5}\times S^{5}
, resulting in an integrable 1d Neumann-Rosochatius model. There (reversing the roles of
\tau
and
\sigma
from that paper to match the above discussion) the
\sigma
-dependence of the ansatz was simple and it was possible to completely remove the
\sigma
-dependence from the Lax connection using a gauge transformation — which is essentially the same problem as computing the monodromy matrix. In our case, with explicit
\sigma
-dependence already in the Lax connection, the problem of directly removing the
\sigma
-dependence by gauge transformations would appear to be much more difficult.

Trivial reduction.

In the simplest ‘trivial’ 1d reduction one assumes that the
fields do not depend on
\sigma
. This is indeed a consistent truncation of the above time-dependent models, obtained by replacing
\partial_{\pm}\to\tau
in their Lagrangians.
For example, in the PCM case, setting
g=g(\tau)
in (
5.16
) gives the 1d action for
geodesic motion on a group space with a time-dependent radius.
The solvability of geodesics in this model is
obvious since the explicit time dependence may be eliminated by a redefinition of
\tau

\displaystyle S_{1}\sim\int d\tau\ \tau\ {\rm Tr}\big[(g^{-1}\partial_{\tau}g)^{2}\big]=\int d\tau^{\prime}\ {\rm Tr}\big[(g^{-1}\partial_{\tau^{\prime}}g)^{2}\big]\ ,\qquad\ \ \tau^{\prime}=\log\tau\ .

(5.42)

The resulting equation of motion
\partial_{\tau^{\prime}}(g^{-1}\partial_{\tau^{\prime}}g)=0
is solved by

\displaystyle g=g_{0}\,e^{\tau^{\prime}u_{0}}=g_{0}\,\tau^{u_{0}}\ ,\qquad\quad g_{0}={\rm const}\in G\ ,\quad\ u_{0}={\rm const}\in\operatorname{Lie}(G)\ .

(5.43)

The same argument equally applies for any case where the time dependence only appears as an overall factor rescaling the Lagrangian – which will follow if there are enough global symmetries that only the overall ‘radius’ can run under the RG flow (e.g. also in the symmetric space
\sigma
-model case).

In general, the global symmetry charges may be found from the monodromy
{\cal M}
. Formally, the monodromy on an infinite line does not converge upon the trivial reduction since
J_{\tau}=g^{-1}\partial_{\tau}g
does not decay at spatial infinity (as it is now independent of
\sigma
).
On a finite space interval, the periodicity condition (
5.39
) is not satisfied so charges will not be conserved. However, making an expansion around the zero of the Lax connection at
w=\infty
(cf. (
1.22
)), the global symmetry is reflected in the
\sigma
independence of the leading
{\mathcal{O}}(w^{-1})
term in
\widehat{L}_{\tau}
,

\displaystyle\widehat{L}_{\tau}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{4}(1+\big[\tfrac{w+\sigma-\tau}{w+\sigma+\tau}\big]^{1/2})J_{+}+\tfrac{1}{4}(1+\big[\tfrac{w+\sigma-\tau}{w+\sigma+\tau}\big]^{-1/2})J_{-}=\tfrac{1}{2}\tau J_{\sigma}\,w^{-1}+{\mathcal{O}}(w^{-2})\ .

(5.44)

This puts the leading term on the right hand side of (
5.38
) in the commutator form (
5.40
). Since the monodromy matrix is the identity at the leading order,
the right hand side of (
5.38
) is of
{\mathcal{O}}(w^{-2})
. It then follows that the
{\mathcal{O}}(w^{-1})
term in the monodromy matrix, which is the global
G_{R}
Noether charge, is
still conserved
76
76

76

The Noether charge for
G_{L}
symmetry is obtained similarly after a gauge transformation of the Lax connection and choosing instead the positive branch of the square root.

\displaystyle{\cal M}=1+\tfrac{1}{2}w^{-1}Q_{R}+{\mathcal{O}}(w^{-2})\ ,\qquad\partial_{\tau}Q_{R}=0\ ,\qquad Q_{R}=\int d\sigma\ \tau J_{\tau}\ .

(5.45)

Non-trivial reduction.

Now let us consider a non-trivial 1d reduction when the 2d fields
do
depend on
\sigma
in a particular prescribed way. Such a reduction will lead to a
non-trivial 1d model, giving a more stringent test of the integrability of the time-dependent 2d model.

Starting from the time-dependent
SU(2)
PCM (
5.16
) parametrized as

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{L}}=-\tfrac{c}{2}\,\tau\,{\rm Tr}[J_{+}J_{-}]=c\,\tau\,\big[\partial_{+}\theta\partial_{-}\theta+\sin^{2}{\theta}(\partial_{+}\phi\partial_{-}\phi+\sin^{2}{\phi}\,\partial_{+}\psi\partial_{-}\psi)\big]\ ,

(5.46)

\displaystyle\qquad g=n^{a}\sigma_{a}\ ,\qquad\sigma_{a}=(I,\sigma_{i})\ ,\ \ i=1,2,3\ ,

\displaystyle\qquad n^{0}n^{0}-n^{i}n^{i}=1\ ,\qquad n^{a}=(\cos{\theta},\ i\sin{\theta}\cos{\phi},\ i\sin{\theta}\sin{\phi}\cos{\psi},\ i\sin{\theta}\sin{\phi}\sin{\psi})\ ,

let us consider, e.g., the following ansatz for a classical solution

\displaystyle\theta=\theta(\tau)\ ,\qquad\phi=m\sigma\ ,\qquad\psi=0\ .

(5.47)

This leads to a consistent reduction of the 2d theory: the
\phi
and
\psi
equations are both satisfied,
while the equation for
\theta(\tau)
follows from the 1d Lagrangian (
\dot{\theta}\equiv\partial_{\tau}\theta
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1}={c}\,\tau\,\big(\dot{\theta}^{2}-m^{2}\sin^{2}{\theta}\big)\ .

(5.48)

Thus the effective 1d model is a time-dependent analog of the ‘sine-Gordon’ mechanics.

Let us assume the
\sigma
-direction is an infinite line, still using the ansatz (
5.47
) (e.g. by taking
m
to be a continuous parameter and formally decompactifying
\phi
).
Then
one can check that
J_{\pm}
is oscillating but bounded at spatial infinity. Hence, as discussed above, the right hand side of (
5.38
) vanishes and the entries of the monodromy matrix are conserved.
While again it is not easy to compute the monodromy explicitly in terms of
\theta
and
\dot{\theta}
,
it can be done in the small field expansion
(\theta,\dot{\theta})\to\varepsilon(\theta,\dot{\theta})
,
\varepsilon\ll 1
since the Lax component
\widehat{L}_{\sigma}
vanishes as
(\theta,\dot{\theta})\to 0
. In this expansion the Lagrangian (
5.48
) becomes (after rescaling it by a factor of
c^{-1}\varepsilon^{-2}
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1}^{\prime}=\tau\big[\dot{\theta}^{2}-m^{2}\varepsilon^{-2}\sin^{2}{(\varepsilon\theta)}\big]=\mathcal{L}^{lin}+\tfrac{1}{3}\varepsilon^{2}\,m^{2}\,\theta^{4}+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{4})\ ,

(5.49)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{lin}=\tau\,\big[\dot{\theta}^{2}-m^{2}\theta^{2}\big]\ .

(5.50)

The leading (‘linearized’) Lagrangian (
5.50
) describes (after the same redefinition
\tau=e^{\tau^{\prime}}
as in (
5.42
))
a harmonic oscillator with time-dependent frequency
m^{2}e^{2\tau^{\prime}}
.
The corresponding
{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon)
terms in the monodromy matrix lead to the conserved charge

\displaystyle\dot{Q}^{lin}=0\ ,\qquad Q^{lin}=\tau\big[\alpha(\tau;w)\,\dot{\theta}-\dot{\alpha}(\tau;w)\,\theta\big]\ ,

(5.51)

\displaystyle\alpha(\tau;w)=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}d\sigma\,e^{-im\sigma}\tfrac{i}{2s_{+}s_{-}}\ ,\qquad\qquad s_{\pm}\equiv\sqrt{w+\sigma\pm\tau}\ .

(5.52)

In Appendix
G
we use this conserved charge to construct the general solution of the linearized equation of motion
(with
\theta=\alpha
being a particular solution).

Computing the perturbative corrections to the monodromy, one then finds for the
conserved charge of the non-linear theory (
5.49
)

\displaystyle\dot{Q}=0\ ,\ \ \ Q=Q^{lin}+\varepsilon^{2}\Big[\int d\sigma\,q^{(1)}+\int_{\sigma_{1}<\sigma_{2}}d\sigma_{1}d\sigma_{2}\,q^{(2)}+\int_{\sigma_{1}<\sigma_{2}<\sigma_{3}}d\sigma_{1}d\sigma_{2}d\sigma_{3}\,q^{(3)}\Big]+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{4})\ ,

\displaystyle\ \ \ q^{(1)}=-\tfrac{m\,e^{-im\sigma}}{3s_{+}s_{-}}(s_{+}s_{-}-w-\sigma)\,\theta^{3}\ ,

(5.53)

\displaystyle\ \ \ q^{(2)}=\tfrac{im}{4s^{1}_{+}s^{1}_{-}s^{2}_{+}s^{2}_{-}}\Big[e^{-im\sigma_{2}}(s_{+}^{1}s_{-}^{1}-w-\sigma_{1})\big[m(s_{+}^{2}s_{-}^{2}-w-\sigma_{2})\theta+i\tau\dot{\theta}\big]-(\sigma_{1}\leftrightarrow\sigma_{2})\Big]\,\theta^{2}\ ,

\displaystyle\ \ \ q^{(3)}=\tfrac{e^{-im(\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2}+\sigma_{3})}}{8s^{1}_{+}s^{1}_{-}s^{2}_{+}s^{2}_{-}s^{3}_{+}s^{3}_{-}}\big[m(s_{+}^{1}s_{-}^{1}-w-\sigma_{1})+i\tau\dot{\theta}\big]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\big[-m(s_{+}^{2}s_{-}^{2}-w-\sigma_{2})+i\tau\dot{\theta}\big]\big[m(s_{+}^{3}s_{-}^{3}-w-\sigma_{3})+i\tau\dot{\theta}\big]\ ,\ \ \quad s_{\pm}^{n}\equiv\sqrt{w+\sigma_{n}\pm\tau}\ .

This perturbative procedure of constructing the conserved charge can be extended to higher orders,
computing more and more
\sigma
integrals at each step.

Let us also mention that the time-dependent
SL(2,\mathbb{R})
PCM (cf. (
5.46
)),

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{L}}=-c\,\tau\Big[\partial_{+}\rho\partial_{-}\rho+\tfrac{1}{2}e^{-2\rho}\,(\partial_{+}x^{+}\partial_{-}x^{-}+\partial_{-}x^{+}\partial_{+}x^{-})\Big]\ ,

(5.54)

admits a ‘non-trivial’ reduction that is manifestly integrable.
Indeed, if we set
x^{+}=a\sigma,\ x^{-}=b\sigma,\ \rho=\rho(\tau)
then the
x^{\pm}
equations are solved
and the equation for
\rho(\tau)
follows from the 1d action (cf. (
5.48
))

\displaystyle S_{1}=-c\,\int d\tau\,\tau\big[(\partial_{\tau}{\rho})^{2}-ab\,e^{-2\rho}\big]\ .

(5.55)

Here the explicit time dependence can be eliminated by redefining
\tau=e^{\tau^{\prime}}
and
\rho=\rho^{\prime}+\tau^{\prime}

so that we end up with the standard 1d Liouville mechanics with the energy being a conserved charge.

5.4
Discussion

We have
observed
a surprising new connection between
classical integrability and the RG flow in 2d theories:
starting with an integrable theory and promoting its couplings to time-dependent functions, its
Lax connection generalizes naturally to the resulting
time-dependent theory only if the coupling functions
solve the 1-loop RG equations
of the original theory.
We demonstrated this on six classes of integrable
\sigma
-models.

One interesting implication is
that the 1-loop
\beta
-functions, which are normally associated with 1-loop divergences in quantum theory,
can thus be obtained in these models through the classical procedure of requiring the existence of a flat Lax connection in
the corresponding time-dependent theory.

As we discussed at the start of the chapter,
such time-dependent models can be naturally embedded into string theory by
starting with a Weyl-invariant
\sigma
-model (
5.3
) with two extra null directions
(u,v)

and fixing a l.c. gauge
u=\tau
.
If the l.c. theory (
5.6
) is classically solvable, it is natural to expect that the corresponding
string
\sigma
-model (
5.3
) should be solvable,
since one may move
from
u=\tau
to
any other
solution of the
u
-equation
\partial_{+}\partial_{-}u=0

by a conformal transformation. The
v
-equations

\partial_{\pm}v=\tfrac{1}{2}{\rm G}_{ij}(\tau,x)\,\partial_{\pm}x^{i}\partial_{\pm}x^{j}

(following from the conformal constraints)
are
linear and so are also readily solvable. It is not immediately clear, however,
if integrability in the sense of admitting a Lax representation should similarly lift up to the
(D+2)
-dimensional string
\sigma
-model directly.

The Lax connections for the time-dependent theories have an
unusual form (depending explicitly on
\tau
and
\sigma
, as in the special
examples discussed in
[
248
,
249
]
). However, given certain boundary conditions, the entries or
the eigenvalues
of the monodromy matrix on an infinite spatial line are conserved.
Due to the explicit
\sigma
dependence in the Lax connection, it is hard to
evaluate the monodromy and find the conserved charges explicitly.
We considered some consistent reductions of the 2d equations of motion to time-dependent 1d systems.
The ‘trivial’ (
\sigma
-independent)
reduction of the PCM is clearly integrable due to the remaining global symmetry.

For the ‘non-trivial’ 1d reduction in Section
5.3.2
we evaluated the monodromy, and hence constructed a conserved charge (
5.53
) in perturbation theory in a small field expansion. The perturbative existence of this conserved charge (following from the existence of a Lax connection)
is a non-trivial property, suggesting
that the 1d theory (
5.48
) may be interpreted as integrable. In contrast, with generic (time-dependent) potentials added to the linearized theory (
5.50
), we would not expect the linearized conserved charge (
5.51
) to admit even a perturbative extension to the non-linear theory.
The time-dependent 1d theory (
5.48
) is certainly not integrable in the Liouville sense as it would fail the Kovacic algorithm test used in
[
236
,
256
]
.
Here we are suggesting a broader notion of integrability relating to the explicit solvability of the equations of motion (allowing non-Liouvillian solutions like Bessel functions, etc.).

One way to fully establish the classical integrability of the
\sigma
-models with local couplings
would be to construct an infinite tower of local conserved charges in involution, in the spirit of
[
252
,
253
,
254
,
229
]
.
In Section 5 of
[
4
]
we attempted this
for the PCM with ‘local couplings’
in the Hamiltonian formulation.
A naive application of the method used for constant couplings does not yield such charges, except in the case of the ‘chiral’ theories only depending on
\xi^{+}
or on
\xi^{-}
,
where it works with a slight modification (see Appendix D of
[
4
]
).
Nevertheless, the form of the Lax matrix and
R
-matrix is suggestive of an underlying algebraic structure.
Understanding it may provide additional insights into the question of integrability.

While we considered time-dependent models at the classical level only, one may wonder if they themselves
are renormalizable, i.e. stable under RG flow. Renormalization of generalized

\sigma
-models with target-space metric depending on the 2d coordinates was considered in
[
257
]
. In the case
we discussed (cf. (
5.6
)) when
{\rm G}_{ij}
depends only on
\tau
, i.e.
\mathcal{L}={\rm G}_{ij}(\tau,x)\partial_{+}x^{i}\partial_{-}x^{j}
, the 1-loop logarithmic counterterm
is proportional to
K_{1}={\rm G}_{ml}{\rm G}^{jk}\partial_{\tau}x^{m}\,\partial_{\tau}\Gamma^{l}_{jk}+\frac{1}{4}\partial_{\tau}{\rm G}^{ij}\partial_{\tau}{\rm G}_{ij}
, where

\Gamma^{l}_{jk}
is the Christoffel connection of
{\rm G}_{ij}
. Thus in general one needs to add also counterterms with one and no derivatives, i.e.
V_{m}(x)\partial_{\tau}x^{m}+T(x)
. However, in the ‘factorized’ case when
{\rm G}_{ij}(\tau,x)=f(\tau){\rm G}_{ij}(x),

like in the time-dependent PCM (
5.16
), the above counterterm becomes an
x
-independent function,

K_{1}\sim(\partial_{\tau}f)^{2}
. Furthermore, if
f
is linear in
\tau
as in (
5.16
) then this
K_{1}
is just a constant
and thus such a model is renormalizable.
This suggests that at least some time-dependent integrable models
discussed in this chapter may have
well-defined quantum generalizations.

Among other possible directions, it would be important to generalize the present investigation to
other integrable
\sigma
-models, e.g. the class of models based on complex homogeneous spaces
whose Ricci flow was studied in
[
258
,
259
]
and the
\mathbb{Z}_{T}
coset models of
[
13
,
260
]
.
Another interesting class of examples would be the integrable
G\times G
and
G\times G/H
models studied in Chapter
3
above.
Furthermore, while in this chapter we have only studied 2-derivative
\sigma
-models,
we expect the connection between
the requirement of integrability of the time-dependent theory and the RG flow to be more general and to apply also to
models with potentials.
Indeed,
one also finds this remarkable connection in the case of the sine-Gordon model,

\displaystyle S=\tfrac{1}{4\pi}\int d\tau d\sigma\,\tfrac{1}{g^{2}}\big[\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{+}x\partial_{-}x+m^{2}\cos{x}\big]\ .

(5.56)

Replacing the couplings
(g,m)
by functions
77
77

77

For a discussion of the sine-Gordon model with a particular ‘non-integrable’
local coupling
m=m(\sigma)
see
[
261
]
and references there.

of
\tau

one can show
that, for the resulting theory to be integrable
(assuming a natural ansatz for a generalization of the Lax connection to the time-dependent theory
—
see Appendix
H
),
the functions
(g(\tau),m(\tau))
should be solutions of the 1-loop RG flow equation for (
5.56
), i.e.
should be given by

\displaystyle m^{2}(\tau)=e^{\beta(g)\,\tau}\,m_{0}^{2}\ ,\ \ \ \qquad g(\tau)=g\ ,\ \ \ \ \qquad\beta(g)=-2+g^{2}\ .

(5.57)

The time-dependent theory,

\displaystyle\widehat{S}=\tfrac{1}{4\pi}\int d\tau d\sigma\,\tfrac{1}{g^{2}}\big[\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{+}x\partial_{-}x+e^{\beta(g)\,\tau}\,m^{2}_{0}\,\cos{x}\big]\ ,

(5.58)

is indeed classically integrable since the explicit
\tau
-dependence in (
5.58
) can
be removed by a 2d conformal transformation getting back to (
5.56
):
under
\xi^{\pm}\to f^{\pm}(\xi^{\pm})
the action
(
5.56
) retains its form with

m^{2}\to{f^{+}}^{\prime}(\xi^{+})\,{f^{-}}^{\prime}(\xi^{-})\,m^{2}
.
Note that
the non-trivial 1d reduction of the
SU(2)
PCM in eq. (
5.48
) is also obtained as the ‘trivial’ reduction
x=x(\tau)

of (
5.58
)
after a redefinition
\tau\to\log\tau
, supporting the expectation that (
5.48
) is an integrable 1d theory.
It would be interesting to explore
whether
this time-dependent integrability–RG flow connection applies also to
other examples of integrable massive theories, such as complex sine-Gordon and Toda models.

One of the features of our construction of the Lax connection for time-dependent theories is that
the constant spectral parameter of the original theory
is replaced by
a function

z\to z(w;\tau,\sigma)
of a new spectral parameter
w
and the 2d coordinates (cf.
(
5.20
),(
5.21
)).
This puts the spectral parameter and 2d space-time coordinates on a more equal footing,
suggesting a possible interpretation from the point of view of the
construction
[
264
]

of many
integrable 2d theories from a 4d Chern-Simons theory,
with the two extra directions related to the complex spectral parameter (see also
[
265
]
and refs. there).
In that context the redefinition of
z
is like changing
the differential structure of the 4d space, i.e. replacing
\partial_{\tau,\sigma}z=0

with
\partial_{\tau,\sigma}z=V_{\tau,\sigma}(z;\tau,\sigma)
(cf. (
5.33
)).
It would be interesting to see if the time-dependent models considered above
can be reproduced from the 4d Chern-Simons construction.
Indeed, the
\sigma
-dependent version of the PCM discussed above
has already been obtained
[
132
]
from a closely related Chern-Simons theory on twistor space.

Chapter 6
Conclusions and future directions

6.1
Integrability vs. RG flow

This thesis is based on the interesting observation that integrable 2d
\sigma
-models appear to be stable under the leading 1-loop RG flow, i.e. they are solutions of the (generalized) Ricci flow equation (
1.9
) with a few couplings running.
Our purpose was to investigate this property beyond the leading 1-loop order. We found in Chapter
2
for integrable
\eta
- and
\lambda
-deformed models, and in Chapter
3
for integrable
G\times G/H
models, that generic examples will require quantum deformation of the target space geometry to remain stable under the 2-loop and higher RG flow. Thus, in each case, there is a particular finite counterterm — or particular choice of bare action — that restores renormalizability beyond 1-loop. Moreover, this is a scheme-independent statement since these counterterms cannot be eliminated by a covariant scheme transformation.

In Section
2.2
, we explicitly constructed these counterterms for the simplest
\eta
-deformed
S^{2}
(or “sausage”) model. In that case, by analogy with the gauged WZW model (whose corrections are well-known, and which arises as a particular limit of the sausage model) we conjectured the exact form (
2.11
) of the quantum deformation to all orders in
\alpha^{\prime}
(or the
\sigma
-model loop expansion). We checked explicitly up to 3 loops that these corrections indeed restore renormalizability in a particular subtraction scheme.

In Section
2.3
, we constructed the leading corrections restoring 2-loop renormalizability to the
\lambda
-models based on
SU(2)\over U(1)
and
SU(2)
. In the simpler
SU(2)\over U(1)
case, the corrections were understood as a familiar
(\partial\log\hbox{det}M)^{2}
determinant (
2.43
) from the quadratic integral over the
\lambda
-model’s 2d gauge field in (
1.78
). However, such 2d integrals are known to be ill-defined
[
127
]
with a local term ambiguity depending on a choice of prescription or regularization. Indeed we find that this simple formula is not sufficient to restore renormalizability in the
SU(2)
case, where the leading corrections take the more complicated form (
2.64
), or (
2.56
) in explicit coordinates.

In Chapter
4
we proved in general that the
\lambda
-model (
1.78
) in the formulation with unintegrated gauge field is renormalizable to all orders, by re-writing the gauge field in terms of group valued scalars and obtaining a
\sigma
-model with a “tripled”
G\times G\times G
configuration space. This means that the corrections required for renormalizability of the
\lambda
-model in “standard” configuration space may all be understood as finite counterterms arising from the ambiguous 2d gauge integral. Clearly the simple
(\partial\log\hbox{det}M)^{2}
prescription is not generally consistent with renormalizability (since it breaks down already for the
SU(2)
case).

An interesting open problem is to find a general prescription for the finite local counterterms produced by integrals of this type,
\int dA\exp\big(i\int A_{+}MA_{-}\big)
, consistent with the RG flow. This would give a general formula for the quantum corrections needed to restore higher-loop renormalizability for all
\lambda
-models, generalizing the
SU(2)\over U(1)
and
SU(2)
results found here. One possible approach based on the work of
[
238
]
would be to try to construct the necessary counterterms from the scheme transformation between our standard 2-loop scheme (
1.9
) and one that is non-Lorentz-covariant but manifestly respects Poisson-Lie symmetry, in which the
\lambda
- and
\eta
-models are claimed to be automatically renormalizable.

For the
G\times G/H
models in Section
3.2
, the manifest (global and local) symmetries alone restrict the theory to a finite set of couplings (
3.16
),(
3.19
) that could run under the RG flow. The integrable theories then correspond to a “surface” in this space of couplings, specified by polynomial equations (
3.25
). Hence we observed that the necessary counterterms to restore 2-loop stability of the integrable surface under the RG flow can be interpreted as a quantum correction to the shape of the surface. This is like a simple, finite-dimensional analog of the general case where the space of
\sigma
-model couplings is essentially infinite (e.g., for the integrable deformed models above).

We also constructed in Chapter
3
a new “branch” of
H
-gauge-invariant
G\times G/H
theories in the case when
H
is abelian, with independent WZ levels on the two copies of the group
G
. We found that a particular subfamily of this new branch is classically integrable and, in the
SU(2)\times SU(2)/U(1)
case, these correspond to a family of integrable
\sigma
-models with target space
T^{1,q}
supported by a particular
{\rm B}
-field. These models are a generalization of the integrable
T^{1,1}
models of
[
218
]
, with the extra parameter
q
being related to the ratio of the two WZ levels.

In integrable examples with enough
manifest
symmetries, such as the undeformed PCM or symmetric space, renormalizability can be an automatic result of the symmetries. However, generic integrable
\sigma
-models may have no manifest global symmetries at all; rather, the integrable structure is related to an intricate structure of
hidden
(non-local or higher spin) symmetries. The effect of such symmetries on the RG flow is far less obvious since, e.g., it is not clear in which way they should constrain the Riemann tensor and
{\rm H}
-field that enter the RG equations (
1.9
). Remarkably, it appears that hidden integrable symmetries impose drastic constraints on the RG flow, restricting the 1-loop RG flow to just a flow of the couplings of the classical theory. We have observed that this property persists also at higher loop orders provided the target space geometry is suitably deformed by quantum (
\alpha^{\prime}
) corrections. Indeed, such a deformation is natural given the the well-known examples of conformal
\sigma
-models where
\alpha^{\prime}
corrections are needed to restore higher-loop conformal invariance.

One interesting open question is to understand how and why integrability implies renormalizability, and perhaps to prove this statement, starting at the leading 1-loop order.
As we discussed in the Introduction, the rationale behind the conjectured integrability–RG flow relationship is that the stability of integrable theories under the RG flow simply corresponds to preserving their integrable structure at the quantum level. In fact, it is natural that such hidden integrable symmetries should require a particular choice of finite local counterterms to be manifest in the quantum theory, just like for standard global symmetries.
It would be interesting to verify this interpretation by checking explicitly that the finite counterterms required for renormalizability are actually restoring some quantum ‘Ward identities’ for hidden integrable symmetries. This would give an interesting semi-classical picture of quantum integrability.
Such computations could be carried out, e.g., for the integrable-deformed models considered in Chapter
2
, in relation to either the higher-spin charges or the non-local charges. For the
\eta
-deformed models, studying the quantum non-local charges would also be an interesting check of the conjectured quantum group symmetry of the quantum theory
[
57
,
137
,
47
,
58
]
.

The integrable
G\times G
models studied in Section
3.1
are an anomaly to the discussion above. These theories are
automatically
stable under the 2-loop RG flow, even though (to our best knowledge) the integrability condition is not protected by any manifest symmetries. Generically we would have expected the integrable “surface” in coupling space to require some quantum deformation (like the
G\times G/H
models) in order to remain stable beyond 1-loop. An interesting open problem is to explain this fact, which surely should not be a coincidence.

The
G\times G
and
G\times G/H
models demonstrate that, while integrability seems to imply renormalizability, the converse statement is not true. These models are renormalizable with a finite set of couplings but most of them are not integrable (only ones with special values of the couplings solving polynomial equations).

Classical integrability may also become anomalous at the quantum level, and we note that renormalizability seems too coarse a measure to pick up on this.
For example the bosonic
\mathbb{C}\mathbf{P}^{N}
model is well known to suffer such an anomaly
[
207
]
(see also
[
208
]
).
However, it is still 2-loop renormalizable
[
77
]
.
78
78

78

For the
\mathbb{C}\mathbf{P}^{N}
model it has been conjectured that quantum integrability
can be restored by including an additional free field
[
209
]
in the classical limit (related models have also appeared in
[
210
]
).
An alternative is to consider the supersymmetric
\mathbb{C}\mathbf{P}^{N}

\sigma
-model in which there is no anomaly
[
211
]
.

We recall that our original motivation for studying stability under the RG flow was that it might help to
identify
integrable
\sigma
-models. In a certain sense, this is what we have been doing: starting from classically integrable theories, we have been constructing particular formulations of the quantum theories (with particular finite counterterms) that are RG-stable at higher-loop orders — with the belief that these formulations are the ones consistent with integrability at the quantum level.

It would be interesting in future to apply the integrability–RG flow connection to discover new examples of integrable
\sigma
-models. First at the level of classical integrability, this may be carried out by imposing stability under the leading 1-loop RG flow. One possible example would be for non-symmetric
G/H
cosets, which generally have several couplings,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\,{\rm Tr}[J_{+}\,\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}h_{i}P_{\mathfrak{p_{i}}}\big)\,J_{-}]\ ,

(6.1)

corresponding to a decomposition of the tangent space into irreducible representations
\mathfrak{p_{i}}
under the adjoint action of
H
(see Section
1.4.3
). It may be possible in certain cases to discover integrable “surfaces” in this coupling space by demanding stability under the RG flow. Indeed, renormalizability of certain non-symmetric coset spaces was previously considered in
[
216
]
.

6.2
“Tripled” formulation

The “tripled” formulation of the
\lambda
-model in Chapter
4
may be viewed as an alternate formulation of the quantum theory where some fields decouple, likely as a manifestation of integrability. The remaining “truncated” theory is automatically stable under the RG flow to all orders due to manifest global symmetries, without needing any quantum deformation.

The general idea of moving to an enlarged configuration space to make manifest extra symmetries (in this case integrability) is reminiscent of T-duality-invariant formulations of string theory
[
205
]
. Such approaches
are essentially 1st order Hamiltonian formulations of the
\sigma
-model, with manifest Lorentz invariance being sacrificed in favour of manifest T-duality invariance.
A related 1st order formulation of Poisson-Lie symmetric
\sigma
-models known as the “E-model” makes the more general Poisson-Lie duality manifest
[
177
]
.

In Section
4.5
, we considered similar reformulations on enlarged configuration spaces of (i) a variant of the
\lambda
-model based on
G^{N}
and (ii)
G\times G/H
models. In each case the resulting model was of product type, i.e. a particular class of coupled models on some space
G^{M}
; and in the
G\times G/H
case there was some “squashing”, or asymmetry between the
H
and
G/H
directions. Re-writing these models in this way is interesting as the corresponding enlarged models will have identical classical integrability and RG flow properties to the original ones. For the
G^{N}

\lambda
-model we find an identification between its RG flow and classical integrability conditions and those of an apparently simpler undeformed
G^{2N}
model. We also learn that it is automatically renormalizable in the tripled formulation due to its manifest symmetries (before imposing the integrability conditions of
[
65
]
). For the
G\times G/H
models we essentially discover a new class of classically integrable and RG-stable squashed
G\times G\times H
models. It would be interesting to study the higher-loop RG flows of these theories to see if the enlarged formulations render the integrability conditions automatically stable at higher-loops (as for the
\lambda
-model).

Another interesting class of
\sigma
-models are those with complex homogeneous target spaces that are equivalent
[
259
,
178
]
to bosonic Gross-Neveu (GN) models
[
180
]
(see also the reviews
[
179
]
). The reformulation of these
\sigma
-models as GN models is essentially equivalent to the introduction of a 2d gauge field, so should be closely related to the “tripled” or “extended” formulations discussed here. It would be interesting to check stability under the 2-loop RG flow in that formulation (which was previously only checked at 1-loop
[
259
]
).

An interesting future direction would be to try to construct a similar tripled formulation of the
\eta
-deformed models. It seems plausible that such a formulation should exist since the
\eta
-models and
\lambda
-models are related (up to analytic continuation) by Poisson-Lie duality a
[
151
,
158
,
156
,
159
]
, or by limits and T-duality
[
151
,
159
]
.

6.3
Non-abelian duality

In the non-abelian dual limit of the
\lambda
-model, the general 2-loop
\beta
-functions obtained from the tripled formulation match those of the original PCM/symmetric space
\sigma
-models. This agreement is a non-trivial check of our
\lambda
-model
\beta
-functions since the 2-loop terms are also scheme-invariant under coupling redefinitions in that single-coupling limit. It is thus natural to expect that the interpolating models for non-abelian duality are renormalizable with the same 2-loop
\beta
-functions as the original models — justified also by their relation to the original models by just an integral over a linear Lagrange multipler imposing the flatness of the gauge field.

Hence, like the
\lambda
-model, the non-trivial corrections found for the NAD models based on
SU(2)/U(1)
and
SU(2)
in Section
2.3.3
should be seen as resulting from the integral over the 2d gauge field.
In certain limits these NAD models degenerate to abelian T-duals and in those cases our corrections matched with the known corrections to the T-duality transformation.

It would be interesting to consider more general models with non-abelian symmetries, which may have spectator coordinates (here
x
or
u,v
), for example

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\partial_{+}x\,\partial_{-}x+f(x)\,{\rm Tr}[J_{+}J_{-}],\qquad\qquad(J\equiv g^{-1}dg,\ \ g\in G)

(6.2)

or perhaps the conformal
\sigma
-model considered in (
5.17
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=-2\,\partial_{+}u\,\partial_{-}v+u\,{\rm Tr}[J_{+}J_{-}]\ .

(6.3)

Starting from such a theory that is integrable and renormalizable (say, for a particular choice of
f(x)
) the interpolating model for NAD is obtained by replacing
J_{\pm}\to A_{\pm}
and adding a Lagrange multiplier constraint
{\rm Tr}[v\,F_{+-}(A)]
. One can then perform the same trick of going to a tripled formulation by re-writing the gauge field in terms of group valued scalars. It would be interesting to study the RG flows for these interpolating models in the tripled formulation and to see if they still automatically match the original models (also at 2-loops) in the presence of spectator fields.

6.4
Quantum corrections and string sigma models

An important application of our results would be to the related
\lambda
- and
\eta
-deformations of superstring
\sigma
-models. For example, focussing on the simplest case of
AdS_{5}\times S^{5}
, the
\lambda
-deformed background has been extracted from a
\sigma
-model formulation
[
175
]
and checked to be a type IIB supergravity solution
[
183
]
. Equivalently, the string
\sigma
-model is 1-loop Weyl-invariant (1-loop scale-invariance was also checked in
[
153
]
). The
\eta
-deformed
AdS_{5}\times S^{5}
background of
[
184
]
was also extracted and checked to be a solution of the supergravity equations.

In general, solutions of the
d=10
type II supergravity equations should receive
\alpha^{\prime}
corrections to become exact string solutions, with the first corrections appearing at order
\alpha^{\prime 3}

[
75
]
. In contrast with the undeformed
AdS_{5}\times S^{5}
background where corrections are prohibited by manifest symmetries,
79
79

79

Exact finiteness of the undeformed
AdS_{5}\times S^{5}
superstring
\sigma
-model (
1.87
) follows because the WZ-type
J^{(1)}J^{(3)}
term is not renormalized due to general arguments and the
J^{(2)}J^{(2)}
term may only be renormalized by an overall factor, but this cannot happen since the two terms are related by
{\kappa}
-symmetry (see footnote 4 of
[
76
]
and refs. there).
it is not clear whether the deformed backgrounds will require non-trivial corrections. It may be that, due to the models’ hidden integrable symmetries, the corrections can be made to vanish in some particular subtraction scheme. On the other hand, this did not happen for the bosonic analogs above, which are also integrable.

It would be interesting to study this question and to understand the structure of the
\alpha^{\prime}
corrections to these backgrounds. For the
\lambda
-deformation, a systematic way to do this may come from adapting the tripled formulation to the superstring case. This seems feasible since the
\lambda
-deformed
AdS_{5}\times S^{5}
superstring was formulated in
[
175
]
in the language of supergroups with a 2d gauge field. In such a tripled formulation, the
\alpha^{\prime}
corrections may be expected to vanish due to
manifest symmetries after a decoupling of some fields
(like the bosonic case), and the exact finiteness of the model may become manifest.

6.5
Sigma models with local couplings

In Chapter
5
we discussed a new and apparently different connection between integrability and the RG flow: upon promoting the couplings of an integrable
\sigma
-model to functions of 2d time, the resulting “local coupling” theory remains classically integrable (with a natural ansatz for its Lax connection) only when the coupling functions solve the 1-loop RG flow, with 2d time playing the role of RG time
t=\log\mu
. This curious pattern was observed in a broad class of integrable
\sigma
-models on groups and symmetric spaces, including integrable
\eta
- and
\lambda
-deformations. It would be good to check this claim for more examples of integrable
\sigma
-models, like the
G\times G
and
G\times G/H
models. We also found the same result for the sine-Gordon model, and it would be interesting to check more examples of integrable theories with potentials, e.g. Toda models.

These results are surprising because it seems that the 1-loop quantum
\beta
-functions are derivable in this way from a purely classical procedure (from demanding that the local-coupling theory admits a flat Lax connection). We also noted that theories with explicit time-dependence governed by the RG flow are naturally obtained in string theory upon fixing the light-cone gauge
u=\tau
, starting from a class of Weyl-invariant
\sigma
-models
[
239
,
246
]
with a covariantly constant null Killing vector and a dilaton linear in the isometry direction,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=-2\,\partial_{+}u\,\partial_{-}v+{\rm G}_{ij}(u;x)\partial_{+}x^{i}\partial_{-}x^{j}\ ,\quad\partial_{u}{\rm G}_{ij}=\beta_{ij}(x)\ ,\qquad(\phi=v+\phi(u,x))\ .

(6.4)

Thus we find that — assuming the “transverse” (
u=\text{const}
) theory to be integrable — the light-cone (
u=\tau
) theory is apparently classically integrable only when the string
\sigma
-model (
6.4
) is 1-loop Weyl invariant. This points to a deeper meaning or explanation of our results, and to the possibility of a large, new class of solvable string backgrounds.

What remains to be clarified is whether these time-dependent (light-cone) theories may really be viewed as solvable. Although the monodromy matrix is formally conserved, it is difficult to explicitly extract the associated conserved charges because of problems making a good expansion in the spectral parameter. We also made attempts to study higher-spin local charges in the paper
[
4
]
, but our results so far were inconclusive. One possible way to demonstrate solvability would be to explicitly construct some classical solutions; indeed this has already been done for the time-dependent PCM example
[
248
]

by adapting the inverse scattering approach of
[
28
,
29
]
.

An interesting extension would be to consider the
(2,2)
supersymmetric
\sigma
-models on certain Kähler symmetric spaces whose
\beta
-functions are known to be 1-loop exact
[
85
]
. Supersymmetric
\sigma
-models on symmetric spaces are classically integrable in general
[
90
]
. Hence the pattern found above for bosonic theories may generalize to the supersymmetric ones. In that case the corresponding string backgrounds built from the 1-loop RG flow (which will be
exact
string solutions) may be completely solvable.

A further question is whether the local-coupling
\sigma
-models admitting Lax representations can be obtained from 4d Chern-Simons theory
[
264
]
(like all of the regular integrable
\sigma
-models considered in this thesis
[
62
,
265
]
). This is a natural expectation, since the 4d perspective places the 2d space-time and the complex spectral parameter on an equal footing, while the Lax connections for the local coupling theories are related to the original ones by a space-time-dependent redefinition of the spectral parameter,
z\to z(w;\tau,\sigma)
.

Appendix A
Conventions

Unless otherwise stated, the 2d space is assumed to be flat Minkowski space with signature
(-+)
, i.e.
\eta_{00}=-\eta_{11}=-1
in terms of the 2d coordinates
\xi^{\mu}=(\xi^{0},\xi^{1})=(\tau,\sigma)
with 2d incides
\mu,\nu=1,2
. The antisymmetric Levi-Civita symbol
\varepsilon_{\mu\nu}
is defined by
\varepsilon^{01}=-\varepsilon^{10}=1
and
\varepsilon_{01}=-\varepsilon_{10}=-1
(with indices raised/lowered by
\eta_{\mu\nu}
). We use 2d light-cone coordinates defined as
\xi^{\pm}=\tfrac{1}{2}(\tau\pm\sigma)
and light-cone derivatives
\partial_{\pm}=\partial_{\tau}\pm\partial_{\sigma}
.

The
\sigma
-model action is normalized as
S=\tfrac{1}{4\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int d^{2}\xi\,\mathcal{L}
with the usual flat-space volume form
d^{2}\xi=d\xi^{0}\wedge d\xi^{1}
.
The Lagrangian is

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=-\big[\eta^{\mu\nu}\,{\rm G}_{mn}(x)+\varepsilon^{\mu\nu}\,{\rm B}_{mn}(x)\big]\,\partial_{\mu}x^{m}\,\partial_{\nu}x^{n}=\big[{\rm G}_{mn}(x)+{\rm B}_{mn}(x)\big]\,\partial_{+}x^{m}\partial_{-}x^{n}\ .

(A.1)

The target space metric is
ds^{2}=G_{mn}dx^{m}dx^{n}
. The
{\rm B}
-field written as a 2-form is
{\rm B}=\tfrac{1}{2}dx^{m}\wedge dx^{n}
and its field strength is
{\rm H}=d{\rm B}
.

The components of
n
-forms are normalized in the standard way,
H_{(n)}=\tfrac{1}{n!}H_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}dx^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx^{i_{n}}
.

We take
G
to be a simple, real Lie group with generators
T_{A}
(
A=1,\ldots,\dim(G)
) satisfying
[T_{A},T_{B}]=i{f^{C}}_{AB}T_{C}
.

We use a non-degenerate bilinear form defined as

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}=\tfrac{1}{2\,\chi_{{}_{G,R}}}\,{\rm tr}_{{}_{R}}\ ,

(A.2)

where
{\rm tr}_{{}_{R}}
is the matrix trace in some faithful representation
R
of
G
. The constant
\chi_{{}_{G,R}}
denotes the
index
of the representation
R
, defined by

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2\,\chi_{{}_{G,R}}}\,{\rm tr}_{{}_{R}}(T_{A}T_{B})=-\tfrac{1}{2\,c_{{}_{G}}}\,f^{C}{}_{DA}f^{D}{}_{CB}\ .

(A.3)

Here
c_{{}_{G}}
denotes the dual Coxeter number of
G
: for the classical groups these are
c_{{}_{SU(N)}}=N
,
c_{{}_{Sp(N)}}=N+1
and
c_{{}_{SO(N)}}=N-2
.

The indices of the adjoint representations coincide with the dual Coxeter numbers,
\chi_{{}_{G,adj}}=c_{{}_{G}}
, and those of the fundamental representations of the classical groups are
\chi_{{}_{SU(n),f}}=\chi_{{}_{Sp(n),f}}=\tfrac{1}{2}
and
\chi_{{}_{SO(n),f}}=1
.

The structure constants then satisfy

\displaystyle{f^{A}}_{BC}{f^{B}}_{AD}=-2\,c_{{}_{G}}{\rm Tr}[T_{C}T_{D}]\ ,\qquad{f^{D}}_{EA}{f^{E}}_{GB}{f^{G}}_{DC}=c_{{}_{G}}\,f_{ABC}\ ,

(A.4)

where we have defined
f_{ABC}={\rm Tr}[T_{C}T_{D}]{f^{D}}_{AB}
.

We shall sometimes consider a Lie subgroup
H\subset G
, which is also assumed to be simple. Then we split the algebra directions as
A=(\alpha,a)
where
T^{\alpha}
are the generators of the
H
and the remaining generators
T^{a}
are orthogonal to
T^{\alpha}
with respect to the Killing form.

The structure constants
{f^{\alpha}}_{\beta\gamma}
of the subgroup
H
then satisfy

\displaystyle{f^{\alpha}}_{\beta\gamma}{f^{\beta}}_{\alpha\delta}=-2\,c_{{}_{H}}\,{\rm Tr}[T_{\gamma}T_{\delta}]\,\qquad{f^{\delta}}_{\varepsilon\alpha}{f^{\varepsilon}}_{{\kappa}\beta}{f^{\kappa}}_{\delta\gamma}=c_{{}_{H}}\,f_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\ ,

(A.5)

where
c_{{}_{H}}
denotes a
rescaled
version of the dual Coxeter number
\tfrac{\chi_{{}_{H,R}}}{\chi_{{}_{G,R}}}c_{{}_{H}}
of
H
.

Appendix B
3-loop
\beta
-function in different renormalization schemes

In general, in quantum field theories
changes of a renormalization scheme are equivalent to local redefinitions of the coupling constants,
80
80

80

Here we reinstate the loop-counting parameter
\alpha^{\prime}=\hbar
.

\displaystyle g^{i}\to\hat{g}^{i}(g)=g^{i}+\alpha^{\prime}\,c^{i}_{jk}g^{j}g^{k}+\ldots\ .

(B.1)

Under (
B.1
) the
\beta
-functions
\beta^{i}\equiv\tfrac{d}{dt}g^{i}
transform as a contravariant vector in the space of couplings:

\displaystyle\delta\beta^{i}\equiv\hat{\beta}^{i}-\beta^{i}=\frac{\partial\,\delta g^{i}}{\partial g^{j}}\beta^{j}-\frac{\partial\beta^{i}}{\partial g^{j}}\delta g^{j}+\ldots\ ,\qquad\qquad\delta g^{i}\equiv\hat{g}^{i}-g^{i}\ ,

(B.2)

where the higher order corrections are non-linear in
\delta g
. Assuming
\beta^{i}
and
\delta g^{i}
both start at 1-loop order, i.e.
are both
{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha^{\prime})
, then
\delta\beta
is
{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha^{\prime 2})
, so the 1-loop
\beta
-function is scheme-invariant.
In single-coupling theories, one can further show that the 2-loop
\beta
-function is also scheme-invariant.

Now let us turn to the particular case of 2d
\sigma
-models, for which the relevant coupling redefinitions (
B.1
) are local redefinitions of the target space geometry.
In order to preserve the manifestly covariant structure of the action (
1.6
) we restrict to covariant redefinitions.
Restricting further to include only redefinitions with a natural interpretation in terms of changing the subtraction scheme,
81
81

81

While the term
\alpha^{\prime 2}\nabla^{2}R_{mn}
in (
B.3
) has no natural interpretation as a change of subtraction scheme starting from the minimal subtraction scheme, it is required in order that the transformation
(
B.4
) be closed under inversion in the case of the
\sigma
-model with a 2d target space,
i.e. in order to be able to move back to the minimal subtraction scheme starting from another scheme.
we consider
{\rm G}_{mn}\to\hat{{\rm G}}_{mn}
with

\displaystyle\hat{{\rm G}}_{mn}={\rm G}_{mn}+c\,\alpha^{\prime}R_{mn}+d\,\alpha^{\prime 2}(R^{2})_{mn}+e\,\alpha^{\prime 2}\nabla^{2}R_{mn}+{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha^{\prime 3})\ ,

(B.3)

\displaystyle(R^{2})_{mn}\equiv R_{mabc}{R_{n}}^{abc}\ ,

where
c,d,e
are arbitrary coefficients (for simplicity we consider the special case with vanishing
{\rm B}
-field; for a general discussion see
[
163
]
).

At the leading
{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha^{\prime})
order, both
\beta_{mn}=\alpha^{\prime}R_{mn}+\ldots
and
\delta{\rm G}_{mn}=c\,\alpha^{\prime}R_{mn}+\ldots
only contain a single term proportional to
R_{mn}
.
Thus at the leading
{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha^{\prime 2})
order in (
B.2
), their contributions cancel and the 2-loop
\beta
-function is invariant.
82
82

82

Note that if one also included redefinitions of the form
\alpha^{\prime}R{\rm G}_{mn}
then this argument would not go through in general dimensions. However, for the special case of a 2d target space, the identity
R_{mn}=\tfrac{1}{2}R{\rm G}_{mn}
means these terms are proportional and the 2-loop
\beta
-function remains invariant.

Specializing further to the case of a 2d target space (relevant for the simplest
SU(2)\over U(1)

\eta
- and
\lambda
-models considered in Chapter
2
), the redefinition (
B.3
) becomes

\displaystyle\hat{{\rm G}}_{mn}={\rm G}_{mn}\Big[1+\tfrac{1}{2}c\,\alpha^{\prime}R+\tfrac{1}{2}d\,\alpha^{\prime 2}R^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}e\,\alpha^{\prime 2}\nabla^{2}R\Big]\ .

(B.4)

Possible scheme dependence starts at 3-loop order,
with (
B.3
) or (
B.4
) being the relevant redefinition at this order.
The most general form of a covariant expression for a 3-loop
\beta
-function
is
83
83

83

In (
B.5
) we have dropped terms of the form
\nabla_{(m}V_{n)}
since these may be absorbed into the diffeomorphism vector term in (
1.8
). We have excluded the term
\alpha^{\prime 2}\nabla^{2}\nabla^{2}R{\rm G}_{mn}
in (
B.5
) since it does not arise naturally through changes of subtraction scheme starting from the minimal subtraction scheme.

\displaystyle\beta_{mn}=\Big[\tfrac{1}{2}\alpha^{\prime}R+\tfrac{1}{4}\alpha^{\prime 2}R^{2}+c_{1}\,\alpha^{\prime 3}R^{3}+c_{2}\,\alpha^{\prime 3}(\nabla R)^{2}+c_{3}\,\alpha^{\prime 3}R\nabla^{2}R\Big]{\rm G}_{mn}+c_{4}\,\alpha^{\prime 3}\nabla_{m}R\nabla_{n}R\ .

(B.5)

Not all of the coefficients
c_{1},\ldots,c_{4}
can be scheme-dependent, since the space of legitimate schemes should be parametrized by the 3-dimensional space of coupling redefinitions (
B.3
).
To find the values of
c_{i}
that are allowed to appear in the
\beta
-function in a particular scheme
let us start from the expression for the
\beta
-function (
B.5
) in the
minimal subtraction scheme where
[
146
,
147
,
148
]

\displaystyle\qquad c_{1}=\tfrac{5}{32}\ ,\qquad c_{2}=\tfrac{1}{16}\ ,\qquad c_{3}=0\ ,\qquad c_{4}=-\tfrac{1}{16}\ .

(B.6)

Implementing the metric redefinition (
B.3
) we find from (
B.2
) that the coefficients in the
transformed
\beta
-function are given by

\displaystyle\hat{c}_{1}=\tfrac{5}{32}+\tfrac{1}{8}(c-2d)\ ,

\displaystyle\hat{c}_{2}=\tfrac{1}{16}-\tfrac{1}{4}(c-2d)-\tfrac{1}{8}(4e+c^{2})\ ,

\displaystyle\hat{c}_{3}=-\tfrac{1}{8}(4e+c^{2})\ ,

\displaystyle\hat{c}_{4}=-\tfrac{1}{16}\ .

(B.7)

We note that since (
B.7
) only depends on the redefinition parameters
c,d,e
through the combinations
c-2d
and
4e+c^{2}
, the choice of
c,d,e
to give any particular values of
c_{1},\ldots,c_{4}
is not unique, but rather there is one free parameter. In the case of the
\eta
-model metric studied in Section
2.2
, the “natural” scheme (
2.19
) is given by (
B.7
) with, e.g.,
(c,d,e)=(0,\tfrac{5}{8},\tfrac{1}{2})
; i.e. it is related to the minimal subtraction scheme by the redefinition

\displaystyle{\rm G}_{mn}^{(\rm nat)}=\big[{\rm G}_{mn}+\tfrac{5}{8}\,\alpha^{\prime 2}({R}^{2})_{mn}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\alpha^{\prime 2}\nabla^{2}R_{mn}\big]^{(\rm min)}\ .

(B.8)

Appendix C
Deriving the integrability conditions for the
G^{N}
model

It was shown in

[
217
]

that the coupled
G^{N}
model (
3.2
) is integrable for particular choices of the couplings
(\rho_{ij},k_{i})
corresponding to realisations of the affine Gaudin models.
Here we shall try to demonstrate
the converse statement: these affine Gaudin models are the
only
integrable cases of the coupled models (
3.2
).

We will assume a natural ansatz (
3.4
) for the Lax connection, valued in
\operatorname{Lie}(G)

(here we explicitly indicate the summation over
i=1,...,N
)
84
84

84

While (
C.1
) is the natural ansatz for the Lax connection arising from affine Gaudin models, it does degenerate at certain points in coupling space. For example, taking
\rho_{ij}
to be diagonal (i.e. decoupled PCM
k
models), one instead requires a Lax connection valued in
\operatorname{Lie}(G)^{N}
. Thus it would also be interesting to consider other ansatze for the Lax connection.

\displaystyle L_{+}=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}(z)\,J_{+}^{(i)}\ ,\qquad\qquad L_{-}=\sum_{i}\beta_{i}(z)\,J_{-}^{(i)}\ ,

(C.1)

where
z
is the spectral parameter.
The curvature of this Lax connection takes the form

\displaystyle F_{+-}(L)=\sum_{i}\Big(\beta_{i}(1-\alpha_{i})\ \partial_{+}J_{-}^{(i)}-\alpha_{i}(1-\beta_{i})\ \partial_{-}J_{+}^{(i)}\Big)+\sum_{i\neq j}\alpha_{i}\beta_{j}\ [J_{+}^{(i)},J_{-}^{(j)}]\ .

(C.2)

The equations of motion of the
G^{N}
model (
3.2
)
(for arbitrary
N
)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} E_{i}\quad\equiv{}&{}\quad\sum_{j}\Big((\rho_{ij}-\delta_{ij}k_{j})\ \partial_{+}J_{-}^{(j)}+(\rho_{ij}+\delta_{ij}k_{j})\ \partial_{-}J_{+}^{(j)}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\rho_{ij}\ [J_{+}^{(i)},J_{-}^{(j)}]+\rho_{ji}\ [J_{-}^{(i)},J_{+}^{(j)}]\Big)\quad=\quad 0\end{aligned}

(C.3)

We note that (
C.2
) and (
C.3
) are the
unique
ways to write these expressions without any terms of the form
[J_{+}^{(i)},J_{-}^{(i)}]
, which have been eliminated using the identity
F_{+-}(J^{(i)})=0
.

If the model (
3.2
) is integrable then,
85
85

85

Here we are assuming integrability and deriving necessary conditions on the couplings. Thus we do not need to worry about whether the
v^{i}(z)
in (
C.4
) are independent functions (which would be relevant for the converse question).

for some

v^{i}(z)
,
we have

\displaystyle F_{+-}(L)=\sum_{i}v^{i}(z)\,E_{i}\ ,

(C.4)

which implies that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\beta_{j}(1-\alpha_{j})=\sum_{i}v^{i}(\rho_{ij}-\delta_{ij}k_{j})\ ,\qquad\qquad\alpha_{j}(1-\beta_{j})=\sum_{i}v^{i}(-\rho_{ji}-\delta_{ij}k_{j})\ ,\\
&\qquad\alpha_{i}\beta_{j}=(v^{i}-v^{j})\rho_{ij}\ ,\ \ \ i\neq j\ \ (\text{no summation})\ .\end{aligned}

(C.5)

This is a system of
N+N+(N^{2}-N)=N^{2}+N
equations. Fixing the freedom to redefine the spectral parameter by setting
v^{1}=z
, there are
N+N+(N-1)=3N-1
“artificial” variables,
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},v^{i\neq 1}
.
After solving for these, there are
(N^{2}+N)-(3N-1)=N^{2}-2N+1
remaining equations to be solved for
the
N^{2}+N
variables
\rho_{ij},k_{i}
. After solving all the equations, this leaves
(N^{2}+N)-(N^{2}-2N+1)=3N-1
free parameters for the integrable theory
(including the WZ levels, which may be continuous for non-compact groups).
86
86

86

One might worry that the integrability constraints on the couplings
\rho_{ij},k_{i}
resulting from (
C.4
) might depend on the spectral parameter
v^{1}=z
. However, this will not happen because there is a rescaling ambiguity
E_{i}\to c_{i}E_{i}
in the definition of the equations of motion (
C.3
). One may thus rescale
E_{1}
to effectively set
v^{1}=z=1
in (
C.4
). Since the constraints on the couplings from (
C.4
) must be invariant under such rescalings, then they must not depend on
z
.

Thus the space of integrable models is
(3N-1)
-dimensional,
which
coincides with the number of free parameters
following from the affine Gaudin construction
[
217
]
.

Specializing to the
N=2
case of
G\times G
, this counting suggests a
5
-dimensional space of integrable models. Then the 6 free parameters
(s,t,u,b,k_{1},k_{2})
in (
3.5
)
should be subject to only one relation to ensure integrability.
Solving the equations (
C.5
) in this case, one indeed obtains the condition (
3.6
)
originally found from the affine Gaudin construction.

To summarize, for general
N
, the space of integrable models has the same dimension as the space of affine Gaudin models. It remains to understand if there may still be extra branches
of integrable theories
not corresponding to the affine Gaudin models (cf. the
G\times G/H
models, where this seems to be the case for abelian
H
, see Section
3.3
). For the
N=2
case of
G\times G
models, we found exact matching between the space of integrable models (
C.5
) and the space of affine Gaudin models satisfying the condition (
3.6
).

Appendix D
Explicit 2-loop
\beta
-functions for
G\times G
and
G\times G/H
models

Here we shall provide the explicit formulae for the 2-loop
\beta
-functions of the general

G\times G
and
G\times G/H
models
that were
used in
the Chapter
3
.
87
87

87

The formulae derived in this Appendix are also available in the Mathematica file attached to the arXiv submission of the paper
[
5
]
.
We will also briefly explain how they were derived.

D.1
G\times G
model

For the
G\times G
model (
3.5
), let us use the notation

\displaystyle\rho_{ij}={\rm h}_{(ij)}+b_{[ij]}=\begin{pmatrix}s&t\\
t&u\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}0&b\\
-b&0\end{pmatrix}\ .

(D.1)

Let the
2\times 2
matrix
n_{ij}
be the
“square root” of
{\rm h}_{ij}={\rm h}_{(ij)}
, and let
m_{ij}
be its inverse,

\displaystyle n_{ik}\,n_{jk}={\rm h}_{ij}\ ,\qquad m_{ik}\,n_{kj}=\delta_{ij}\ ,\qquad\qquad n_{ij}=n_{(ij)},\quad\ m_{ij}=m_{(ij)}\ .

(D.2)

The target space metric of the
\sigma
-model (
3.5
) is ‘‘diagonalized’’ by the vielbein
1-form,

88
88

88

See Appendix
A
for the group theory conventions.
The generators
T_{A}
satisfy
[T_{A},T_{B}]=i{f^{C}}_{AB}T_{C}
and we define
f_{ABC}={\rm Tr}[T_{C}T_{D}]{f^{D}}_{AB}
. For simple groups
G
, the structure constants satisfy

{f^{A}}_{BC}{f^{B}}_{AD}=-2c_{{}_{G}}{\rm Tr}[T_{C}T_{D}]

and
{f^{D}}_{EA}{f^{E}}_{GB}{f^{G}}_{DC}=c_{{}_{G}}f_{ABC}
, where
c_{{}_{G}}
is the dual Coxeter number of
G
.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &E^{Ai}=n_{ik}\,J^{(k)A}\ ,\qquad A=1,\ldots,\dim{G}\ ,\ \ i=1,2\ ,\\
&J^{(k)}\equiv T_{A}J^{(k)A}=\big(g^{(k)}\big)^{-1}dg^{(k)}\ .\end{aligned}

(D.3)

Then the coefficients of the metric
ds^{2}={\rm G}_{Ai,Bj}E^{Ai}E^{Bj}
and the 3-form
{\rm H}=d{\rm B}=\tfrac{1}{6}{\rm H}_{Ai,Bj,Ck}E^{Ai}\wedge E^{Bj}\wedge E^{Ck}
are given by
89
89

89

The overall factor of
i
in (
D.5
) simply reflects the fact that the vielbein (
D.3
) is imaginary. This makes no difference and could be eliminated by just multiplying
E^{Ai}\to iE^{Ai}
.

\displaystyle{\rm G}_{Ai,Bj}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\,{\rm Tr}[T_{A}T_{B}]\,\delta_{ij}\ ,

(D.4)

\displaystyle{\rm H}_{Ai,Bj,Ck}=\tfrac{i}{2}f_{ABC}\big[k_{l}\,m_{il}\,m_{jl}\,m_{kl}+b_{lp}(m_{ip}\,m_{jl}\,m_{kl}+m_{il}\,m_{jp}\,m_{kl}+m_{il}\,m_{jl}\,m_{kp})\big]\ .

(D.5)

From Cartan’s structure equation
dE^{Ai}+\widehat{\omega}^{Ai}{}_{Bj}\wedge E^{Bj}=T^{Ai}
with torsion
T^{Ai}=\tfrac{1}{2}{{\rm H}^{Ai}}_{Bj,Ck}E^{Bj}\wedge E^{Ck}
, we obtain the torsionful spin connection,

\displaystyle\widehat{\omega}^{Ai}{}_{Bj}=\tfrac{i}{2}{f^{A}}_{BC}M_{ijk}E^{Ck}\ ,

(D.6)

\displaystyle M_{ijk}\equiv m_{il}m_{jl}n_{kl}-m_{il}n_{jl}m_{kl}-n_{il}m_{jl}m_{kl}

(D.7)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+k_{l}\,m_{il}\,m_{jl}\,m_{kl}+b_{lp}(m_{ip}\,m_{jl}\,m_{kl}+m_{il}\,m_{jp}\,m_{kl}+m_{il}\,m_{jl}\,m_{kp})\ .

The torsionful Riemann curvature
\widehat{R}^{Ai}{}_{Bj}\equiv\tfrac{1}{2}\widehat{R}^{Ai}{}_{Bj,Ck,Dl}E^{Ck}\wedge E^{Dl}=d\widehat{\omega}^{Ai}{}_{Bj}+\widehat{\omega}^{Ai}{}_{Ck}\wedge\widehat{\omega}^{Ck}{}_{Bj}
is then found in terms of
M_{ijk}
to be

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \widehat{R}^{Ai}{}_{Bj,Ck,Dl}={}&\tfrac{1}{4}\big[2{f^{A}}_{BE}{f^{E}}_{CD}M_{ijp}n_{pq}m_{kq}m_{lq}\\
&\qquad\qquad+{f^{A}}_{CE}{f^{E}}_{BD}M_{ipk}M_{pjl}-{f^{A}}_{DE}{f^{E}}_{BC}M_{ipl}M_{pjk}\big]\ .\end{aligned}

(D.8)

It is then straightforward to substitute (
D.5
),(
D.7
),(
D.8
) into the 2-loop
\beta
-functions in the
{\rm G}{\rm B}
scheme (
1.9
), obtaining explicit formulae for
the RG equations
\frac{d}{dt}\rho_{ij}=\beta_{ij}(n_{11},n_{12},n_{22},b,k_{1},k_{2})
depending on the components of
n_{ij}
. Using a computer symbolic algebra package (e.g. Mathematica) it is easy to rewrite these expressions in terms of the components
s,t,u
of the “squared” coupling
{\rm h}_{ij}=n_{ik}n_{jk}
in (
D.1
), with all the square roots cancelling out
as the Riemann tensor and the
{\rm H}
-field must clearly be rational functions of
\rho_{ij}
. We thus obtain the
\beta
-functions
in the form given in (
3.8
),

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\qquad\qquad\frac{d}{dt}\rho_{ij}=\alpha^{\prime}\,\beta_{ij}^{(1)}+\alpha^{\prime 2}\,\beta_{ij}^{(2)}+\ldots\ ,\\
\beta_{ij}^{(1)}={}&{c\,_{{}_{G}}}(su-t^{2})^{-2}\,F^{(4)}_{ij}(s,t,u,b,k_{1},k_{2})\ ,\qquad\beta_{ij}^{(2)}={\rm c}_{{}_{G}}^{2}(su-t^{2})^{-5}\,F^{(9)}_{ij}(s,t,u,b,k_{1},k_{2})\ ,\end{aligned}

(D.9)

where the explicit form of the homogeneous polynomials
F^{(4)}_{ij}
and
