D.2
G\times G/H
model

The computation of the
\beta
-functions for the gauge invariant
G\times G/H
model (
3.16
),(
3.19
) is similar to the
G\times G
case above, except that one has to correctly handle the gauge invariance.

We shall again use the notation (
D.1
) and (
D.2
),
with the symmetric “square root” of

{\rm h}_{ij}=\rho_{(ij)}

being
n_{ij}
, and its inverse being
m_{ij}
.
In the computation below, we shall denote certain combinations of
n_{ij}
and
m_{ij}
by

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &p_{ij}=m_{1i}m_{1j}+m_{2i}m_{2j}\ ,\quad&&\nu_{ij}=n_{1i}m_{1j}-n_{2i}m_{2j}\ ,\\
&\chi_{ij}=\tfrac{k}{2}p_{ij}+\tfrac{b}{2}(m_{1i}m_{2j}+m_{2i}m_{1j})\ ,\quad&&\lambda_{ij}=m_{1i}m_{1j}-m_{2i}m_{2j}\ .\end{aligned}

(D.10)

We shall split up the generators
T_{A}
of
G
into
T_{\alpha}\in\operatorname{Lie}(H)
and
T_{a}\in\operatorname{Lie}(G)/\operatorname{Lie}(H)
(which are orthogonal with respect to the Killing form).

Assuming from the beginning that the matrix
r_{ij}
satisfies the gauge invariance condition (
3.19
),
90
90

90

Alternatively, one could obtain the same results by starting with
r_{ij}
unconstrained, i.e. without gauge invariance imposed. One could first compute the torsionful Riemann tensor for the target space geometry (
3.16
) with general
r_{ij}
,
\rho_{ij}
. The gauge invariance condition (
3.19
) would
then be imposed and the resulting Riemann tensor projected onto the non-degenerate directions of the metric
{\rm G}_{MN}
.
the target space metric of the
\sigma
-model (
3.16
) is diagonalized by the
vielbein,
91
91

91

The index
M
denotes all tangent space directions.
In the
G\times G
case in (
D.3
) we had
M=(Ai)
, while here

M=(\alpha,\bar{\alpha},ai)
. Both
G
and
H
are assumed to be simple.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &E^{M}=(e^{\alpha},e^{\overline{\alpha}},e^{ai})=\Big(\sqrt{r}({B}^{(1)\alpha}-{B}^{(2)\alpha})\,,\ {B}^{(1)\alpha}+{B}^{(2)\alpha}\,,\ n_{ik}P^{(k)a}\Big)\ ,\\
&{B}^{(k)}\equiv T_{\alpha}{B}^{(k)\alpha}=P_{H}\big[\big(g^{(k)}\big)^{-1}dg^{(k)}\big]\ ,\qquad P^{(k)}\equiv T_{a}P^{(k)a}=P_{G/H}\big[\big(g^{(k)}\big)^{-1}dg^{(k)}\big]\ .\end{aligned}

(D.11)

In this frame, the metric
ds^{2}={\rm G}_{MN}E^{M}E^{N}
and the 3-form
{\rm H}=\tfrac{1}{6}{\rm H}_{MNP}E^{M}\wedge E^{N}\wedge E^{P}
have the following non-zero components

\displaystyle{\rm G}_{\alpha\beta}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\,{\rm Tr}[T_{\alpha}T_{\beta}]\ ,\qquad

\displaystyle{\rm G}_{ai,bj}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\,{\rm Tr}[T_{a}T_{b}]\ ,

(D.12)

\displaystyle{\rm H}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}=\tfrac{i}{2}kr^{-3/2}f_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\ ,\qquad

\displaystyle{\rm H}_{\alpha,bi,cj}=ir^{-1/2}\chi_{ij}f_{\alpha bc}\ .

(D.13)

The
H
gauge invariance is reflected in the vanishing of all
\overline{\alpha}
components of
{\rm G}_{MN}
and
{\rm H}_{MNP}
, and, in particular, the fact that
{\rm G}_{MN}
is degenerate as a result. One could explicitly fix a gauge, eliminating some target space directions and removing this degeneracy. Instead, we find it more convenient to lift the degeneracy with a small parameter
{\epsilon}
acting as a regulator,
92
92

92

The use of the “regulator”
{\epsilon}
is a short-cut for the following gauge-fixing procedure. Fixing an “axial” gauge
{iX^{\mu}({B}^{(1)}_{\mu}+{B}^{(2)}_{\mu})=y(\xi)\in\operatorname{Lie}{H}}
(
\mu=1,2
is the 2d index), the path integral should be
independent of the choice of the constant 2d vector
X^{\mu}
and the algebra-valued function
y(\xi)
of the 2d coordinates. Inserting the
\delta
-function of the gauge fixing condition into the path integral and then
integrating over
X^{\mu}
and
y
with a Gaussian measure, i.e.

e^{-\tfrac{1}{2}X^{\mu}X_{\mu}-\tfrac{1}{2}{\epsilon}\int d^{2}\xi\,{\rm Tr}[yy]}
, the result should be independent of
{\epsilon}
(here we assume
Euclidean 2d signature but the same is true also in Minkowski signature after an analytic continuation).
Integrating first over
y
we get

\int d^{2}X\exp\big[{-\tfrac{1}{2}X^{\mu}X^{\nu}\big(\delta_{\mu\nu}-{\epsilon}\,{\rm Tr}[({B}^{(1)}_{\mu}+{B}^{(2)}_{\mu})({B}^{(1)}_{\nu}+{B}^{(2)}_{\nu})]\big)}\big]
.
Integrating over
X_{\mu}
restores the 2d Euclidean invariance and the result to leading order in the
\varepsilon\to 0
limit
is equivalent to simply adding the regulator term
\Delta\mathcal{L}=-\tfrac{1}{2}{\epsilon}\,{\rm Tr}[({B}^{(1)}_{\mu}+{B}^{(2)}_{\mu})^{2}]
corresponding to (
D.14
).

\displaystyle{\rm G}_{\overline{\alpha}\overline{\beta}}=-\tfrac{1}{2}{\epsilon}\,{\rm Tr}[T_{\alpha}T_{\beta}]\ .

(D.14)

Computing the torsionful Riemann tensor as in Section

D.1
,
one finds that it has a finite
{\epsilon}\to 0
limit.
This means that the resulting Riemann tensor for
{\epsilon}=0
is unambiguous (since there are no divergences that could create finite-term ambiguities). Finally, we project out the
\overline{\alpha}
directions to obtain the non-zero components

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\widehat{R}^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\delta\varepsilon}=-\tfrac{1}{4r}f^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\gamma}f^{\gamma}{}_{\delta\varepsilon}+\tfrac{k^{2}}{4r^{3}}(f^{\alpha}{}_{\delta\gamma}f^{\gamma}{}_{\beta\varepsilon}-f^{\alpha}{}_{\varepsilon\gamma}f^{\gamma}{}_{\beta\delta})\ ,\\
&\widehat{R}^{\alpha}{}_{\beta,dk,el}=(\tfrac{k}{2r}\lambda_{kl}-\tfrac{1}{2}p_{kl})f^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\gamma}f^{\gamma}{}_{de}+\tfrac{1}{r}A_{kj}A_{lj}(f^{\alpha}{}_{dc}f^{c}{}_{\beta e}-f^{\alpha}{}_{ec}f^{c}{}_{\beta d})\ ,\\
&\widehat{R}^{\alpha}{}_{bi,\delta,el}=-\tfrac{1}{2r}A_{ji}g_{jl}f^{\alpha}{}_{bc}f^{c}{}_{\delta e}-\tfrac{k}{2r^{2}}A_{li}f^{\alpha}{}_{\delta\gamma}f^{\gamma}{}_{be}+\tfrac{1}{r}A_{lj}C_{ji}f^{\alpha}{}_{ec}f^{c}{}_{b\delta}\ ,\\
&\widehat{R}^{ai}{}_{bj,dk,el}=(C_{ij}\lambda_{kl}-\tfrac{1}{2}\delta_{ij}p_{kl})f^{a}{}_{b\gamma}f^{\gamma}{}_{de}+\tfrac{1}{r}A_{ki}A_{lj}f^{a}{}_{d\gamma}f^{\gamma}{}_{be}-\tfrac{1}{r}A_{li}A_{kj}f^{a}{}_{e\gamma}f^{\gamma}{}_{bd}\ ,\\
&\widehat{R}^{ai}{}_{bk,\delta,\varepsilon}=-\tfrac{1}{4r}\delta_{ik}f^{a}{}_{b\gamma}f^{\gamma}{}_{\delta\varepsilon}+\tfrac{1}{r}C_{ij}C_{jk}(f^{a}{}_{\delta c}f^{c}{}_{b\varepsilon}-f^{a}{}_{\varepsilon c}f^{c}{}_{b\delta})\ ,\\
&\qquad A_{ij}\equiv\tfrac{1}{4}(\nu_{ji}-\nu_{ij})-\chi_{ij}+\tfrac{r}{2}\lambda_{ij}\ ,\qquad C_{ij}\equiv-\tfrac{1}{4}(\nu_{ji}+\nu_{ij})+\chi_{ij}+\tfrac{r}{2}\lambda_{ij}\ .\\
\end{aligned}

(D.15)

All that remains is to substitute (
D.13
),(
D.15
) into the 2-loop
\beta
-functions (
1.9
) in the
{\rm G}{\rm B}
scheme. The resulting expression is hard to evaluate as it contains hundreds of terms, each proportional to a contraction of the form
(ffff)_{\alpha\beta}
or

(ffff)_{ab}

where each
f
denotes a component
f_{\gamma\delta\varepsilon}
or
f_{\gamma de}
of the structure constants, and indices are contracted using the Killing form
{\rm Tr}[T_{A}T_{B}]
. One can show, however, that there are only 11 independent such contractions after accounting for the antisymmetry of the structure constants.
This allows for
the efficient evaluation of the resulting
\beta
-functions for
r
and
\rho_{ij}
. These are first obtained depending on
n_{11},n_{12},n_{22}
but, as discussed in subsection
D.1
, they may be rewritten in terms of
s,t,u
with all square roots cancelling. As a result, we obtain the 2-loop generalization of the 1-loop
\beta
-functions in (
3.27
)–(
3.32
),

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}{\rm h}_{p}=\alpha^{\prime}\beta_{{\rm h}_{p}}^{(1)}+\alpha^{\prime 2}\beta_{{\rm h}_{p}}^{(2)}\ ,\qquad\qquad{\rm h}_{p}\equiv(r,s,t,b,u)\ ,

(D.16)

where
\beta_{{\rm h}_{p}}^{(2)}
are given by the following expressions:

Appendix E
Derivation of RG flow from existence of Lax connection for time-dependent sigma models

Here we shall provide some further details of the derivation of the RG flow
from the existence of a Lax representation for the
(\tau,\sigma)
-dependent models in Section
5
.

E.1
Derivation of the equations (
5.31
),(
5.32
)

To derive (
5.31
),(
5.32
), we shall ignore terms proportional to the derivatives of the couplings
\partial_{\pm}h_{\alpha}
and match the other terms between the flatness condition of the Lax connection and the equation of motion. The algebraic equations (
5.31
),(
5.32
) follow essentially because these ‘non-derivative’
terms do not change upon the introduction of space-time dependence of the couplings.

Let us consider
the cases of models associated to a group and to a symmetric space
separately.
For the group space case, the original equations of motion following from the flatness of Lax in (
5.26
) are the flatness
and the conservation of the current
\mathcal{A}_{\pm}
.
In the
(\tau,\sigma)
-dependent model (
5.25
), these equations may only be modified by
{\mathcal{O}}(\partial h)
terms

\displaystyle F_{+-}(\mathcal{A})={\mathcal{O}}(\partial h)\ ,\qquad\qquad\partial_{+}\mathcal{A}_{-}+\partial_{-}\mathcal{A}_{+}={\mathcal{O}}(\partial h)\ .

(E.1)

They should follow from the flatness of the Lax connection ansatz (
5.28
),

\displaystyle F_{+-}(\widehat{L})=p_{+}p_{-}F_{+-}(\mathcal{A})+\big[p_{-}(1-p_{+})\partial_{+}\mathcal{A}_{-}-p_{+}(1-p_{-})\partial_{-}\mathcal{A}_{+}\big]+{\mathcal{O}}(\partial p)\ .

(E.2)

Comparing first
the terms with
\partial_{\pm}\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{A}^{2}
, i.e. neglecting the
{\mathcal{O}}(\partial h)
and
{\mathcal{O}}(\partial p)
terms in (
E.1
),(
E.2
),
the matching of (
E.1
) and (
E.2
) then implies

\displaystyle p_{-}(1-p_{+})=-p_{+}(1-p_{-})\ .

(E.3)

This leads to the algebraic equation (
5.31
).

For the symmetric space case, the original equations of motion following from the vanishing of the curvature of (
5.27
) are the flatness condition for
(\mathcal{B}+\mathcal{P})_{\pm}
and the equations
D^{\mathcal{B}}_{\pm}\mathcal{P}_{\mp}=0
, where
D^{\mathcal{B}}_{\mu}
is the covariant derivative with respect to the connection
\mathcal{B}_{\mu}\in\operatorname{Lie}(H)
.
In the
(\tau,\sigma)
-dependent model (
5.25
) these equations are modified, as in (
E.1
),

\displaystyle F_{+-}(\mathcal{B+P})={\mathcal{O}}(\partial h)\ ,\qquad D^{\mathcal{B}}_{\pm}\mathcal{P}_{\mp}={\mathcal{O}}(\partial h)\ .

(E.4)

These should follow from the flatness of the ansatz for the Lax pair in (
5.29
),

\displaystyle F_{+-}(\widehat{L})={}

\displaystyle q_{+}q_{-}F_{+-}(\mathcal{B+P})+(r_{-}-q_{+}q_{-})D^{\mathcal{B}}_{+}\mathcal{P}_{-}-(r_{+}-q_{+}q_{-})D_{-}^{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{P}_{+}

\displaystyle\ +q_{-}(1-q_{+})\partial_{+}\mathcal{B}_{-}-q_{+}(1-q_{-})\partial_{-}\mathcal{B}_{+}+(r_{+}r_{-}-q_{+}q_{-})[\mathcal{P}_{+},\mathcal{P}_{-}]

\displaystyle\ +r_{-}(q_{+}-1)[\mathcal{B}_{+},\mathcal{P}_{-}]-r_{+}(q_{-}-1)[\mathcal{B}_{-},\mathcal{P}_{+}]+{\mathcal{O}}(\partial q,\partial r)\ .

(E.5)

Comparing the terms in (
E.4
) and (
E.5
) that contain

\partial\mathcal{B}
,
\partial\mathcal{P}
,
\mathcal{B}^{2}
,
\mathcal{P}^{2}
and
\mathcal{BP}
, i.e. neglecting the
{\mathcal{O}}(\partial h)
terms in (
E.4
) and
{\mathcal{O}}(\partial q,\partial r)
terms in (
E.5
),
we conclude in particular that the coefficients of the extra
\partial\mathcal{B}
,
[\mathcal{B,P}]
and
[\mathcal{P,P}]
terms in
(\ref{2008sc2})
must vanish. Assuming
q_{\pm},r_{\pm}
are not all zero (so that the Lax connection (
5.29
) is not identically zero) the only solution is
q_{+}=q_{-}=1
and
r_{+}r_{-}=1
, i.e. the conditions in (
5.32
).

E.2
RG flow in PCM case

The matching of the
{\mathcal{O}}(\partial h)
terms then forces the coupling functions to solve the RG flow equations. The general structure of this argument is explained in eqs. (
5.33
)–(
5.36
), but here we shall run through it explicitly for the simplest PCM example.

Together, (
5.28
) and (
5.31
) lead to the following ansatz for the Lax connection (cf. (
5.21
))

\displaystyle\widehat{L}_{\pm}=\tfrac{1}{2}\Big(1+[z(\tau,\sigma)]^{\pm 1}\Big)J_{\pm}\ ,

(E.6)

whose curvature is (
F_{+-}(J)=0
since
J=g^{-1}dg
)

\displaystyle F_{+-}(\widehat{L})

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1-{z}^{2}}{4{z}}(\partial_{+}J_{-}+\partial_{-}J_{+})-\tfrac{\partial_{+}z}{2z^{2}}J_{-}-\tfrac{\partial_{-}z}{2}J_{+}\ .

(E.7)

The equation of motion for the PCM with coupling
h(\tau,\sigma)
is (generalizing (
5.18
))

\displaystyle h(\partial_{+}J_{-}+\partial_{-}J_{+})+\partial_{+}hJ_{-}+\partial_{-}hJ_{+}=0\ .

(E.8)

For this to be equivalent to the vanishing of the curvature (
E.7
)
the ratios
of the different
coefficients should match.
This leads to the equations of the form (
5.33
), i.e.

\displaystyle\partial_{+}z=-\tfrac{z}{2}(1-z)^{2}\,\tfrac{\partial_{+}h}{h}\ ,\qquad\partial_{-}z=-\tfrac{1}{2z}(1-z)^{2}\,\tfrac{\partial_{-}h}{h}\ .

(E.9)

The consistency condition
\partial_{+}(\partial_{-}z)-\partial_{-}(\partial_{+}z)=0
gives

\displaystyle\tfrac{(1-z^{2})^{2}}{2hz}\ \partial_{+}\partial_{-}h=0\ .

(E.10)

It is remarkable that the
z
dependence has totally factored out
(a term proportional to
\partial_{+}h\partial_{-}h
is absent here due to a special cancellation).
Excluding the trivial cases
z(\tau,\sigma)=\pm 1
, which would not encode the correct equation of motion, we find that the Lax connection (
E.6
) only matches the correct equation of motion if
\partial_{+}\partial_{-}h=0
, i.e. if

h=f^{+}(\xi^{+})+f^{-}(\xi^{-})
.
Any such solution is related to the PCM’s 1-loop RG flow,
h=c\,\tau
, by a 2d conformal transformation.

E.3
RG flow for theories with multiple couplings

In Section
5.2
we explained the derivation of the RG flow
focussing on the case with only one coupling (see eqs. (
5.33
)–(
5.36
)).
The same conclusion also holds for the group space
\eta
-model with two couplings
h
and
\varkappa
(see Table
1
), and more generally is expected to be true for multi-coupling theories.

There are multiple independent structures in the equation of motion (for the
\eta
-model these involve different
powers of the
R
-matrix) and correspondingly in the flatness of the Lax connection. Matching
the coefficients of these structures in the
\eta
-model case yields
two
pairs of equations (cf. (
5.33
)),

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}z=U_{\tau}(z;h,\varkappa)\ ,\qquad\partial_{\sigma}z=U_{\sigma}(z;h,\varkappa)\ ,

(E.11)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}z=V_{\tau}(z;h,\varkappa)\ ,\qquad\partial_{\sigma}z=V_{\sigma}(z;h,\varkappa)\ .

(E.12)

In general, for an
N
-coupling theory we would expect to find
N
pairs of equations.

As a system of equations for
z
, (
E.11
),(
E.12
) is clearly overdetermined. In two combinations of these equations the
z
dependence cancels to give relations between
h(\tau,\sigma)
and
\varkappa(\tau,\sigma)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}(\varkappa^{-1}h)=\partial_{\sigma}(\varkappa^{-1}h)=0\ .

(E.13)

Eq. (
E.13
)
implies that
\nu\equiv\frac{h}{4\varkappa}
is a constant; this coincides precisely with the 1-loop RG invariant
of the
\eta
-model (see Table
1
).
For an
N
-coupling theory, we may expect to obtain
(N-1)
RG invariants
\nu_{r}
in this way.

Then
the system (
E.11
),(
E.12
) reduces to just two equations — effectively returning to the single-coupling case of equations (
5.33
). Again, the consistency condition for the two remaining equations takes the remarkable form (
5.34
), where the
\beta
-function
\beta(h)\equiv\beta^{h}(h,\nu)
is understood as a function of the coupling
h
and the RG invariant. As in eqs. (
5.35
),(
5.36
) it then follows (modulo a conformal transformation) that
h(\tau,\sigma)
depends only on
\tau
and follows the 1-loop RG flow.
The same should generalize to the
N
-coupling case with

(N-1)
independent RG invariants
\nu_{r}
that can be chosen as constants.

Appendix F
On non-local charges in time-dependent sigma models

In Section
5.3
we discussed the construction of non-local charges in the time-dependent PCM.
Here we shall comment on the other time-dependent models in Table
1
,
and, in particular, on the symmetric space
\lambda
-model.

The construction of the conserved monodromy matrix satisfying (
5.40
) works similarly, although for the models built on symmetric spaces (symmetric space
\sigma
-model and symmetric space
\lambda
-model), it is only the eigenvalues of the monodromy matrix that are conserved (because the boundary value of
L_{\tau}
does not vanish). A sufficient boundary condition in all cases is that
g(\tau,\sigma)\to g_{0}
at spatial infinity and that
(g-g_{0})
decays sufficiently fast so
that the monodromy converges at spatial infinity. As in the PCM example it is hard to evaluate the conserved charges explicitly and thus to verify that they are infinite in number (i.e. depend non-trivially on the spectral parameter
w
).

Except for the symmetric space
\lambda
-model, all the other models have global symmetries.
As for the PCM in (
5.45
), the associated
charges can be obtained by expanding the monodromy around
w=\infty
.
93
93

93

This applies also for the symmetric space
\sigma
-model after applying the gauge transformation
L_{\pm}\to gL_{\pm}g^{-1}-\partial_{\pm}gg^{-1}
to the Lax connection
L=J^{H}_{\pm}+z^{\pm 1}J_{\pm}^{G/H}
, obtaining the alternate Lax connection
L_{\pm}=\tfrac{1}{2}(1-z^{\pm 1})(-2gJ_{\pm}^{G/H}g^{-1})
of the ‘group space’ form (
5.26
), instead of (
5.27
) (modulo sign reversal of
z
).

In the remainder of this appendix, we shall consider
the trivial reduction of the time-dependent symmetric space
\lambda
-model, which has no manifest global symmetries.
In general, the eigenvalues of the monodromy matrix would be conserved on an infinite spatial line;
however, in the “trivial” reduction where the fields depend only on time (see Section
5.3
), the monodromy matrix does not converge at spatial infinity since
L_{\sigma}
does not vanish there.
Below we will try
to shed some light on this issue.

Let us first recall what happens for geodesics in the usual time-independent case, where the Lax connection is
L_{\pm}=A_{\pm}^{H}+z^{\pm 1}\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}A_{\pm}^{G/H}
. In the trivial reduction we have
A_{\pm}=A_{\pm}(g(\tau))
so
the periodicity condition (
5.39
) is satisfied and the eigenvalues of the monodromy conserved even on a finite interval of length
a
. The path-ordered exponential trivializes to give
{\cal M}=\exp{(a\,L_{\sigma})}
and hence, equivalently,
the eigenvalues of
L_{\sigma}=\tfrac{1}{2}(A_{+}^{H}-A_{-}^{H}+\tfrac{z}{\sqrt{\lambda}}A_{+}^{G/H}-\tfrac{z^{-1}}{\sqrt{\lambda}}A_{-}^{G/H})
are conserved. For example,
let us consider the simplest
SU(2)\over U(1)

\lambda
-model (with the
U(1)
subgroup generated by
\sigma_{2}
), parametrized after gauge fixing as (cf. eqs. (
2.36
),(
2.37
))

\displaystyle g=e^{i\alpha\sigma_{3}}e^{i\beta\sigma_{2}}\ ,\qquad\qquad\cos{\alpha}=\sqrt{p^{2}+q^{2}}\ ,\qquad\ \tan{\beta}=\frac{p}{q}\ ,

(F.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\frac{k}{1-\lambda^{2}}\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}(\partial p)^{2}+(1+\lambda)^{2}(\partial q)^{2}}{1-p^{2}-q^{2}}\ .

(F.2)

The trivial 1d reduction, or geodesic motion, is described by the Lagrangian (
\dot{}\equiv\partial_{\tau}
)

\displaystyle L=\frac{k}{1-\lambda^{2}}\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}\dot{p}^{2}+(1+\lambda)^{2}\dot{q}^{2}}{1-p^{2}-q^{2}}\ .

(F.3)

There is only one independent eigenvalue
l
of
L_{\sigma}
since it is a traceless
2\times 2
matrix. Its expansion around, e.g.,

z=1
gives (at least) two independent conserved charges for the geodesic motion

\displaystyle\dot{Q}_{1}=\dot{Q}_{2}=0\ ,\qquad\qquad l=\tfrac{1}{2(\lambda^{2}-1)}\sqrt{Q_{1}}+(z-1)^{2}\tfrac{\lambda}{\lambda^{2}-1}\frac{Q_{2}}{\sqrt{Q_{1}}}+\ldots\ ,

(F.4)

\displaystyle\quad Q_{1}=\frac{-(\lambda-1)^{4}q^{2}\dot{p}^{2}+2(\lambda^{2}-1)^{2}pq\dot{p}\dot{q}+(1+\lambda)^{2}[4\lambda(q^{2}-1)-(\lambda-1)^{2}p^{2}]\dot{q}^{2}}{(1-p^{2}-q^{2})^{2}}\ ,

(F.5)

\displaystyle\quad Q_{2}=\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}\dot{p}^{2}+(1+\lambda)^{2}\dot{q}^{2}}{1-p^{2}-q^{2}}\ ,

(F.6)

with
Q_{2}
proportional to the Hamiltonian.

Returning to the time-dependent case, the monodromy is not defined on an infinite line, and on any finite interval the periodicity condition is not satisfied due to the explicit
\sigma
dependence of the Lax connection for generic values of
w
. However, at certain special values of
w
satisfying
\exp{(\tfrac{c}{k}w)}=\pm\infty
, the
\sigma
dependence disappears to give flat connections

\displaystyle[\partial_{+}+A_{+}^{H}+\tfrac{1}{\lambda(\tau)}A_{+}^{G/H},\ \partial_{-}+A_{-}^{H}+A_{-}^{G/H}]=0\ ,

(F.7)

\displaystyle[\partial_{+}+A_{+}^{H}+A_{+}^{G/H},\ \partial_{-}+A_{-}^{H}+\tfrac{1}{\lambda(\tau)}A_{-}^{G/H}]=0\ ,\qquad\qquad\lambda(\tau)=\exp{(-\tfrac{c}{k}\tau)}\ ,

(F.8)

generalizing the same expressions from the time-independent case (
\lambda(\tau)\to\lambda
). At these values the periodicity condition
is
satisfied on any finite interval. The monodromy trivializes as in the time-independent case to give
{\cal M}=\exp{(a\,L_{\sigma})}
, so the eigenvalues of
L_{\sigma}
are again conserved.

In fact, the two flat connections (
F.7
),(
F.8
) are related by a gauge transformation, so their conserved charges are the same. Hence, the maximum number of independent charges obtained from the flat connections (
F.7
),(
F.8
) is
r={\rm rank}(G)

for a symmetric space
G/H
. This number is generally less than the number of fields,
\dim{G/H}=\dim{G}-\dim{H}
(e.g. for
SO(n+1)/SO(n)
we get
r=n-1<\dim{SO(n+1)/SO(n)}=n
), so this is not sufficient for integrability.

For example, in the
{SU(2)\over U(1)}\sim{SO(3)\over SO(2)}
case (
F.2
), where there are 2 fields, we only obtain
r=1
conserved charge,

\displaystyle Q=\frac{[\lambda(\tau)-1]^{4}(p^{2}-1)\dot{p}^{2}+2[\lambda(\tau)^{2}-1]^{2}pq\dot{p}\dot{q}+[\lambda(\tau)+1]^{4}(q^{2}-1)\dot{q}^{2}}{[\lambda(\tau)^{2}-1]^{2}(1-p^{2}-q^{2})^{2}}\ ,

(F.9)

for the trivial 1d reduction, which is a time-dependent generalization of (
F.3
),

\displaystyle L=\frac{k}{1-\lambda^{2}}\frac{(1-\lambda(\tau))^{2}\dot{p}^{2}+(1+\lambda(\tau))^{2}\dot{q}^{2}}{1-p^{2}-q^{2}}\ ,\quad\qquad\lambda(\tau)=\exp{(-\tfrac{c}{k}\tau)}\ .

(F.10)

In the time-independent limit
\lambda(\tau)\to\lambda
(obtained, e.g.,
by shifting
\tau\to-\tfrac{k}{c}\log{\lambda}+b\tau
and taking
b\to 0
), this charge becomes a particular combination of the charges (
F.5
),(
F.6
) in the time-independent theory,

\displaystyle Q\to Q_{1}-(\lambda-1)^{2}Q_{2}\ .

(F.11)

The presence of the charge (
F.9
) in the time-dependent 1d theory is a non-trivial property
However, at least upon restricting consideration to special values of
w
, we have not found
enough charges for the integrability of the geodesics.
Since the construction of charges is subtle, depending on the value of
w
, boundary conditions and the choice of the spatial domain
(the periodicity condition (
5.39
) must be satisfied and, if the interval is infinite, the monodromy must converge at infinity),
it is possible that the monodromy constructed on some special domain would yield further conserved charges,
but this remains to be clarified.

Appendix G
Time-dependent 1d harmonic oscillator and conserved charge

In Section
5.3
we came across a particular time-dependent, linear, 1d model (
5.50
).
Starting with a general time-dependent linear 1d action
94
94

94

We do not include the term
f(\tau)\theta\dot{\theta}

as it can be put in the form
k(\tau)\theta^{2}
by adding a total derivative.

\displaystyle S=\int d\tau\,\big[h(\tau)\,\dot{\theta}^{2}-k(\tau)\,\theta^{2}\big]\ ,\qquad\dot{\theta}\equiv\partial_{\tau}\theta\ ,

(G.1)

one may redefine
\tau
as
\tau\to t(\tau)
,
\dot{t}(\tau)=h^{-1}(\tau)
to put all time dependence in the harmonic potential term

\displaystyle S=\int dt\,\big[{\theta^{\prime}}^{2}-m^{2}(t)\,\theta^{2}]\ ,\qquad\qquad m^{2}(t)=k(\tau(t))h(\tau(t))\ ,\ \ \ \theta^{\prime}\equiv\partial_{t}\theta\ .

(G.2)

The corresponding equation of motion is

\displaystyle\theta^{\prime\prime}+m^{2}(t)\,\theta=0\ .

(G.3)

It is easy to see that for a given function
\theta_{0}(t)
, the quantity

\displaystyle Q=\theta_{0}\,\theta^{\prime}-{{\theta}^{\prime}_{0}}\,\theta\ ,

(G.4)

is conserved on-shell if and only if
\theta_{0}
is a particular solution of the equation of motion (
G.3
).
Furthermore,
such a conserved charge provides a first integral for (
G.3
),

\displaystyle Q={\theta_{0}}\,\theta^{\prime}-\theta^{\prime}_{0}\,\theta=C_{1}=\text{const}\ \ \quad\to\quad(\tfrac{\theta}{\theta_{0}})^{\prime}=\tfrac{C_{1}}{\theta_{0}^{2}}\ .

(G.5)

Integrating this first-order equation
yields the general solution of (
G.3
) (
C_{2}={\rm const}
),

\displaystyle\theta(t)=C_{1}\,\theta_{0}(t)\int\tfrac{dt}{\theta_{0}^{2}(t)}+\ C_{2}\,\theta_{0}(t)\ ,

(G.6)

with
\theta=\theta_{0}
being, of course, a special case.
Thus (
G.3
) is solvable if a particular solution
\theta_{0}
can be constructed explicitly.

Changing back to the original parametrization (
G.1
) (
t\to\tau
), the conserved charge (
G.5
)
takes the form

\displaystyle Q=h(\tau)\big[{\theta_{0}}\,\dot{\theta}-\dot{\theta}_{0}\,\theta\big]\ ,

(G.7)

where
\theta_{0}=\theta_{0}(\tau)
is a particular solution of (
G.1
), while (
G.6
) becomes

\displaystyle\theta(\tau)=C_{1}\,\theta_{0}(\tau)\int\tfrac{d\tau}{h(\tau)\,\theta_{0}^{2}(\tau)}+C_{2}\,\theta_{0}(\tau)\ .

(G.8)

The linearized theory (
5.50
) corresponds to (
G.1
) with
h(\tau)=k(\tau)=\tau
.
The conserved charge in (
5.51
),(
5.52
) is indeed of the form (
G.7
).
From the monodromy matrix one finds that

Q=\tau[\gamma(\tau)\,\theta+\alpha(\tau)\,\dot{\theta}]
where

\displaystyle\hskip-8.5359pt\gamma(\tau)=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}d\sigma\,e^{-im\sigma}\,\tfrac{m(s_{+}s_{-}-w-\sigma)}{2\tau s_{+}s_{-}},\ \ \ \alpha(\tau)=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}d\sigma\,\,e^{-im\sigma}\,\tfrac{i}{2s_{+}s_{-}},\ \ \ s_{\pm}\equiv\sqrt{w+\sigma\pm\tau}\ .

(G.9)

Let us show that
\gamma=-\dot{\alpha}
, as required to match (
G.7
). Indeed, the term proportional to
\dot{\theta}
in the leading
{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon)

expansion of the flatness equation
\partial_{\tau}L_{\sigma}-\partial_{\sigma}L_{\tau}+[L_{\tau},L_{\sigma}]=0
tells us that the integrands in (
G.9
)
satisfy
\partial_{\tau}[\tfrac{e^{-im\sigma}i}{2s_{+}s_{-}}]+\tfrac{e^{-im\sigma}m(s_{+}s_{-}-w-\sigma)}{2\tau s_{+}s_{-}}=\partial_{\sigma}\,[\tfrac{e^{-im\sigma}\,i(s_{+}s_{-}-w-\sigma)}{2\tau s_{+}s_{-}}]
.
Integrating over
\sigma
and noting that
\tfrac{e^{-im\sigma}\,i(s_{+}s_{-}-w-\sigma)}{2\tau s_{+}s_{-}}
vanishes at
\sigma=\pm\infty
,
95
95

95

This follows from the periodicity condition (
5.39
) since it is the coefficient of
\dot{\theta}
in a component of the leading
{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon)
term in
L_{\tau}
.

we conclude that
\gamma+\dot{\alpha}=0
.

Since the charge in (
5.51
) is conserved on-shell (from the monodromy matrix construction),
it follows that
\alpha(\tau)
is a particular solution, and thus the general solution (
G.8
) is in this case given by

\displaystyle\theta=C_{1}\,\alpha(\tau)\int\tfrac{d\tau}{\tau\,\alpha^{2}(\tau)}+C_{2}\,\alpha(\tau)\ .

(G.10)

Appendix H
Lax connection in time-dependent sine-Gordon model

As discussed in Section
5.4
, the sine-Gordon model,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\tfrac{1}{g^{2}}\big[\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{+}x\partial_{-}x+m^{2}\cos{x}\big]\ ,

(H.1)

displays the same pattern as the
\sigma
-models considered above: upon promoting the couplings
(g,m)
to functions of 2d time
\tau
, the Lax connection naturally generalizes to the resulting time-dependent model only if the time dependence
is given (up to rescalings of time) by the 1-loop RG flow of the original model,

\displaystyle m^{2}(\tau)=e^{\beta(g)\,\tau}m_{0}^{2}\ ,\ \ \ \qquad g(\tau)=g\ ,\ \ \ \ \qquad\beta(g)=-2+g^{2}\ .

(H.2)

Below we shall justify this claim in more detail.

As was noted in section
5.4
, the time-dependent theory obtained from (
H.2
)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{L}}=\tfrac{1}{g^{2}}\big[\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{+}x\partial_{-}x+e^{\beta(g)\,\tau}\,m^{2}_{0}\,\cos{x}\big]\ ,

(H.3)

is clearly integrable
since the explicit
\tau
-dependence in (
H.3
) can
be removed by a 2d conformal transformation getting back to (
H.1
).
Indeed, starting with the Lax pair
[
266
]
for the original
sine-Gordon model (
H.1
) (
\sigma_{i}
are Pauli matrices)

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055ptL_{\pm}=\pm\tfrac{i}{4}\partial_{\pm}x\ \sigma_{3}+\tfrac{i}{2}z^{\pm 1}\,m\cos{\tfrac{x}{2}}\ \sigma_{1}\pm\tfrac{i}{2}z^{\pm 1}\,m\sin{\tfrac{x}{2}}\ \sigma_{2}\ ,

(H.4)

and applying a conformal transformation
\xi^{\pm}\to f^{\pm}(\xi^{\pm})
, one obtains a Lax connection
for the time-dependent theory (
H.3
),
96
96

96

Note that, as for the
\sigma
-models discussed in Chapter
5
above, the dependence on the spectral parameter
z
in (
H.5
) is again correlated with
a constant shift of
\sigma
.

\displaystyle\widehat{L}_{\pm}=\pm\tfrac{i}{4}\partial_{\pm}x\ \sigma_{3}+\tfrac{i}{2}z^{\pm 1}\,e^{\beta(g)\,\xi^{\pm}}\,m_{0}\cos{\tfrac{x}{2}}\ \sigma_{1}\pm\tfrac{i}{2}z^{\pm 1}\,e^{\beta(g)\,\xi^{\pm}}\,m_{0}\sin{\tfrac{x}{2}}\ \sigma_{2}\ .

(H.5)

Note that this Lax connection follows the same ansatz (
5.11
) as in the
\sigma
-model case:
it is obtained from the original Lax connection (
H.4
) by the replacements

z\to\widehat{z}=e^{\frac{1}{2}\beta(g)\sigma}z
and
m_{0}\to m(\tau)=e^{\frac{1}{2}\beta(g)\tau}m_{0}
.

Conversely, suppose we replace the couplings
(g,m)
in (
H.1
)
with general functions of
(\tau,\sigma)
and then demand that the resulting theory (cf. (
5.25
))

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{L}}=\tfrac{1}{g^{2}(\tau,\sigma)}\big[\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{+}x\partial_{-}x+m^{2}(\tau,\sigma)\,\cos{x}\big]

(H.6)

admits a Lax representation. Motivated by (
H.4
), we shall assume the following ansatz for the Lax connection (cf. (
5.28
),(
5.29
))

\displaystyle\widehat{L}_{\pm}=f_{\pm}(\tau,\sigma)\ \tfrac{i}{4}\partial_{\pm}x\,\sigma_{3}+\,v_{\pm}(\tau,\sigma)\ \tfrac{i}{2}\cos{\tfrac{x}{2}}\,\sigma_{1}+w_{\pm}(\tau,\sigma)\ \tfrac{i}{2}\sin{\tfrac{x}{2}}\,\sigma_{2}\ .

(H.7)

Then
matching coefficients of various terms in the zero-curvature condition for (
H.7
) and in
the equation of motion corresponding to (
H.6
) leads to the following constraints on the coefficient functions in (
H.7
)
and the coupling functions,

\displaystyle f_{\pm}=\pm 1\ ,\qquad\qquad w_{\pm}=\pm v_{\pm}\ ,\qquad\qquad\partial_{\mp}v_{\pm}=0\ ,

(H.8)

\displaystyle g(\tau,\sigma)=g={\rm const}\ ,\qquad\qquad v_{+}v_{-}=m^{2}(\tau,\sigma)\ .

(H.9)

It follows from (
H.8
),(
H.9
) that
v_{\pm}=\pm w_{\pm}=v_{\pm}(\xi^{\pm})
,
m^{2}(\tau,\sigma)=v_{+}(\xi^{+})\,v_{-}(\xi^{-})
.
Finally, applying a conformal transformation to (
H.7
),
we may set, e.g.,
v_{\pm}(\xi^{\pm})\to z^{\pm 1}\,e^{\beta(g)\,\xi^{\pm}}\,m_{0}
,
m^{2}(\tau,\sigma)\to e^{\beta(g)\,\tau}\,m_{0}^{2}
,
thus bringing the Lagrangian (
H.6
) and the Lax connection (
H.7
) to the form (
H.3
) and (
H.5
)
where the time dependence is given by the 1-loop RG flow (
H.2
) of the standard sine-Gordon model.
